# GLEANINGS IN BUDDHA-FIELDS

---

## I

## A LIVING GOD

### I

Whatever their size, the temples or shrines of pure <term>Shintō</term> are all built in the same ancient style. The typical shrine is a windowless, rectangular building of unpainted timber with a very steep, overhanging roof; the front is the gable end, and the upper part of the permanently closed doors consists of wooden lattice-work—usually a grating of bars set closely together and crossing each other at right angles. In most cases, the structure is raised slightly above the ground on wooden pillars; and the unusual peaked facade, with its visor-like openings and the elaborate projections of beam-work above its gable-angle, might remind a European traveler of certain old Gothic styles of dormer windows. There is no artificial color. The

<summary>This page introduces the architectural characteristics of Shintō shrines, describing their unpainted timber construction, steep roofs, and unique facades that resemble Gothic dormers.</summary>

<keywords>Shintō, architecture, shrines, Japan, timber, gable, lattice-work, Gothic style</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of Shintō shrine architecture, focusing on how the natural weathering of the wood blends the structures into the environment and the spiritual impression they leave on the observer.</meta>

plain wood <gloss>1</gloss> soon turns, under the action of rain and sun, to a natural gray, varying according to surface exposure from the silvery tone of birch bark to the somber gray of basalt. So shaped and so tinted, the isolated country *yashiro* may seem less like a work of joinery than a feature of the scenery—a rural form related to nature as closely as rocks and trees—something that came into existence only as a manifestation of Ohotsuchi-no-Kami, the Earth-god, the primeval divinity of the land.

Why certain architectural forms produce a feeling of weirdness in the beholder is a question I would like to theorize about someday; for now, I will venture only to say that Shintō shrines evoke such a feeling. It grows with familiarity instead of weakening, and a knowledge of popular beliefs is likely to intensify it. We have no English words by which these strange shapes can be sufficiently described—much less any language able to communicate the peculiar impression which they make. Those Shintō terms which we loosely translate as “temple” and “shrine” are really untrans-

<gloss>1</gloss> Usually *hinoki* (*Chamæcyparis obtusa*).

He also explores the unique sense of weirdness these structures evoke, which defies easy translation into English.</summary>

<keywords>Shintō, yashiro, Ohotsuchi-no-Kami, architecture, Earth-god, hinoki wood, Japanese aesthetics, shrines</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the untranslatability of Shintō terms, specifically examining why "ghost-house" might be a more accurate English descriptor for a yashiro than "temple" or "shrine."</meta>

# A LIVING GOD

latable; — I mean that the Japanese ideas attached to them cannot be conveyed by translation. The so-called “august house” of the Kami is not so much a temple, in the classic meaning of the term, as it is a haunted room, a spirit-chamber, a ghost-house; many of the lesser divinities are truly ghosts—ghosts of great warriors and heroes and rulers and teachers, who lived and loved and died hundreds or thousands of years ago. I imagine that to the Western mind the word “ghost-house” will convey, better than such terms as “shrine” and “temple,” some vague notion of the strange character of the *Shintō miya* or *yashiro*—containing in its perpetual dusk nothing more substantial than symbols or tokens, the latter probably of paper. Now the emptiness behind the screened front is more suggestive than anything material could possibly be; and when you remember that millions of people during thousands of years have worshiped their great dead before such *yashiro*—that a whole race still believes those buildings are occupied by invisible, conscious personalities—you are also likely to reflect how difficult it would be to prove the

<summary>The author explains that Shintō shrines are best understood as "ghost-houses" inhabited by the spirits of ancestors and heroes. He suggests that the physical emptiness within these structures, containing only symbolic paper tokens, powerfully reinforces the belief that they are tenanted by invisible, conscious personalities.</summary>

<keywords>Shintō, Kami, ghost-house, yashiro, miya, ancestor worship, Japanese religion, spirits, Japanese architecture</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of how Westerners might react to the spiritual atmosphere of Shintō shrines, moving from the logic of "ghost-houses" to the psychological pressure of a shared national belief.</meta>

->A LIVING GOD<-

faith absurd. No! In spite of Western hesitations—in spite of whatever you may think it practical to say or not to say at a later time about the experience—you may very likely find yourself for a moment forced into an attitude of respect toward these possibilities. Mere cold reasoning will not help you much in the opposite direction. The evidence of the senses counts for little: you know there are so many realities which can neither be seen nor heard nor felt, but which exist as forces—tremendous forces. Then again, you cannot mock the conviction of forty million people while that conviction thrills all around you like the air—while you are conscious that it is pressing upon your psychic being just as the atmosphere presses upon your physical being. As for myself, whenever I am alone in the presence of a Shintō shrine, I have the sensation of being haunted; and I cannot help thinking about the possible perceptions of the haunter. And this tempts me to imagine how I should feel if I myself were a god—dwelling in some old Izumo shrine on the summit of a hill, guarded by stone lions and shadowed by a holy grove.

<summary>The author explains how the collective belief of millions creates a palpable spiritual atmosphere that can compel even a skeptical Westerner to respect the possibility of the supernatural. He describes feeling a sense of being "haunted" at Shintō shrines, leading him to wonder what it would be like to exist as a deity within one.</summary>

<keywords>Shintō, Izumo, Western perspective, psychic experience, religious conviction, spirituality, shrine, Japanese culture</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's imaginative exercise of what it would feel like to exist as a Shintō deity, transitioning from the physical environment of the shrine to the supernatural capabilities of the spirit.</meta>

->A LIVING GOD<-

My home might be incredibly small, but it would never be too small, because I would have neither size nor form. I would be only a vibration—a motion as invisible as ether or magnetism—though I would be able at times to shape for myself a shadow-body in the likeness of my former visible self, whenever I wished to make an appearance.

As air is to a bird or water is to a fish, so all substance would be permeable to my essence. I would pass at will through the walls of my dwelling to swim in the long gold bath of a sunbeam, to vibrate in the heart of a flower, or to ride on the back of a dragonfly.

Power over life and power over death would be mine—along with the power of self-extension, the power of self-multiplication, and the power of being in all places at one and the same moment. Simultaneously, in a hundred homes, I would hear myself worshipped, and I would inhale the smoke of a hundred offerings. Each evening, from my place within a hundred household shrines, I would see the holy lights lit for me in small lamps of red clay and small lamps of brass—the lights of the

<vocab>home, vibration, magnetism, shadow-body, appearance, dragonfly, self-extension, self-multiplication, worshipped, offerings, household shrines, small lamps</vocab>

<summary>The author imagines the existence of a Shintō deity as a formless, omnipresent vibration capable of passing through solid matter and appearing in multiple places at once. He describes the sensory experience of such a spirit, from interacting with nature to receiving daily offerings in many different households simultaneously.</summary>
<keywords>Shintō, divinity, supernatural powers, spiritual essence, offerings, household shrines, Japanese folklore, omnipresence</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's exploration of his imagined existence as a Shintō deity, moving from his presence in household shrines to his role in a larger hillside shrine where he receives the formal prayers and vows of the community.</meta>

# A LIVING GOD

<term>Kami</term>, lit with the purest fire and fed with the purest oil.

But in my *<term>yashiro</term>* on the hill, I would have the greatest honor: there, at times, I would gather the multitude of my selves together; there, I would unify my powers to answer prayers.

From the shadows of my ghost-house, I would look for the coming of sandaled feet and watch brown, supple fingers weaving the knotted papers—which are records of vows—to my bars, and observe the motion of the lips of my worshipers making prayer: —

> — “ *Harai-tamai kiyomé-tamaé !* . . . We have beaten drums, we have lit fires; yet the land thirsts and the rice fails. Deign, out of your divine pity, to give us rain, O <term>Daimyōjin</term> ! ”

> — “ *Harai-tamai kiyomé-tamaé !* . . . I am dark, too dark, because I have worked in the field, because the sun has looked upon me. Deign, in your majesty, to make me white, very white—white like the women of the city, O <term>Daimyōjin</term> ! ”

<summary>The author imagines presiding over a hillside shrine as a deity, observing the rituals and listening to the varied prayers of his worshipers. These petitions range from desperate pleas for rain to save failing crops to personal requests for beauty and fair skin.</summary>
<keywords>Kami, yashiro, Daimyōjin, Shintō, prayer, ritual, agriculture, Japanese folklore, devotion</keywords>

— 
> “ *Harai-tamai kiyomé-tamaé!* . . . For our son, Tsukamoto Motokichi, a soldier of twenty-nine: that he may conquer and return to us quickly—soon, very soon—we humbly pray, O <term>Daimyōjin</term>! ”

Sometimes a girl would whisper her whole heart to me: 
> “ I am a maiden of eighteen years, loved by a young man of twenty. He is good; he is true; but we are poor, and the path of our love is dark. Help us with your great divine pity! — help us so that we may be united, O <term>Daimyōjin</term>! ”

Then she would hang a thick, soft lock of hair on the bars of my shrine — her own hair, glossy and black as a crow's wing, and tied with a cord of mulberry paper. And in the fragrance of that offering — the simple fragrance of her peasant youth — I, the ghost and god, would find again the feelings of the years when I was a man and a lover.

Mothers would bring their children to my threshold and teach them to revere me, saying, 
> “ Bow down before the great, bright God; pay homage to the <term>Daimyōjin</term>. ”

Then I would hear the fresh, soft clapping of little

# A LIVING GOD

hands, and remember that I, the ghost and god, had been a father.

Daily I would hear the splash of pure, cool water poured out for me, and the tinkle of thrown coins, and the pattering of dry rice into my wooden box, like the pattering of rain; and I would be refreshed by the spirit of the water and strengthened by the spirit of the rice.

Festivals would be held to honor me. Priests, in black headgear and linen vestments, would bring me offerings of fruit, fish, seaweed, rice cakes, and rice wine—masking their faces with sheets of white paper so as not to breathe upon my food. And the <term>miko</term>, their daughters—fair girls in crimson <term>hakama</term> and robes of snowy white—would come to dance with the tinkling of little bells and the waving of silken fans, so that I might be gladdened by the bloom of their youth and delight in the charm of their grace. And there would be music from many thousands of years ago—strange music of drums and flutes—and songs in a language no longer spoken; while the <term>miko</term>, the darlings of the gods, would poise and pose before me:—

> . . . “ *Whose maidens are these,— the maidens who stand like flowers before the deity? They are the maidens of the august deity.*
> “ *The majestic music, the dancing of the maidens,— the deity will be pleased to hear, the deity will rejoice to see.*
> “ *Before the great bright God the maidens dance,— the maidens all like flowers newly opened.* ” . . .

Votive gifts of many kinds would be given to me: painted paper lanterns bearing my sacred name, and towels of various colors printed with the number of years of the giver, and pictures commemorating the fulfillment of prayers for the healing of sickness, the saving of ships, the quenching of fire, and the birth of sons.

Also my <term>Karashishi</term>, my guardian lions, would be honored. I would see my pilgrims tying straw sandals to their necks and to their paws, with a prayer to the <term>Karashishi-Sama</term> for strength of foot.

I would see fine moss, like emerald fur, growing slowly, slowly, upon the backs of those lions;—I would see the sprouting of

<meta>This page continues the author's meditation on his existence as a Shinto deity, transitioning from the description of offerings to the passage of time as seen through the aging of his shrine's statues and the changing seasons in the valley below.</meta>

# A LIVING GOD

lichens upon their flanks and upon their shoulders, in speckles of dead-silver, in patches of dead-gold;—I would watch, through years of generations, the gradual sideward sinking of their pedestals undermined by frost and rain, until at last my lions would lose their balance, and fall, and break their mossy heads off. After which the people would give me new lions of another form—lions of granite or of bronze, with gilded teeth and gilded eyes, and tails like a torment of fire.

Between the trunks of the cedars and pines, between the jointed columns of the bamboos, I would observe, season after season, the changes of the colors of the valley: the falling of the snow of winter and the falling of the snow of cherry blossoms; the lilac spread of the *miyakobana*; the blazing yellow of the *natané*; the sky-blue mirrored in flooded levels—levels dotted with the moon-shaped hats of the toiling people who would love me; and at last the pure and tender green of the growing rice.

The *muku-birds* and the *uguisu* would fill the shadows of my grove with ripplings and

--- Page 23 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s reflection on his hypothetical existence as a Shinto deity, moving from the description of the sounds of his shrine's grove to a discussion on the historical tradition of "living gods" in Japan.</meta>

# A LIVING GOD

purlings of melody; — the bell-insects, the crickets, and the seven marvelous <term>cicadas</term> of summer would make all the wood of my ghost-house thrill to their musical storms. At times I would enter, like an ecstasy, into their tiny lives to quicken the joy of their clamor and to magnify the sonority of their song.

But I can never become a god, — for this is the nineteenth century; and nobody can be really aware of the nature of the sensations of a god — unless there are gods in the flesh. Are there? Perhaps — in very remote districts — one or two. There used to be living gods.

In ancient times, any man who did something extraordinarily great or good or wise or brave might be declared a god after his death, no matter how humble his condition in life. Also, good people who had suffered great cruelty and injustice might be <term>apotheosized</term>; and there still survives the popular inclination to pay posthumous honor and to pray to the spirits of those who die voluntary deaths under particular circumstances, — to the souls of unhappy lovers, for example. (Probably the old customs which made this ten-

<summary>The author explains that while he can imagine the sensations of a deity inhabiting nature, true "living gods" are a concept from an earlier era. He describes the tradition of deifying individuals for their virtues, suffering, or tragic deaths.</summary>
<keywords>Shintoism, living gods, deification, apotheosis, nineteenth century, cicadas, ancestor worship, posthumous honor, Japanese folklore</keywords>

--- Page 24 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the deification of individuals in Japan, completing the word "tendency" from the previous page's final sentence regarding the origins of these customs.</meta>

dency originated from the desire to appease the troubled spirit, although today the experience of great suffering seems to be thought of as qualifying the person who endures it for divine conditions of being—and there would be no foolishness at all in such a thought.) But there were even more remarkable deifications. Certain people, while still alive, were honored by having temples built for their spirits and were treated as gods; not, indeed, as national gods, but as lesser divinities—<term>tutelary deities</term> <note>protective or guardian gods</note>, perhaps, or village gods. There was, for instance, Hamaguchi Gohei, a farmer of the district of Arita in the province of Kishu, who was made a god before he died. And I think he deserved it.

## II

Before telling the story of Hamaguchi Gohei, I must say a few words about certain laws—or, more accurately, customs having all the force of laws—by which many village communities were ruled in <term>pre-Meiji</term> times. These customs were based on the social experience of ages; and though they differed in minor details according to the province

<summary>The author describes how living individuals could be deified as local or protective gods in Japan, specifically citing the case of the farmer Hamaguchi Gohei. He begins to explain the strict communal customs and laws that governed Japanese villages before the Meiji era.</summary>
<keywords>deification, Hamaguchi Gohei, Arita, Kishu, pre-Meiji Japan, tutelary deities, village customs, Shintoism, Japanese history</keywords>

--- Page 25 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the customs and laws that governed village communities in pre-Meiji Japan, explaining how these traditions ensured social harmony and regulated individual conduct.</meta>

# A LIVING GOD

or district, their main meaning was essentially the same everywhere. Some were ethical, some industrial, some religious; and all matters were regulated by them—even individual behavior. They preserved peace, and they enforced mutual help and mutual kindness. Sometimes there might be serious fighting between different villages—little peasant wars over questions of water supply or boundaries; but quarreling between men of the same community could not be tolerated in an age of <term>vendetta</term>, and the whole village would resent any unnecessary disturbance of the internal peace. To some degree, this state of things still exists in the more old-fashioned provinces: the people know how to live without quarreling, let alone fighting. Everywhere, as a general rule, Japanese people fight only to kill; and when a sober man goes so far as to strike a blow, he essentially rejects communal protection and takes his life into his own hands with every probability of losing it.

The private conduct of the other sex was regulated by some remarkable obligations entirely outside of written codes. A peasant girl, before marriage, enjoyed far more liberty

<vocab>Japanese, vendetta, communal protection, peasant wars, internal peace</vocab>

<summary>This page describes how communal customs in pre-Meiji Japan enforced social harmony, required mutual kindness, and strictly prohibited internal quarreling. It notes that while inter-village conflicts occurred, individual violence within a community was seen as a rejection of communal protection, punishable by death or social exclusion.</summary>
<keywords>communal laws, village life, social harmony, pre-Meiji Japan, vendetta, Japanese customs, conflict resolution</keywords>

--- Page 26 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the customs and laws governing village communities in pre-Meiji Japan, specifically focusing on the social expectations for young women and the strict penalties for violating communal trust.</meta>

# A LIVING GOD

than was permitted to city girls. She might be known to have a lover; and unless her parents objected very strongly, no blame would be given to her: it was regarded as an honest union—honest, at least, in intention. But once she had made a choice, the girl was held bound by that choice. If it was discovered that she met another admirer secretly, the people would strip her naked, allowing her only a <term>shuro-leaf</term> for an apron, and drive her in mockery through every street and alley of the village. During this public disgrace of their daughter, the parents of the girl did not dare to show their faces in public; they were expected to share her shame, and they had to remain in their house with all the shutters fastened up. Afterward, the girl was sentenced to banishment for five years. But at the end of that period, she was considered to have expiated her fault, and she could return home with the certainty of being spared further reproaches.

The obligation of mutual help in times of calamity or danger was the most imperative of all communal obligations. In case of fire, especially, everyone was required to give

<keywords>social customs, village life, banishment, public shaming, shuro-leaf, communal obligations, mutual help, Japanese history</keywords>

Even children were not exempt from this
duty. In towns and cities, of course, things
were organized differently; but in any small
country village the universal duty was very
plain and simple, and neglecting it would have
been considered unpardonable.

A curious fact is that this obligation of
mutual help extended to religious matters:
everyone was expected to call for the help of
the gods for the sick or the unfortunate, when-
ever asked to do so. For example, the village
might be ordered to perform a <term>*sendo-mairi*</term>¹
on behalf of someone seriously ill. On such
occasions the <term>Kumi-chō</term> (each <term>Kumi-chō</term> was
responsible for the conduct of five or more
families) would run from house to house cry-
ing, "So-and-so is very sick:

---

¹ <note>To perform a <term>*sendo-mairi*</term> means to make one thousand visits to a temple and to repeat one thousand prayers to the deity. However, it is considered necessary only to go from the gate or the <term>*torii*</term> of the temple courtyard to the place of prayer and back one thousand times, repeating the prayer each time; and the task may be divided among any number of people—ten visits by one hundred people, for instance, being just as effective as a thousand visits by a single person.</note>

# A LIVING GOD

"please, everyone hurry to perform a *<term>sendo-mairi</term>*!"
At that point, no matter how occupied they were at the moment, every soul in the settlement was expected to hurry to the temple—taking care not to trip or stumble on the way, as a single misstep during the performance of a *<term>sendo-mairi</term>* was believed to mean misfortune for the sick. . . .

### III

Now, regarding Hamaguchi.
Since time immemorial, the shores of Japan have been swept at irregular intervals of centuries by enormous tidal waves—tidal waves caused by earthquakes or by submarine volcanic action. These awful, sudden risings of the sea are called by the Japanese *<term>tsunami</term>*. The last one occurred on the evening of June 17, 1896, when a wave nearly two hundred miles long struck the northeastern provinces of Miyagi, Iwate, and Aomori, wrecking dozens of towns and villages, ruining whole districts, and destroying nearly thirty thousand human lives. The story of Hamaguchi Gohei is the story of a similar calamity which happened long before the Meiji era, on another part of the Japanese coast.

<vocab>sendo-mairi, Hamaguchi Gohei, tsunami, Miyagi, Iwate, Aomori, Meiji era</vocab>

<summary>This page concludes the explanation of the communal ritual of sendo-mairi and introduces Hamaguchi Gohei within the historical context of devastating tsunamis in Japan.</summary>
<keywords>sendo-mairi, ritual, Hamaguchi Gohei, tsunami, earthquake, Japan, Meiji era, Miyagi, Iwate, Aomori</keywords>

--- Page 29 ---
<meta>This page follows the introduction of Hamaguchi Gohei and the historical context of tsunamis, providing details about his social status in the village and the geography of his home.</meta>

# A LIVING GOD

He was an old man at the time of the event that made him famous. He was the most influential resident of his village; for many years, he had been its *muraosa*, or headman, and he was as well-liked as he was respected. The people usually called him *Ojiisan*, which means Grandfather, but because he was the wealthiest member of the community, he was sometimes officially referred to as the *Chōja*. He used to advise the smaller farmers about their interests, arbitrate their disputes, lend them money when needed, and sell their rice for them on the best possible terms.

Hamaguchi’s large thatched farmhouse stood at the edge of a small plateau overlooking a bay. The plateau, mostly used for rice farming, was surrounded on three sides by thickly wooded peaks. From its outer edge, the land sloped down in a massive green hollow, as if it had been scooped out, toward the water's edge. This entire slope, about three-quarters of a mile long, was terraced so that, when viewed from the open sea, it looked like an enormous flight of green steps divided in the center by a narrow white zigzag—a streak

<summary>This page introduces Hamaguchi Gohei as a respected and wealthy village leader and describes the terraced, coastal landscape where his farmhouse was located.</summary>
<keywords>Hamaguchi Gohei, muraosa, Ojiisan, Chōja, headman, rice farming, terraces, Japan geography</keywords>

...of mountain road. Ninety thatched dwellings and a Shinto temple, comprising the village itself, stood along the curve of the bay; other houses climbed, scattered up the slope for some distance on either side of the narrow road leading to the *Chōja’s* home.

One autumn evening, Hamaguchi Gohei was looking down from the balcony of his house at some preparations for a celebration in the village below. There had been an excellent rice crop, and the peasants were going to celebrate their harvest with a dance in the courtyard of the *ujigami* <note>Shinto parish temple</note>. The old man could see the festival banners (*nobori*) fluttering above the roofs of the single street, the strings of paper lanterns hung between bamboo poles, the decorations of the shrine, and the brightly colored gathering of the young people. He had no one with him that evening except for his little grandson, a boy of ten; the rest of the household had gone early to the village. He would have accompanied them if he had not been feeling weaker than usual.

The day had been oppressive; and in spite of a rising breeze, there was still in the air

<summary>Hamaguchi Gohei watches from his balcony as the village prepares for a harvest festival, remaining home with only his grandson due to his health.</summary>
<keywords>Hamaguchi Gohei, Shinto temple, harvest festival, ujigami, nobori, village celebration, autumn</keywords>

...that sort of heavy heat which, according to the experience of the Japanese peasant, at certain seasons precedes an earthquake. And presently, an earthquake came. It was not strong enough to frighten anybody; but Hamaguchi, who had felt hundreds of shocks in his time, thought it was strange—a long, slow, spongy motion. Probably it was only the after-tremor of some immense seismic action very far away. The house crackled and rocked gently several times; then all became still again.

As the quaking ceased, Hamaguchi's keen old eyes were anxiously turned toward the village. It often happens that the attention of a person gazing fixedly at a particular spot or object is suddenly diverted by the sense of something not consciously seen at all—by a mere vague feeling of the unfamiliar in that dim outer circle of unconscious perception which lies beyond the field of clear vision. This was how Hamaguchi became aware of something unusual in the offing <note>the part of the sea visible from the shore</note>. He rose to his feet and looked at the sea. It had darkened quite suddenly, and it was acting strangely. It seemed to be moving against the wind. *It was running away from the land.*

<summary>A mild earthquake strikes the plateau, and while it is not immediately frightening, Hamaguchi Gohei notices the sea behaving strangely as it begins to recede rapidly from the shore.</summary>
<keywords>earthquake, Hamaguchi Gohei, seismic activity, sea recession, tsunami, unconscious perception</keywords>

--- Page 32 ---
<meta>This page follows the description of the earthquake's subtle arrival and Hamaguchi's observation of the sea's unusual retreat from the shoreline.</meta>

# A LIVING GOD

Within a very short time, the whole village had noticed the phenomenon. Apparently, no one had felt the previous movement of the ground, but everyone was clearly astounded by the movement of the water. They were running to the beach, and even beyond the beach, to watch it. No such receding of the tide had been witnessed on that coast within living memory. Things never seen before were appearing; unfamiliar spaces of ribbed sand and stretches of weed-covered rock were left bare even as Hamaguchi watched. And none of the people below seemed to guess what that massive recession of the sea meant.

Hamaguchi Gohei himself had never seen such a thing before; but he remembered things told to him in his childhood by his grandfather, and he knew all the traditions of the coast. He understood what the sea was going to do. Perhaps he thought of the time needed to send a message to the village, or to get the priests of the Buddhist temple on the hill to ring their big bell. . . . But it would take much longer to describe what he might have thought than it took for him to think it. He simply called to his grandson: —

"Tada! — Quick, — very quick! . . . Light a torch for me."

<term>Taimatsu</term>, or pine-torches, are kept in many coastal homes for use on stormy nights, and also for use at certain <term>Shintō</term> festivals. The child lit a torch at once; and the old man hurried with it to the fields, where hundreds of rice-stacks, representing most of his invested wealth, stood waiting to be moved. Approaching those nearest the edge of the slope, he began to set the torch to them — hurrying from one to another as quickly as his old limbs could carry him. The sun-dried stalks caught like tinder; the rising sea breeze blew the fire landward; and soon, row after row, the stacks burst into flame, sending columns of smoke into the sky that met and joined into one enormous, cloudy swirl. Tada, astonished and terrified, ran after his grandfather, crying —

"<term>Ojiisan</term>! Why? <term>Ojiisan</term>! Why? — why?"

But Hamaguchi did not answer: he had no time to explain; he was thinking only of the four hundred lives in danger. For a while the child stared wildly at the burning rice; then he burst into tears, and ran back to the

The acolyte of the hill temple, seeing the fire, set the big bell booming; and the people responded to the double alarm. Hamaguchi watched them hurrying in from the sands, across the beach, and up from the village like a swarm of ants—and, to his anxious eyes, they seemed to move no faster, for the moments felt terribly long to him. The sun was setting; the wrinkled floor of the bay and a vast, pale, speckled expanse beyond it lay exposed to the final orange glow; and still the sea was retreating toward the horizon.

In reality, however, Hamaguchi did not have very long to wait before the first group of rescuers arrived—about twenty agile young peasants who wanted to fight the fire at once. But the <term>Chōja</term>, holding out both arms, stopped them.

"Let it burn, lads!" he commanded—"leave it be! I want the whole *mura* here. There is a great danger—*taihen da!*"

The whole village was coming, and Hamaguchi counted them. All the young men and boys were soon on the spot, as well as many of the more active women and girls. Then came most of the older folk, mothers with babies on their backs, and even children—for children could help to pass buckets of water. Even the elders, who were too feeble to keep up with the first rush, could be seen well on their way up the steep ascent. The growing crowd, still knowing nothing of the reason, looked alternately in sorrowful wonder at the flaming fields and at the impassive face of their <term>Chōja</term>. And the sun went down.

“Grandfather is crazy—I am afraid of him!” sobbed Tada, in answer to a number of questions. “He is crazy. He set fire to the rice on purpose; I saw him do it!”

“As for the rice,” cried Hamaguchi, “the child tells the truth. I set fire to the rice. . . . Are all the people here?”

The <term>Kumi-chō</term> and the heads of families looked around them and down the hill, and replied, “All are here, or very soon will be. . . . We cannot understand this thing.”

Everyone looked eastward through the twilight and saw a long, lean, dim line at the edge of the dusky horizon, like the shadow of a coast where no coast had ever been—a line that thickened as they watched, broadening as a coastline broadens to the eyes of someone approaching it, yet incomparably more quickly. For that long darkness was the returning sea, towering like a cliff and racing more swiftly than a kite flies.

“*Tsunami!*” shrieked the people; and then all shrieks and all sounds and all ability to hear sounds were obliterated by a nameless shock heavier than any thunder, as the colossal swell struck the shore with a weight that sent a shudder through the hills, and with a burst of foam like a blaze of sheet-lightning. Then for an instant nothing was visible but a storm of spray rushing up the slope like a cloud; and the people scattered back in panic from the mere threat of it. When they looked again, they saw a white horror of sea raging over the place where their homes had been. It drew back roaring, tearing out the

On the plateau for a time, no word was spoken. Everyone stared speechlessly at the desolation below—the horror of hurled rocks and bare, split cliffs, the confusion of scooped-up deep-sea debris and gravel flung over the empty site of dwellings and the temple. The village was gone; the greater part of the fields were gone; even the terraces had ceased to exist; and of all the homes that had been around the bay, nothing recognizable remained except two straw roofs tossing wildly offshore. The lingering terror of the death they had escaped and the shock of the total loss kept everyone silent, until the voice of Hamaguchi was heard again, saying gently:

“*That was why I set fire to the rice.*”

Then the old man wept a little, partly because he was happy, and partly because he was old and weak and had been severely tested.

“My house remains,” he said, as soon as he could find words, automatically caressing Tada’s brown cheeks; “and there is room for many. Also, the temple on the hill stands; and there is shelter there for the others.”

Then he led the way to his house; and the people cried and shouted.

The period of distress was long, because in those days there were no means of quick communication between one district and another, and the help needed had to be sent from far away. But when better times came, the people did not forget their debt to Hamaguchi Gohei. They could not make him rich; nor would

Moreover, gifts could never have been enough to express their reverence for him; for they believed that the spirit within him was divine. So they declared him a god and thereafter called him Hamaguchi <term>DAIMYŌJIN</term>, thinking they could give him no greater honor; and truly, no greater honor in any country could be given to a mortal man. And when they rebuilt the village, they built a temple to his spirit and fixed a tablet above the front of it bearing his name in gold Chinese characters; and they worshipped him there with prayers and offerings. I cannot say how he felt about it; I only know that he continued to live in his old thatched home on the hill with his children and his grandchildren, just as humanly and simply as before, while his soul was being worshipped in the shrine below. He has been dead for more than a hundred years; but his temple, they tell me, still stands, and the people still pray to the spirit of the good old farmer to help them in times of fear or trouble.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Hamaguchi Gohei's deification, specifically focusing on a philosopher's explanation of how a person's spirit can be worshipped while they are still alive.</meta>

<language>English</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>28</page-num>
<header>A LIVING GOD</header>

I asked a Japanese philosopher and friend to explain to me how the peasants could rationally imagine the spirit of Hamaguchi in one place while his living body was in another. I also asked whether it was only one of his souls that they had worshipped during his life, and whether they imagined that particular soul had detached itself from the rest to receive homage.

“The peasants,” my friend answered, “think of the mind or spirit of a person as something which, even during life, can be in many places at the same instant. . . . Such an idea is, of course, quite different from Western ideas about the soul.”

“Any more rational?” I mischievously asked.

“Well,” he responded, with a Buddhist smile, “if we accept the doctrine of the unity of all mind, the idea of the Japanese peasant would appear to contain at least some faint shadow of truth. I could not say as much for your Western notions about the soul.”

<vocab>Japanese philosopher, Hamaguchi, soul, spirit, Western ideas, Buddhist smile, doctrine of the unity of all mind, adumbration</vocab>

The philosopher explains that Japanese belief allows for a spirit to exist in multiple places at once, suggesting this concept may hold more truth than Western ideas of the soul.</summary>
<keywords>Japanese philosophy, Hamaguchi Gohei, soul, spirit, Western thought, Buddhism, unity of mind, deification, religious theory</keywords>

## II

### OUT OF THE STREET

#### I

“THESE,” said Manyemon, putting a roll of beautifully written Japanese manuscript on the table, “are Vulgar Songs. If they are to be mentioned in some esteemed book, perhaps it would be best to say that they are 'popular,' so that Westerners are not misled.”

Next to my house, there is a vacant lot where washermen (*<term>sentakuya</term>*) work in the traditional manner—singing as they work and beating the wet clothes against large, flat stones. Every morning at dawn, their singing wakes me; I enjoy listening to it, though I often cannot make out the words. It is full of long, strange, and mournful melodies. Yesterday, the apprentice—a boy of fifteen—and the master washerman were singing al-

Thus it happened that Manyemon made

By “vulgar,” Manyemon meant songs written in
the speech of the common people. He is him-
self an expert in classical verse and despises
the *hayari-uta*, or popular ditties of the day; it re-
quires something very delicate to please him.
And I am not qualified to write about what
pleases him; for one must be a very good
Japanese scholar to deal with the superior
varieties of Japanese poetry. If you care to
know how difficult the subject is, just study
the chapter on <term>prosody</term> in Aston’s *Grammar
of the Japanese Written Language*, or the
introduction to Professor Chamberlain’s *Clas-
sical Poetry of the Japanese*. Her poetry is
the one original art which Japan has certainly
not borrowed either from China or from any
other country; and its most refined charm is
the essence, irreproducible, of the very flower
of the language itself: hence the difficulty of
representing, even partially, in any Western
language, its subtler delicacies of sentiment,

--- Page 44 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's exploration of Japanese folk songs, contrasting the simple, sincere nature of popular compositions with the complex, scholarly nature of classical poetry mentioned on the previous page.</meta>

<page-num>32</page-num>
<header>OUT OF THE STREET</header>

allusion and color. However, no scholarship is needed to understand the compositions of the common people: they are characterized by the greatest possible simplicity, directness, and sincerity. In short, their real art lies in their absolute artlessness. That was why I wanted them. Springing straight from the heart of the eternal youth of the race, these little bursts of song—like the untaught poetry of every culture—express what belongs to all human experience rather than to the limited life of a specific class or era; and even in their melodies, the fresh and powerful pulses of their primal source still resound.

Manyemon had written down forty-seven songs, and with his help, I made free translations of the best ones. They were very brief, varying from seventeen to thirty-one syllables in length. Nearly all Japanese poetic meter consists of simple alternations of lines of five and seven syllables; the frequent exceptions which popular songs offer to this rule are merely irregularities that the singer can smooth over by either slurring or prolonging certain vowel sounds. Most of the songs

->Mi-dzu no na-ga-ré to<-
->Ko-i no mi-chi.<gloss>1</gloss><-

Among various deviations from this structure, I found patterns of 7-7-7-7-5, 5-7-7-7-5, 7-5-7-5, and 5-7-5; but the classical five-line form (*tanka*), represented by 5-7-5-7-7, was entirely absent.

Terms indicating gender were also absent; even the expressions corresponding to "I" and "you" were seldom used, and the words meaning "beloved" applied equally to either sex. The gender of the speaker was suggested only by the traditional value of a comparison, the use of a particular emotional tone, or the mention of some detail of a costume, as in this verse:

->*I am the water-weed drifting, — finding no place of attachment :*<-
->*Your flower, I wonder, when and where shall it begin to bloom?*<-

---

<note>1 Literally: "Since the Age of the Gods, the things that have not changed are the flowing of water and the way of love."</note>

<summary>The author describes the syllable structure of Manyemon’s collected folk songs, noting they often consist of twenty-six syllables and lack the classical tanka form. He also highlights the absence of gender-specific terms in these poems, where the speaker's identity is only revealed through subtle contextual clues.</summary>
<keywords>Manyemon, tanka, syllable structure, Japanese poetry, gender-neutral language, poetic meter, literary analysis</keywords>

--- Page 46 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's exploration of gendered language and symbolism in Japanese folk songs, moving from general rules to specific poetic examples involving floral metaphors and divination.</meta>

<page-num>34</page-num>
<header>OUT OF THE STREET</header>

Evidently, the speaker is a girl who wishes for a lover; the same comparison spoken by a man would sound to Japanese ears much like a man’s comparison of himself to a violet or a rose would sound to English ears. For the same reason, one can tell that the speaker in the following song is not a woman:

> *Flowers in both my hands—flowers of plum and cherry:*
> *Which will be, I wonder, the flower that gives me fruit?*

Womanly charm is compared to both the cherry blossom and the plum blossom; however, the quality symbolized by the plum blossom is always moral rather than physical.<gloss>1</gloss> The verse represents a man who is strongly attracted to two girls: one, perhaps a dancer, who is very beautiful to look at; the other, who has a beautiful character. Which one shall he choose to be his lifelong companion?

One more example:

> *Too long, with pen in hand, idling, fearing, and doubting,*
> *I cast my silver pin for the test of the tatamizan.*

Here, we know from the mention of the hairpin that the speaker is a woman, and we can also assume that she is a <term>geisha</term>. The kind of divination called <term>tatamizan</term> is especially

---

<note>1 See *Glimpses of Unfamiliar Japan*, volume 2, page 357.</note>

<summary>Hearn explains how gender and character are identified in Japanese poetry through specific symbols, such as the plum blossom representing morality and the cherry blossom representing physical beauty. He also notes how objects like hairpins and practices like tatamizan divination reveal the speaker's identity as a woman or geisha.</summary>
<keywords>Japanese poetry, symbolism, plum blossom, cherry blossom, geisha, tatamizan, divination, gender identity, Lafcadio Hearn</keywords>

--- Page 47 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of tatamizan divination and introduces the thematic organization of the Japanese street and folk songs collected by the author.</meta>

<page-num>35</page-num>
<header>OUT OF THE STREET</header>

popular with dancing-girls. The rush covering of floor-mats (*tatami*), woven over a frame of thin strings, shows on its upper surface a regular series of lines about three-quarters of an inch apart. The girl throws her pin upon a mat, and then counts the lines it touches. According to their number, she considers herself lucky or unlucky. Sometimes a little pipe—geishas’ pipes are usually made of silver—is used instead of the hairpin.

The theme of all the songs was love, as indeed it is of the vast majority of the Japanese *chansons des rues et des bois* <gloss>songs of the streets and the woods</gloss>; even songs about celebrated places usually contain some amatory suggestion. I noticed that almost every simple phase of the emotion, from its earliest budding to its uttermost ripening, was represented in the collection; and I therefore tried to arrange the pieces according to the natural emotional sequence. The result had some dramatic suggestiveness.

### III

It was in this way that these little songs, composed across different generations and in various parts of Japan by many different people, seemed to form themselves for me into the ghost of a romance—the shadow of a story needing no name of time, place, or person, because it is eternally the same in all times and places.

Manyemon asks which of the songs I like best, and I turn through his manuscript again to see if I can make a choice. Outside, in the bright spring air, the washers are working; I hear the heavy *pon-pon* of wet robes being beaten, as regular as a heartbeat. Suddenly, as I muse, the voice of the boy soars up in one long, clear, shrill, splendid rocket-tone—and breaks—and softly trembles down in shimmers of fractional notes, singing the song that Manyemon remembers hearing when he himself was a boy:

> *Things have never changed since the Time of the Gods:*
> *The flowing of water, the Way of Love.*

<vocab>Manyemon, Japan, Time of the Gods, Way of Love</vocab>

<summary>The narrator reflects on how these diverse Japanese street songs coalesce into a timeless, universal story of romance. The passage ends with a boy's song that compares the unchanging nature of love to the eternal flowing of water.</summary>
<keywords>Japanese street songs, Manyemon, romance, Time of the Gods, folk music, nature of love, oral tradition</keywords>

# III

## NOTES OF A TRIP TO KYŌTO

### I

The plan was to celebrate the eleven-hundredth anniversary of the founding of Kyōto in the spring; however, an outbreak of an epidemic caused the festival to be postponed until the autumn, and the celebration began on the 15th day of the tenth month. Little nickel festival medals, designed to be pinned to the chest like military decorations, were for sale for half a yen each. These medals entitled the wearers to special discounted fares on all Japanese railroad and steamship lines, as well as other desirable privileges, such as free entrance to wonderful palaces, gardens, and temples. On October 23rd, I found myself in possession of a medal and traveling to Kyōto on the first morning train, which was overcrowded with people eager to witness the great historical processions announced for the

24th and 25th. Many had to travel standing, but the crowd was good-natured and merry. A number of my fellow passengers were Ōsaka <term>geisha</term> going to the festival. They entertained themselves by singing songs and by playing <term>ken</term> with some male acquaintances, and their playful antics and funny cries kept everybody amused. One had an extraordinary voice, with which she could twitter like a sparrow.

You can always tell by the voices of women conversing anywhere—in a hotel, for example—if there happen to be any <term>geisha</term> among them, because the peculiar timbre given by professional training is immediately recognizable. The wonderful character of that training, however, is clearly revealed only when the truly professional tones of the voice are used—falsetto tones, never emotionally moving, but often curiously sweet. Now, the street singers, the poor blind women who sing ballads with the natural voice only, use tones that move one to tears. The voice is generally a powerful contralto; and the deep tones are the tones that touch the heart. The falsetto tones of the geisha rise into a <term>treble</term> above the natural

## II

The first surprise with which Kyōto greeted her visitors was the beauty of her festival decorations. Every street had been prepared for illumination. Before each house, a new lantern post of unpainted wood had been planted, from which a lantern bearing some appropriate design was suspended. There were also national flags and sprigs of pine above each entrance. But the lanterns created the charm of the display. In each section of the street, they were of the same form, were fixed at exactly the same height, and were protected from potential bad weather by the same kind of covering. There was no pageant on the morning of my arrival, and I spent a couple of hours delightfully at the festival exhibition of <term>kakemono</term> in the imperial summer palace called <term>Omuro Gosho</term>. Unlike the professional art display which I had seen in the spring, this represented chiefly the work of students; and I found it incomparably more original and attractive. Nearly all the pictures, thousands in number, were for sale, at prices ranging from three to fifty <term>yen</term>; and it was impossible not to buy to the limit of one's purse. There were studies of nature evidently made on the spot: such as a glimpse of hazy autumn rice-fields, with dragonflies darting over the drooping grain; maples crimsoning above a tremendous gorge; ranges of peaks steeped in morning mist; and a peasant's cottage perched on the verge of some dizzy mountain road. But I have no intention to test the reader's patience with a description of pictures. I mention my visit to the display only because of something I saw there more interesting than any picture. Near the main entrance was a specimen of handwriting, intended to be mounted as a <term>*kakemono*</term> <note>a vertical hanging scroll</note> later on, and temporarily fixed upon a board about three feet long by eighteen inches wide—a Japanese poem. It was a marvel of calligraphy. Instead of the usual red stamp or seal with which the Japanese calligrapher marks his masterpieces, I saw the red imprint of a tiny, tiny hand—a living hand, which had been smeared with crimson printing-ink and skillfully pressed upon the paper. I could distinguish those little finger-marks of which Mr. Galton <note>Sir Francis Galton, a pioneer in the study of fingerprints</note> has taught us the characteristic importance.

That writing had been done in the presence of His Imperial Majesty by a child of six years—or of five, according to our Western method of calculating age from the date of

birth. The Prime Minister, Marquis Ito, saw the miracle and adopted the little boy, whose current name is therefore Ito Medzui.

Even Japanese observers could scarcely believe the evidence of their own eyes. Few adult calligraphers could surpass that writing. Certainly no Western artist, even after years of study, could repeat the feat performed by the brush of that child before the Emperor. Of course, such a child is born only once in a thousand years—to fulfill, or almost fulfill, the ancient Chinese legends of divinely inspired writers.

Still, it was not the beauty of the thing itself that impressed me, but the strange, extraordinary, and undeniable proof it provided of an inherited memory so vivid that it was almost equal to the recollection of past lives. Generations of deceased calligraphers were revived in the fingers of that tiny hand. This was never the work of an individual five-year-old child, but beyond all question, the work of ghosts—the countless ghosts that form the collective ancestral soul. It was visible and tangible proof of psychological and physiological wonders justifying both the

### III

After looking at all the pictures, I visited the great palace garden, which has only recently been opened to the public. It is called the Garden of the Cavern of the Genii. (At least "genii" is about the only word one can use to translate the term "<term>Sennin</term>," for which there is no real English equivalent; the <term>Sennin</term>, who are supposed to possess immortal life and to haunt forests or caverns, are Japanese—or rather Chinese—mythological transformations of the Indian <term>Rishi</term>.) The garden deserves its name. I felt as if I had indeed entered an enchanted place.

It is a <term>landscape garden</term>—a <term>Buddhist</term> creation belonging to what is now simply a palace, but which was once a <term>monastery</term> built as a religious retreat for emperors and princes weary of earthly vanities. The first impression received after passing the gate is that of a grand old English park: the colossal trees, the mowed grass, the broad walks, and the fresh sweet scent of greenery all awaken English

<summary>The author describes a visit to the Garden of the Cavern of the Genii in Kyoto, explaining the mythological origins of the "Sennin." He notes that the garden, once a religious retreat for royalty, strikingly resembles a traditional English park.</summary>
<keywords>Kyoto, Shinto, Buddhism, Sennin, Rishi, landscape garden, monastery, Garden of the Cavern of the Genii</keywords>

--- Page 62 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of the Garden of the Cavern of the Genii, detailing the transition from its English-like appearance to its distinctly Eastern and ancient Buddhist character.</meta>

memories. But as you proceed further, these memories are slowly erased, and the true <term>Oriental</term> impression becomes clear: you notice that the forms of those mighty trees are not European; various and surprising exotic details reveal themselves; and then you are gazing down upon a body of water containing high rocks and islets connected by bridges of the strangest shapes. Gradually—only gradually—the immense charm, the mysterious <term>Buddhist</term> charm of the place, grows and grows upon you; and the sense of its vast antiquity becomes distinct enough to touch that chord of aesthetic feeling which brings a vibration of awe.

Considered as a human work alone, the garden is a marvel: only the skilled labor of thousands could have joined together its mere bones—the enormous rocky skeleton of its plan. Once this was shaped, filled with soil, and planted, Nature was left alone to finish the wonder. Working through ten centuries, she has surpassed—even unspeakably magnified—the artist’s dream. Without exact information, no stranger unfamiliar with the laws and the purpose of <term>Japanese garden construction</term> could imagine that all this had a

human designer about a thousand years ago: the effect is that of a section of primeval forest, preserved untouched from the beginning and walled away from the rest of the world in the heart of the old capital. The rock faces, the great fantastic roots, the shadowed bypaths, and the few ancient engraved <term>monoliths</term> are all cushioned with the moss of ages; and climbing plants have developed stems a foot thick that hang across spaces like monstrous serpents. Parts of the garden vividly recall some aspects of tropical nature in the <term>Antilles</term>;—though one misses the palms, the bewildering web and weave of <term>lianas</term>, the reptiles, and the sinister daytime silence of a West Indian forest. The joyous storm of bird life overhead is astonishing and gratefully proclaims to the visitor that the wild creatures of this monastic paradise have never been harmed or frightened by humans. As I arrived at last, with regret, at the exit gate, I could not help feeling envious of its keeper: even to be a servant in such a garden would be a privilege well worth praying for.

<summary>The author describes the garden as a secluded primeval forest, highlighting its ancient mossy rocks, thick vines, and vibrant birdlife. He compares its atmosphere to a tropical wilderness and expresses a profound desire to live within its peaceful, monastic confines.</summary>

### IV

Feeling hungry, I told my rickshaw puller to take me to a restaurant because the hotel was very far; and the <term>kuruma</term> carried me into an obscure street and stopped before a rickety-looking house with some misspelled English painted above the entrance. I remember only the word “forign.” After taking off my shoes, I climbed three flights of steep, dangerous stairs—or rather, ladders—to find a set of rooms on the third floor furnished in a foreign style. The windows were glass; the linen was satisfactory; the only Japanese things were the floor mats and a welcome smoking box. American <term>chromo-lithographs</term> decorated the walls. Nevertheless, I suspected that few foreigners had ever been in the house: it stayed in business by sending Western meals, in little tin boxes, to native hotels; and the rooms had undoubtedly been prepared for Japanese visitors.

I noticed that the plates, cups, and other utensils bore the monogram of a long-defunct English hotel which used to exist in one of the open ports. The dinner was served by pleasant-looking girls, who had certainly been

Suddenly, I noticed something on a table at the other end of the room that resembled a music box and was covered with a piece of crochet work! I went over to it and discovered the wreckage of a <term>herophone</term> <note>a type of crank-operated mechanical musical instrument that used perforated paper discs or rolls</note>. There were plenty of perforated musical selections. I fixed the crank in place and tried to force out the music of a German song entitled "Five Hundred Thousand Devils." The herophone gurgled, moaned, roared for a moment, sobbed, roared again, and relapsed into silence. I tried several other selections, including "*Les Cloches de Corneville*"; but the noises produced were about the same in every case. It was obvious that the object had been bought—along with the monogrammed <term>delft</term> <note>a type of glazed earthenware</note> and <term>britannia ware</term> <note>a silver-white alloy similar to pewter</note>—at an auction in one of the foreign settlements. One must have lived in Japan to understand why the object seemed so exiled, so pathetically out of place, so completely misun—

An equally singular but more pleasant experience awaited me on the road back to the hotel. I stopped at a second-hand furniture shop to look at some curiosities and noticed, among a lot of old books, a large volume bearing the title **ATLANTIC MONTHLY** in letters of much-tarnished gold. Looking closer, I saw "Vol. V. Boston: Ticknor & Fields. 1860." Volumes of *The Atlantic* from 1860 are not common anywhere. I asked the price, and the Japanese shopkeeper said fifty <term>sen</term> <note>a Japanese monetary unit equal to one-hundredth of a yen</note> because it was "a very large book." I was much too pleased to think of bargaining with him and secured the prize. I looked through its stained pages for old friends and found them—all anonymous in 1865, but many world-famous by 1895. There were installments of "*Elsie Venner*" under the title of "*The Professor’s Story*"; chapters of "*Roba di Roma*"; and a poem called "*Pythagoras*," which has since been renamed—

What made me feel a little old-fashioned was to recognize in the quaint spellings Ziogoon, Tycoon, Sintoo, Kiusiu, Fide-yosi, and Nobanunga — spellings used by the old Dutch and old Jesuit writers — the modern and familiar <term>Shōgun</term>, <term>Taikun</term>, <term>Shintō</term>, <term>Kyūshū</term>, <term>Hideyoshi</term>, and <term>Nobunaga</term>.

I spent the evening wandering through the illuminated streets and visited some of the countless shows. I saw a young man writing Buddhist texts and drawing horses with his feet; the extraordinary thing about the work was that the texts were written backwards — from the bottom of the column up, just as an ordinary calligrapher would write them from the top of the column down — and the pictures of horses always began with the tail. I saw a kind of amphitheater, with an aquarium instead of an arena, where mermaids swam and sang Japa—

nese songs. I saw maidens “made by magic out of flowers” by a Japanese cultivator of chrysanthemums. And every so often I peeped into the toy shops, full of novelties. What especially struck me there was the display of that astounding ingenuity by which Japanese inventors are able to reach, at a cost too small to mention, precisely the same results as those shown by our expensive mechanical toys. A group of paper cocks and hens were set to pecking imaginary grain out of a basket by the pressure of a bamboo spring—the whole thing costing half a cent. An artificial mouse ran about, turning and scurrying, as if trying to slip under mats or into cracks: it cost only one cent and was made with a bit of colored paper, a spool of baked clay, and a long thread; you only had to pull the thread, and the mouse began to run. Paper butterflies, moved by an equally simple device, began to fly when thrown into the air. An artificial cuttlefish began to wriggle all its tentacles when you blew into a little rush tube fixed under its head.

When I decided to return, the lanterns were out and the shops were closing; the streets grew dark around me long before I reached the hotel. After the great glow of the illumination, the enchantments of the shows, the merry tumult, and the sea-like sound of wooden sandals, this sudden onset of blankness and silence made me feel as if the previous experience had been unreal—an illusion of light, color, and noise created just to deceive, as in the stories of goblin foxes <note>Kitsune; mythical foxes in Japanese folklore known for their shape-shifting and trickery.</note>. But the quick vanishing of everything that makes up a Japanese festival night really gives a sharper edge to the pleasure of memory: there is no slow fading of the phantasmagoria <note>A sequence of real or imaginary images like those seen in a dream.</note>, and its memory is thus kept free from the slightest touch of sadness.

->v<-

While I was thinking about the fleeting charm of Japanese amusements, the question arose: Are not all pleasures intense in proportion to their briefness? Proof of this would provide strong support for the Buddhist theory regarding the nature of pleasure. We know that mental enjoyments are power-

...full in proportion to the complexity of the feelings and ideas composing them; and the most complex feelings would therefore seem to necessarily be the briefest. In any case, Japanese popular pleasures have the double peculiarity of being fleeting and complex—not merely because of their delicacy and their variety of detail, but because this delicacy and variety are incidental, depending upon temporary conditions and combinations.

Among such conditions are the seasons of flowering and of fading, hours of sunshine or the full moon, a change of place, or a shifting of light and shade. Among these combinations are the fleeting holiday manifestations of the cultural spirit <note>The inherent character or spirit of a people; the "race genius."</note>: fragile things used to create an illusion; dreams made visible; memories revived in symbols, images, ideographs, dashes of color, and fragments of melody; and countless tiny appeals to both individual experience and national sentiment. And the emotional result remains incommunicable to Western minds, because the myriad little details and suggestions producing it belong to a world that is incomprehensible without years of familiarity—a world of traditions, beliefs, and superstitions,

feelings and ideas about which foreigners, as a general rule, know nothing. Even by the few who do know that world, the nameless, delicious sensation—the great, vague wave of pleasure excited by the spectacle of Japanese enjoyment—can only be described as *the feeling of Japan*.

An interesting sociological fact is suggested by the amazing cheapness of these pleasures. The charm of Japanese life presents us with the extraordinary phenomenon of poverty as an influence in the development of aesthetic sentiment, or at least as a factor in deciding the direction and expansion of that development. If not for poverty, the race could not have discovered, ages ago, the secret of making pleasure the most common instead of the most expensive of experiences—the divine art of creating beauty out of nothing!

One explanation for this affordability is the capacity of the people to find a much keener pleasure than we do in everything natural—in landscapes, mists, clouds, and sunsets—and in the sight of birds, insects, and flowers—as the vividness of their artistic presentations

Perhaps inexpensive Japanese pleasures might be broadly divided into those of time and place provided by nature with the help of humanity, and those of time and place invented by people at the suggestion of nature. The former category can be found in every province and multiplies yearly. Some location is chosen on a hill or coast, by a lake or river: gardens are made, trees are planted, and rest-houses are built to command the finest points of view; and the wild site is soon transformed into a place of pilgrimage for pleasure-seekers. One spot is famous for cherry trees, another for maples, another for wisteria; and each of the seasons—even snowy winter—helps to make the particular beauty of some resort. The sites of the most celebrated temples, or at least the majority of them, were selected in this way—always where the beauty of nature could inspire and

--- Page 74 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of how Japanese religious sites were strategically chosen for their natural beauty, and how this environment influences the spiritual and aesthetic lives of the people.</meta>

<page-num>62</page-num>

# NOTES OF A TRIP TO KYOTO

...aid the work of the religious architect, and where it still has the power to make many a person wish that they could become a Buddhist or Shintō priest. Indeed, religion in Japan is everywhere associated with famous scenery: with landscapes, waterfalls, peaks, rocks, and islands; with the best places from which to view the blossoming of flowers, the reflection of the autumn moon on water, or the sparkling of fireflies on summer nights.

Decorations, illuminations, and street displays of every sort—but especially those of holy days—make up a large part of the pleasures of city life that everyone can share. The appeals thus made to aesthetic imagination at festivals represent the labor, perhaps, of tens of thousands of hands and minds; however, each individual contributor to the public effort works according to their own particular thought and taste, even while obeying old rules, so that the total final result is a wondrous, bewildering, and incalculable variety. Anyone can contribute to such an occasion; and everyone does, for the cheapest materials are used. Whether it is paper, straw, or stone, it makes no real difference: the artistic sense is superbly independent.

of the material. What shapes that material is a perfect understanding of something natural, something real. Whether it is a flower made of chicken feathers, a clay turtle, duck, or sparrow, or a cardboard cricket, mantis, or frog, the idea is fully conceived and exactly realized. Mud spiders seem to be spinning webs; paper butterflies deceive the eye. No models are needed to work from; or rather, the model in every case is simply the precise memory of the object or living fact. I asked at a doll-maker’s shop for twenty tiny paper dolls, each with a different hairstyle—the whole set intended to represent the principal Kyōto styles of dressing women’s hair. A girl went to work with white paper, paint, paste, and thin strips of pine; the dolls were finished in about the same time that an artist would have taken to draw a similar number of such figures. The actual time required was only enough for the necessary movements of her fingers—not for correcting, comparing, or improving: the image in the brain realized itself as fast as the slender hands could work. This is how most of the wonders of festival nights are created: toys brought into existence

Children who on other occasions would attract no attention are converted into fairies by a few deft touches of paint and powder, and by costumes designed for artificial light. An artistic sense of line and color is enough for any transformation. The tones of decoration are never accidental, but are based on knowledge; even the lantern illuminations prove this fact, as only certain tints are used in combination. But the entire exhibition is as fleeting as it is wonderful. It vanishes much too quickly to be criticized. It is a mirage that leaves you marveling and dreaming for a month after seeing it.

Perhaps one inexhaustible source of the contentment and simple happiness belonging to ordinary Japanese life is found in this universal affordability of pleasure. Visual delight is for everyone. Not only the seasons nor the festivals provide enjoyment; almost any quaint street, any

In our great cities, beauty is for the rich; there are bare walls, foul pavements, and smoky skies for our poor, and the tumult of hideous machinery—a hell of eternal ugliness and joylessness invented by our civilization to punish the atrocious crime of being unfortunate, weak, stupid, or overconfident in the morality of one’s fellow man.

->VI<-

When I went out the next morning to view the great procession, the streets were packed so full of people that it seemed impossible for anybody to go anywhere. Nevertheless, all were moving, or rather circulating; there was a universal gliding and slipping, like fish in a school. I found no difficulty in getting through the apparently solid press of heads and shoulders to the house of a friendly

<page-num>66</page-num>

# NOTES OF A TRIP TO KYOTO

merchant, about half a mile away. How any crowd could be packed so closely, and yet move so freely, is a riddle to which Japanese character alone can provide the key. I was not rudely jostled even once. But Japanese crowds are not all alike: there are some through which an attempt to pass would lead to unpleasant consequences. Of course, the yielding fluidity of any gathering is in proportion to its gentleness; but the amount of that gentleness in Japan varies greatly according to locality. In the central and eastern provinces, the kindliness of a crowd seems to be proportional to its lack of experience with “the new civilization.” This vast gathering, of probably no fewer than a million people, was astonishingly good-natured and good-humored, because the majority of those composing it were simple country folk. When the police finally cleared a path for the procession, the multitude at once arranged itself in the least selfish manner possible—little children to the front, adults to the rear.

I was kindly given a kneeling-cushion in the front room of the merchant’s house; but although the cushion was of the softest and the courtesy shown to me was of the sweetest, I became weary of the immobile posture at last and went out into the crowd, where I could vary the experience of waiting by standing first on one foot, and then on the other. Before thus deserting my post, however, I had the privilege of seeing some very charming Kyoto ladies, including a princess, among the merchant’s guests. 

Kyoto is famous for the beauty of its women; and the most charming Japanese woman I ever saw was in that house—not the princess, but the shy young bride of the merchant’s eldest son. That the proverb about beauty being only skin-deep “is but a skin-deep saying,” Herbert Spencer has amply proved by the laws of physiology; and the same laws show that grace has a much more profound significance than beauty. The charm of the bride was just that rare form of grace which represents the economy of force in the whole framework of the physical structure—

It is very rare indeed that one sees a pretty woman in Japan who would look equally pretty in any clothing other than her own beautiful national attire. What we usually call grace in Japanese women is a daintiness of form and manner, rather than what a Greek would have termed grace. In this instance, one felt certain that her long, light, slender, fine, and faultlessly formed figure would ennoble any costume; there was just that suggestion of supple elegance which the sight of a young bamboo gives when the wind blows.

To describe the procession in detail would needlessly tire the reader, and I shall venture only a few general remarks. The purpose of the pageant was to represent the various official and military styles of dress worn during the great periods of the history of Kyoto, from the time of its foundation in the eighth century to the present Meiji era, and also the chief military figures of that history. <language>English</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>69</page-num>

# NOTES OF A TRIP TO KYOTO

procession, depicting <term>daimyō</term>, <term>kugé</term>, <term>hatamoto</term>, <term>samurai</term>, retainers, carriers, musicians, and dancers. The dancers were portrayed by <term>geisha</term>; and some were dressed so as to look like butterflies with large, bright wings. All the armor and the weapons, the ancient head-dresses and robes, were genuine relics of the past, lent for the occasion by old families, by professional antique dealers, and by private collectors. The great captains—Oda Nobunaga, Kato Kiyomasa, Iyeyasu, and Hideyoshi—were represented according to tradition; a man with a face truly resembling a monkey was even found to play the part of the famous <term>Taikō</term>.

While these visions of past centuries were passing by, the people remained perfectly silent—a fact which, as strange as the statement may seem to Western readers, indicated extreme pleasure. It is not really in accordance with national character to express applause through noisy displays—by shouting and clapping hands, for example. Even the military cheer is an import; and the tendency toward loud displays of emotion in Tōkyō is probably as artificial as it is modern. I remember two impressive silences in Kobé during 1895.

<summary>The author describes a historical procession in Kyoto featuring genuine ancient artifacts and figures from Japanese history, noting that the crowd's silence is a traditional sign of deep appreciation. He contrasts this quiet respect with modern, imported habits of noisy applause seen in cities like Tokyo.</summary>

<page-num>70</page-num>

# NOTES OF A TRIP TO KYOTO

The first instance occurred during an imperial visit. There was a vast crowd; the front ranks knelt down as the Emperor passed, yet there was not even a whisper. The second remarkable silence occurred upon the return of the victorious troops from China, who marched under the triumphal arches erected to welcome them without hearing a single syllable from the people. I asked why, and the answer was, “We Japanese believe we can better express our feelings through silence.” I may also observe here that the ominous silence of the Japanese armies before some of the recent battles terrified the noisy Chinese much more than the first firing of the artillery <note>The original text uses "batteries," referring to a unit of guns or artillery.</note>. Despite exceptions, it may be stated as a general truth that in Japan, the deeper the emotion—whether of pleasure or pain—and the more solemn or heroic the occasion, the more naturally silent are those who feel or act.

Some foreign spectators criticized the display as spiritless, and commented on the unheroic bearing of the great captains and the obvious fatigue of their followers, who were oppressed under a scorching sun by the unaccustomed weight of armor. But to the Japanese, all

He notes that while foreign observers saw the participants' fatigue and silence as a lack of spirit, it actually reflected a traditional heroic composure.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author’s observations of a festival procession in Kyoto, transitioning from the crowd's reaction to reflections on the human qualities of Japan's historical military leaders.</meta>

# NOTES OF A TRIP TO KYOTO

This only made the pageant seem more real, and I fully agreed with them. As a matter of fact, the greatest heroes of military history have appeared at their best only during exceptional moments; even the toughest veterans have known fatigue. Undoubtedly, Nobunaga, Hideyoshi, and Kato Kiyomasa must have looked just as dusty, and ridden or marched just as wearily, more than once as their representatives did in the Kyoto procession. For any educated Japanese person, no purely theatrical idealism obscures the sense of humanity regarding their country's greatest men. On the contrary, it is the historical evidence of that ordinary humanity that most endears them to the hearts of the common people; by contrast, it makes all aspects of their inner lives that were not ordinary seem even more admirable and exemplary.

After the procession, I went to the Dai-Kioku-Den, the magnificent memorial Shinto temple built by the government and described in a previous book. Upon showing my medal, I was allowed to pay my respects to the spirit of the benevolent Kwammu-Tennō <note>Kwammu-Tennō refers to Emperor Kanmu (reigned 781–806), who established Kyoto as the imperial capital.</note> and to drink a little rice wine in his honor from a new wine cup.

<summary>The author observes that the physical fatigue of participants in a Kyoto procession humanizes Japan’s historical heroes, making their actual achievements more remarkable. He concludes the day by visiting the Dai-Kioku-Den temple to offer respects to the spirit of Emperor Kanmu.</summary>
