made of pure white clay and presented by a lovely child-<term>miko</term> <note>a shrine maiden</note>. After the libation, the little priestess packed the white cup into a neat wooden box and asked me to take it home as a souvenir; one new cup is presented to every purchaser of a medal.

Such small gifts and memories make up much of the unique pleasure of Japanese travel. In almost any town or village, you can buy as a souvenir some pretty or curious thing made only in that one place, and not to be found elsewhere. Again, in many parts of the interior, a small generosity is certain to be acknowledged by a gift, which, however inexpensive, will seldom fail to prove a surprise and a pleasure. Of all the things which I picked up here and there, while traveling about the country, the prettiest and most beloved are the curious little presents obtained in this way.

on one typical woman. In a letter to the authorities, Yuko had spoken of a family claim, and this was criticized as a feminine weakness. She had, indeed, achieved the extinction of personal selfishness, but she had been “very foolish” to speak about her family. In some other ways, the book was disappointing. Under the raw, strong light of its commonplace revelations, my little sketch, “Yuko,” written in 1894, seemed for the moment much too romantic. And yet the real poetry of the event remained undiminished—the pure ideal that impelled a girl to take her own life merely to give proof of the love and loyalty of a nation. No small, mean, dry facts could ever belittle that great fact.

The sacrifice had stirred the feelings of the nation much more than it had touched my own. Thousands of photographs of Yuko and thousands of copies of the little book about her were sold. Multitudes visited her tomb and made offerings there, and gazed with tender reverence at the relics in Makkeiji; and all this, I thought, for the best of reasons. To the Japanese, who recognize that the truth of beauty belongs to the inner being, commonplace details are precious: they help to emphasize and confirm the conception of heroism. Those poor blood-stained trifles—the coarse, simple robes and sash, the small, cheap purse, the records of a visit to the pawnbroker, the glimpses of plain, humble, everyday humanity shown by the letters and the photographs and the minute precision of police records—all serve, like visual evidence, to perfect the generous understanding of the feeling that prompted the act. Had Yuko been the most beautiful person in Japan, and her family of the highest rank, the meaning of her sacrifice would have been felt far less deeply. In actual life, as a general rule, it is the common person, not the extraordinary one, who performs noble deeds; and the people, seeing best, by the help of ordinary facts, what is heroic in one of their own class, feel themselves honored. Many of us in the West will have to learn our ethics over again from the common people. Our cultivated classes have

### VIII

I returned by another way, through a district which I had never seen before—one made entirely of temples. It was a district of great spaces—vast and beautiful and hushed as if by enchantment. Yet what was the charm made of? The wonderful walls were only painted mud; the gates and the temples only frames of wood supporting tiles; the shrubbery, the stonework, the lotus ponds, mere landscape gardening. Nothing solid, nothing enduring; but a combination so beautiful of lines and colors and shadows that no speech could paint it. No! Even if those earthen walls were turned into lemon-colored marble, and their tiling into amethyst; even if the material of the temples were transformed into a substance as precious as that of the palace described in the *Sutra of the Great King of Glory*—still the aesthetic suggestion, the dreamy repose, the mellow loveliness and softness of the scene, could not be enhanced in the least. Perhaps it is just because the material of such a creation is so frail that its art is so marvelous. The most wonderful architecture, the most entrancing

## IV

### DUST

> “Let the <term>Bodhisattva</term> look upon all things as having the nature of space—as permanently equal to space; without essence, without substance.” — <term>Saddharma-Pundarîka</term>.

I have wandered to the edge of the town; and the street I followed has roughened into a country road and begins to curve away through rice fields toward a village at the foot of the hills. Between the town and the rice fields, a vague, unoccupied stretch of land serves as a favorite playground for children. There are trees, and patches of grass to roll on, and many butterflies, and plenty of little stones. I stop to look at the children.

By the roadside, some are amusing themselves with wet clay, making tiny models of mountains and rivers and rice fields; tiny mud villages, also—imitations of peasants’ huts—and little mud temples, and mud gardens with

ponds and humped bridges and imitations of stone lanterns (<term>tōrō</term>); likewise miniature cemeteries, with bits of broken stone for monuments. And they play at funerals—burying corpses of butterflies and <term>semi</term> (cicadas), and pretending to repeat <term>Buddhist sutras</term> over the grave. Tomorrow they will not dare to do this; for tomorrow will be the first day of the <term>festival of the Dead</term>. During that festival, it is strictly forbidden to disturb insects, especially <term>semi</term>, some of which have on their heads little red characters said to be the names of souls.

Children in all countries play at death. Before the sense of personal identity comes, death cannot be seriously considered; and childhood thinks in this regard more correctly, perhaps, than self-conscious maturity. Of course, if these little ones were told, some bright morning, that a playmate had gone away forever—gone away to be <term>reborn</term> elsewhere—there would be a very real though vague sense of loss, and much wiping of eyes with multicolored sleeves; but presently the loss would be forgotten and the playing resumed. The idea of ceasing to exist could not possibly enter

<meta>This page continues the author's reflection on the Japanese Buddhist child's perspective on death, explaining how their religious upbringing treats life and matter as essentially ghostly and transitory.</meta>

# DUST

a child’s mind: the butterflies and birds, the flowers, the foliage, the sweet summer itself, only play at dying; they seem to go, but they all come back again after the snow is gone. The real sorrow and fear of death arise in us only through the slow accumulation of experience with doubt and pain; and these little boys and girls, being Japanese and Buddhists, will never, in any event, feel about death just as you or I do. They will find reason to fear it for someone else’s sake, but not for their own, because they will learn that they have died millions of times already and have forgotten the trouble of it, much like one forgets the pain of successive toothaches. In the strangely penetrating light of their faith, which teaches the ghostly nature of all substance, whether granite or gossamer—just as those recently discovered X-rays reveal the ghostly nature of flesh—this present world of theirs, with its larger mountains, rivers, and rice fields, will not appear to them much more real than the mud landscapes they made in childhood. And much more real it probably is not.

myself seized by the idea of substance as non-reality.

This sense of the emptiness of things comes only when the air temperature is so perfectly balanced with the temperature of life that I can forget I have a body. Cold forces painful notions of solidity; cold sharpens the delusion of personality; cold stirs up egotism; cold numbs thought and shrivels the tiny wings of dreams.

Today is one of those warm, quiet days when it is possible to think of things as they truly are—when ocean, peak, and plain seem no more real than the arch of blue emptiness above them. Everything is a mirage—my physical self, the sunlit road, the slow rippling of the grain under a sleepy wind, the thatched roofs beyond the haze of the rice fields, and the blue crumpling of the bare hills behind everything. I have the double sensation of being a ghost myself and of being haunted—haunted by the enormous, glowing specter of the world.

Just as Night devours all lesser shadows, this ghostly earth will swallow us at last, and then itself vanish away. It is not lifeless: it feeds upon life, and visible life grows out of it. It is the dust of Karma, waiting to enter into new combinations—the dust of an older Being in that state between birth and birth which the Buddhist calls *Chū-U*.

### DUST

It is made of forces, and of nothing else; and those forces are not of this planet only, but of countless vanished spheres.

Is there anything visible, tangible, or measurable that has never been mixed with sentience? — an atom that has never vibrated to pleasure or to pain? — air that has never been a cry or speech? — a drop that has never been a tear? Assuredly, this dust has felt. It has been everything we know, as well as much that we cannot know. It has been nebula and star, planet and moon, more times than can be spoken. It has even been a deity — the Sun-God of worlds that circled and worshiped in other aeons. “Remember, Man, you are but dust!” — a saying that is profound only in terms of materialism, which stops short at the surface of things. For what is dust? “Remember, Dust, you have been a Sun, and a Sun you shall become again! . . . You have been Light, Life, and Love; — and into all of these, by ceaseless cosmic magic, you shall be turned many times again!”

Then she tries to impress the idea of this shape on the baby's memory with a practical illustration—probably learned at school. # V
# ABOUT FACES IN JAPANESE ART

### I

A VERY interesting essay upon the Japanese art collections in the National Library was read by Mr. Edward Strange at a meeting of the Japan Society held last year in London. Mr. Strange proved his appreciation of Japanese art by an exposition of its principles—the subordination of detail to the expression of a sensation or idea, and the subordination of the particular to the general. He spoke especially of the decorative element in Japanese art and of the <term>Ukiyo-yé</term> school of color printing. He remarked that even the heraldry of Japan, as illustrated in little books costing only a few pence each, contained “an education in the planning of conventional ornament.” He referred to the immense industrial value of Japanese stencil designs. He tried to explain the nature of

Finally, he pointed out the harmony between certain Japanese principles and the doctrines of one of the modern Western schools of Impressionism.

Such an address could hardly fail to provoke adverse criticism in England, because it suggested a variety of new ideas. English opinion does not prohibit the importation of ideas: the public will even complain if fresh ideas are not regularly set before it. To persuade it to accept new beliefs or thoughts without question—to coax it to jump to a conclusion—would be about as easy as making the mountains skip like rams. It was not possible that Mr. Strange’s fair but almost enthusiastic admiration of Japanese art could pass without challenge; yet one would scarcely have anticipated a challenge from the ranks of the Japan Society itself. The report, however, shows that Mr. Strange’s views were received even by that society in the characteristic English way. The idea that English artists could learn anything important from studying Japanese methods was practically dismissed; and the criticisms made by various members indicated that the philosophical part of the paper had been either misunderstood or ignored. One gentleman innocently complained that he could not imagine “why Japanese art should be utterly lacking in facial expression.” Another declared that there could never have been any lady like the ladies in Japanese prints; and he described the faces portrayed there as “absolutely insane.”

->II<-

Although the criticisms provoked by Mr. Strange’s essay were unjust to Japanese art, they were natural and indicated nothing worse than ignorance of that art and a misunderstanding of its purpose. It is not an art whose meaning can be read at a glance; years of study are necessary for a proper understanding of it. I cannot pretend that I have mastered the knowledge of its nuances and phases, but I can say truthfully that the faces in the old picture books and in the cheap prints of today—especially those in the illustrated Japanese newspapers—do not seem the least bit unreal to me, much less “absolutely insane.” There was a time when they did appear fantastic to me. Now I find them always interesting, and occasionally beautiful. If I am told that no other European would say so, then I must declare that all other

I see the women of the Japanese picture books in every Japanese street. If trained art critics who have lived in Japan laugh at this assertion, I reply that they cannot have lived in Japan long enough, or felt its life intimately enough, or studied its art impartially enough, to qualify themselves to understand even the commonest Japanese drawing.

Before I came to Japan, I used to be puzzled by the absence of facial expression in certain Japanese pictures. I confess that the faces, although even then not devoid of a certain strange charm, seemed impossible to me. Afterward, during the first two years of East Asian experience—that period in which the stranger is likely to imagine that he is learning all about a people whom no Westerner can ever really understand—I could recognize the grace and truth of certain forms and feel something of the intense charm of color in Japanese prints; but I had no perception of the deeper meaning of that art. Even the full significance of its color I did not know: much that was simply true I then thought outlandish. While conscious of the charm of many things, the reason for the charm I could not guess. But this was because I still remained under old barbaric influences—influences that blinded me to the meaning of Japanese drawing. And now, having at last learned a little, it is the Western art of illustration that appears to me conventional, undeveloped, and semi-barbarous. The pictorial attractions of English weeklies and American magazines now impress me as flat, coarse, and clumsy. My opinion on the subject, however, is limited to the ordinary class of Western illustration as compared with the ordinary class of Japanese prints.

...everywhere equally intelligible. Some of it—the very best—probably is, and some Japanese art is as well. But I can assure the reader that the ordinary art of Western book illustration or magazine engraving is just as incomprehensible to the Japanese as Japanese drawings are to Europeans who have never seen Japan. For a Japanese person to understand our common engravings, he must have lived abroad. For an Occidental to perceive the truth, the beauty, or the humor of Japanese drawings, he must know the life which those drawings reflect.

One of the critics at the meeting of the Japan Society found fault with the absence of facial expression in Japanese drawing, labeling it as conventional. He compared Japanese art on this ground with the art of the ancient Egyptians and held both to be inferior because they were restricted by convention. Yet surely the age that makes *Laocoön* a classic ought to recognize that Greek art itself was not free from conventions. It was an art that we can scarcely hope ever to equal, but it was more conventional than any existing form of art. And since it proved that even the divine could find

--- Page 118 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s defense of Japanese artistic conventions, arguing that they are based on reality whereas Greek conventions are based on an idealized, superhuman perfection.</meta>

<page-num>106</page-num>
# ABOUT FACES IN JAPANESE ART

development within the limits of artistic convention, the charge of formality is not an accusation worth making against Japanese art. Someone might respond that Greek conventions were conventions of beauty, while those of Japanese drawing have neither beauty nor meaning. But such a statement is only possible because Japanese art has not yet found its own Winckelmann or its Lessing, whereas Greek art, through the work of generations of modern critics and teachers, has been made somewhat more understandable to us than it could have been to our barbarian ancestors. The conventional Greek face cannot be found in real life, as no living head presents such a large facial angle; however, the conventional Japanese face can be seen everywhere, once the true value of its symbol in art is properly understood. The face in Greek art represents an impossible perfection, a superhuman evolution. The seemingly inexpressive face drawn by Japanese artists represents the living, the actual, and the everyday. The former is a dream; the latter is a common fact.

<summary>Hearn argues that Japanese artistic conventions are based on observable reality rather than the idealized, superhuman beauty of Greek art. He suggests that the perceived "formality" of Japanese art is misunderstood simply because it lacks the extensive critical interpretation that has helped Westerners appreciate Greek standards.</summary>

### III

A partial explanation of the apparent physiognomical <note>facial</note> conventionalism in Japanese drawing is simply that law of the subordination of individualism to type, of personality to humanity, and of detail to feeling, which the misunderstood lecturer, Mr. Edward Strange, unsuccessfully tried to teach the Japan Society something about. The Japanese artist depicts an insect, for example, as no European artist can: he makes it come alive; he shows its unique motion, its character, and everything by which it is immediately distinguished as a type—and all this with a few brushstrokes. But he does not attempt to represent every vein upon each of its wings, or every separate joint of its antennae: 1 he depicts it as it is actually seen at a glance, not as it is studied in detail. We never see all the details of the body of a grasshopper, a butterfly, or a bee in the moment that we perceive it perching somewhere; we ob—

method may astonish even scientists.

->IV<-

A few general remarks about the treatment of faces in ordinary Japanese drawing may help in understanding what that treatment teaches.

Youth is indicated by the absence of all but essential touches, and by the clean, smooth curves of the face and neck. Except for the touches which suggest eyes, nose, and mouth, there are no lines. The curves speak sufficiently of fullness, smoothness, and ripeness. For story illustration, it is not necessary to elaborate on features, as the age or social condition is indicated by the style of the coiffure and the fashion of the dress. In female figures, the absence of eyebrows indicates the wife or

I am reminded here of a curious fact that I do not remember seeing mentioned in any book about Japan. The newly arrived Westerner often complains of his inability to distinguish one Japanese person from another, and attributes this difficulty to the absence of strongly marked <term>physiognomy</term> in the race. He does not imagine that our more sharply accentuated <term>Occidental</term> features produce the very same effect upon the Japanese. Many and many a person has said to me, "For a long time I found it very hard to tell one foreigner from another: they all seemed to me alike."

individual; it is only the hint of a physiognomical law. In the case of immature youth (faces of boys and girls), there is merely a general indication of softness and gentleness—the abstract rather than the concrete charm of childhood.

In the portrayal of more mature types, the lines are more numerous and more accentuated—illustrating the fact that character necessarily becomes more marked in middle age, as the facial muscles begin to show. But there is only the suggestion of this change, not any study of individualism.

In the representation of old age, the Japanese artist gives us all the wrinkles, the hollows, the shrinking of tissues, the “crow’s-feet,” the gray hairs, and the change in the line of the face following the loss of teeth. His old men and women show character. They delight us by a certain worn sweetness of expression, a look of benevolent resignation; or they repel us by an aspect of hardened cunning, greed, or envy. There are many types of old age; but they are types of human conditions, not of personality. The picture is not drawn from a model; it is not the reflec-

Here it is worth noting that the restraint of Japanese art in the matter of facial expression aligns with the ethics of Eastern society. For ages, the rule of conduct has been to mask all personal feelings as much as possible—to hide pain and passion under an outward appearance of smiling friendliness or of stoic resignation. One key to the mysteries of Japanese art is Buddhism.

->V<-

I have said that when I now look at a foreign illustrated newspaper or magazine, I can find little pleasure in the engravings. Most often they repulse me. The drawing seems coarse and hard to me, and the realism of the design seems trivial. Such work leaves nothing to the imagination and usually reveals the effort it required. A common Japanese drawing leaves much to the imagination—in fact, it irresistibly stimulates it—and never reveals effort. Everything in a common European engraving is detailed and individ-

<meta>This page continues the comparison between Japanese and Western art, focusing on the treatment of detail and realism. It argues that while both styles use detail, Japanese art uses it to highlight universal laws or ideals, whereas Western art focuses on individual particularities.</meta>

individualized. Everything in a Japanese drawing is impersonal and suggestive. The former reveals no law: it is a study of individual details. The latter always teaches something about law and suppresses individual details except in their relation to that law.

One may often hear Japanese people say that Western art is too realistic, and this judgment contains truth. But the realism in it which offends Japanese taste—especially regarding facial expression—is not criticized merely because of the minuteness of detail. Detail itself is not condemned by any art, and the highest art is that in which detail is most exquisitely elaborated. The art which perceived the divine, which rose above nature’s best, and which discovered <term>supramundane</term> <note>transcendent or heavenly</note> ideals for animal and even floral shapes, was characterized by the sharpest possible perfection of detail. And in higher Japanese art, as in Greek art, the use of detail aids rather than opposes the aspirational aim. What most displeases in the realism of our modern illustration is not the multiplicity of detail but, as we shall soon see, the significance of detail.

<meta>This page continues the argument that Japanese art is scientific in its approach to capturing universal types, contrasting it with "aspirational" or religious art (like ancient Greek art), yet finding a common ground in their shared refusal to focus on individual details.</meta>

<page-num>116</page-num>
<header>ABOUT FACES IN JAPANESE ART</header>

...nese art is by its method scientific in the true sense. The higher art, the aspirational art (whether Japanese or ancient Greek), is, on the contrary, essentially religious in its method.

Where the scientific and the aspirational extremes of art meet, one may expect to find some universal aesthetic truth recognized by both. They agree in their impersonality: they refuse to individualize. And the lesson of the very highest art that ever existed suggests the true reason for this common refusal.

What does the charm of an antique head express, whether in marble, gem, or mural painting—for instance, that marvelous head of Leucothea <note>A sea goddess in Greek mythology; the "Head of Leucothea" is a famous antique sculpture</note> which prefaces the work of Winckelmann <note>Johann Joachim Winckelmann (1717–1768), a German art historian and archaeologist</note>? It is unnecessary to seek the reply from the works of mere art critics. Science alone can provide it. You will find it in Herbert Spencer’s essay on *Personal Beauty*. The beauty of such a head signifies a superhumanly perfect development and balance of the intellectual faculties. All those variations of feature constituting what we call "expression" represent departures from a perfect type just in proportion as they represent what is termed "character"—and they are, or ought to be,

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the relationship between physical beauty and character, drawing on the theories of Herbert Spencer to explain why certain facial expressions or features are perceived as departures from a perfect type.</meta>

more or less disagreeable or painful because "the aspects which please us are the outward correlatives of inward perfections, and the aspects which displease us are the outward correlatives of inward imperfections." Mr. Spencer goes on to say that although there are often grand natures behind plain faces, and although fine countenances frequently hide small souls, "these anomalies do not destroy the general truth of the law any more than the perturbations of planets destroy the general ellipticity of their orbits."

Both Greek and Japanese art recognized the physiognomical <note>The study of a person's facial features or expression, especially when regarded as indicative of character</note> truth which Mr. Spencer put into the simple formula, "*Expression is feature in the making*." The highest art, Greek art, rising above the real to reach the divine, gives us the dream of feature perfected. Japanese realism, so much larger than our own as to be still misunderstood, gives us only "*feature in the making*," or rather, the general law of feature in the making.

   Although the facial aspects that really attract us may be considered the outward correlates of inward perfections, or of approaches to perfections, we generally confess an interest in <term>physiognomy</term> which by no means speaks to us of inward moral perfections, but rather suggests perfections of the opposite kind. When we exclaim, "What force!" upon seeing a head with prominent bushy brows, a sharp nose, deep-set eyes, and a massive jaw, we are indeed expressing our recognition of force—but only the sort of force underlying instincts of aggression and brutality. When we praise the character of certain strong <term>aquiline</term> faces, certain so-called Roman profiles, we are really praising the traits that mark a race of prey. But it will be noticed that when the artist intends to exhibit the ideal of beauty or of virtue, he is still obliged to borrow from ancient knowledge. As a borrower, he is never quite successful, since he belongs to a humanity that is in many respects much below the ancient Greek level. A German philosopher has well said, “The resuscitated Greeks would, with perfect truth, declare our works of art in all departments to be thoroughly barbaric.” How could they be otherwise in an age which openly admires intelligence less for its power to create and preserve than for its power to crush and destroy?

Why this admiration of capacities which we would certainly not like to have exercised against ourselves? Largely, no doubt, because we admire what we wish to possess, and we understand the immense value of aggressive power, especially intellectual power, in the great competitive struggle of modern civilization.

<meta>This page continues the comparison between modern Western art, ancient Greek art, and Japanese art, specifically focusing on how each reflects the values and struggles of its respective culture.</meta>

art would be found ethically not only below Greek art, but even below Japanese art. Greek art expressed the aspiration of a people toward what is divinely beautiful and divinely wise. Japanese art reflects the simple joy of existence, the perception of natural law in form and color, the perception of natural law in change, and the sense of life made harmonious by social order and by self-restraint. Modern Western art reflects the thirst for pleasure, the idea of life as a battle for the right to enjoy, and the unpleasant qualities which are essential to success in the competitive struggle.

It has been said that the history of Western civilization is written in the Western <term>physiognomy</term>. It is at least interesting to study Western facial expressions through Eastern eyes. I have frequently amused myself by showing European or American illustrations to Japanese children and hearing their innocent comments on the faces depicted in them. A complete record of these comments might prove to be valuable as well as interesting; but for current purposes, I shall offer only the results of two experiments.

The first experiment was with a little boy, nine years old, before whom, one evening, I placed several issues of an illustrated magazine. After turning over a few of the pages, he exclaimed, “Why do foreign artists like to draw horrible things?”

“What horrible things?” I inquired.

“These,” he said, pointing to a group of figures representing voters at the polls.

“Why, those are not horrible,” I answered. “We think those drawings are very good.”

“But the faces! There cannot really be such faces in the world.”

“We think those are ordinary men. We very seldom draw really horrible faces.”

He stared in surprise, evidently suspecting that I was not being serious.

To a little girl of eleven, I showed some engravings representing famous European beauties.

“They do not look bad,” was her comment. “But they seem so much like men, and their eyes are so big! . . . Their mouths are pretty.”

“Just to see them,” she exclaimed, “I would die! I do not like this book.”

I set before her a Japanese picture book—a book of views of the *Tokaido*. She clapped her hands joyfully and pushed my half-inspected foreign magazine out of the way.

# VI

## NINGYŌ-NO-HAKA

Manyemon had coaxed the child indoors and made her eat. She appeared to be about eleven years old, intelligent, and pathetically docile. Her name was Iné, which means “springing rice;” and her frail slimness made the name seem appropriate.

When she began, under Manyemon's gentle persuasion, to tell her story, I anticipated something strange from the accompanying change in her voice. She spoke in a high, thin, sweet tone, perfectly even—a tone as changeless and unemotional as the humming of the little kettle over its charcoal bed. Not infrequently in Japan, one may hear a girl or a woman utter something touching or cruel or terrible in just such a steady, level, penetrating tone, but never anything indifferent. It always means that feeling is being kept under control.

“There were six of us at home,” said Iné—“mother and father and father's mother, who

<summary>Manyemon persuades a young girl named Iné to share her life story, noting her remarkably controlled and rhythmic way of speaking. The girl begins by describing her family members at home.</summary>
<keywords>Iné, Manyemon, Ningyō-no-haka, Japanese customs, emotional control, family history, springing rice</keywords>

--- Page 137 ---
<meta>This page continues Iné's narrative about her family history, following the description of her grandmother from the previous page.</meta>

was very old, and my brother and myself, and a little sister. Father was a <term>hyōguya</term>, a paper-hanger; he papered sliding screens and also mounted <term>kakemono</term>. Mother was a hairdresser. My brother was apprenticed to a seal-cutter.

“Father and mother did well; mother made even more money than father. We had good clothes and good food, and we never had any real sorrow until father fell sick.

“It was the middle of the hot season. Father had always been healthy; we did not think that his sickness was dangerous, and he did not think so himself. But the very next day he died. We were very much surprised. Mother tried to hide her heart and to serve her customers as before. But she was not very strong, and the pain of father’s death came too quickly. Eight days after father’s funeral, mother also died. It was so sudden that everyone wondered. Then the neighbors told us that we must make a <term>ningyō-no-haka</term> at once—or else there would be another death in our house. My brother said they were right, but he put off doing what they told him. Perhaps he did not have money

<summary>Iné describes her family's prosperous life as artisans until the sudden, back-to-back deaths of her father and mother. Neighbors warn the children to perform the ningyō-no-haka ritual to prevent a third death from occurring in the household.</summary>
<keywords>Iné, hyōguya, kakemono, ningyō-no-haka, family tragedy, bereavement, Japanese customs, Meiji era</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Iné's story regarding the sudden deaths of her parents and the traditional Japanese ritual required to stop further tragedy in the family.</meta>

# NINGYO-NO-HAKA

enough, I do not know; but the *haka* was not made." . . .

"What is a *<term>ningyō-no-haka</term>*?" I interrupted.

"I think," Manyemon answered, "that you have seen many *<term>ningyō-no-haka</term>* without knowing what they were; — they look just like the graves of children. It is believed that when two members of a family die in the same year, a third must also soon die. There is a saying, *Always three graves*. So when two from one family have been buried in the same year, a third grave is made next to the graves of those two, and in it is placed a coffin containing only a little figure made of straw — a *<term>wara-ningyō</term>*; and over that grave a small tombstone is set up, bearing a <term>kaimyō</term>.<gloss>1</gloss> The priests of the temple to which the graveyard belongs write the <term>kaimyō</term> for these little gravestones. By making a *<term>ningyō-no-haka</term>*, it is thought that a death may be prevented. . . . We are listening for the rest, Iné."

---

<sup>1</sup> The posthumous Buddhist name of the deceased person is carved upon the tomb or *<term>haka</term>*.

> “There were still four of us—grandmother, brother, myself, and my little sister. My brother was nineteen years old. He had finished his apprenticeship just before father died; we thought that was like the gods taking pity on us. He had become the head of the house. He was very skillful in his business and had many friends; therefore, he could support us. He made thirteen <term>yen</term> the first month; that is very good for a <term>seal-cutter</term>. Next morning he was not able to get up; he had a very high fever. We nursed him as well as we could and sat up at night to watch over him, but he did not get better. When you were alive, we all loved you. None of us ever spoke unkind words to you. Why do you now want to take the boy? You know that he is the only pillar of our house. But our brother said that mother was still pulling him by the sleeve. When the sun went down, he died.

->*Sodé shiboru.*<note>“Children without parents, like the seagulls of the coast. Evening after evening the sleeves are wrung.” The word *<term>chidori</term>*—applied indiscriminately to many kinds of birds—is used here for seagull. The cries of the seagull are thought to express melancholy and desolation; hence the comparison. The long sleeve of the Japanese robe is used to wipe the eyes as well as to hide the face in moments of grief. To “wring the sleeve”—that is, to wring the moisture from a tear-drenched sleeve—is a common expression in Japanese poetry.</note>

My sister was adopted by a *<term>tatamiya</term>*, a mat-maker—one of my father’s friends. She is treated kindly; she even goes to school!”

# NINGYO-NO-HAKA

"*Aa fushigi na koto da! — aa komatta ne?*" murmured Manyemon. Then there was a moment or two of sympathetic silence. Iné bowed low in thanks and rose to leave. As she slipped her feet under the straps of her sandals, I moved toward the spot where she had been sitting to ask the old man a question. She noticed my intention and immediately made an indescribable sign to Manyemon, who responded by stopping me just as I was about to sit down beside him.

"She wishes," he said, "that the master will honorably strike the matting first."

"But why?" I asked in surprise—noticing only that under my bare feet, the spot where the child had been kneeling felt comfortably warm.

Manyemon answered:—

"She believes that to sit down upon the place made warm by the body of another is to take into one's own life all the sorrow of that other person—unless the place is struck first."

At this, I sat down without performing the rite; and we both laughed.

# VII

## IN ŌSAKA

->*Takaki ya ni*<-

### I

Nearly three hundred years ago, Captain John Saris, visiting Japan in the service of the “Right Honorable Company, the merchants of London trading into the East Indies,” wrote concerning the great city of Ōsaka (as the name is now transliterated): —
> “We found Osaka to be a very great town, as large as London within the walls, with many fair timber bridges of a great height, serving to pass over a river there as wide as the Thames at London. Some fair houses

<summary>This page introduces Chapter VII with a poem by Emperor Nintoku celebrating the prosperity of his people and begins a historical description of Osaka using the 17th-century account of Captain John Saris.</summary>
<keywords>Osaka, Emperor Nintoku, Captain John Saris, East India Company, historical description, urban prosperity</keywords>

--- Page 145 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of Ōsaka, comparing Captain John Saris’s 17th-century observations with the city’s state in the late 19th century, including its historical castle and the etymology of its name.</meta>

we found there, but not many. It is one of the chief seaports of all Japan; having a castle in it, marvelously large and strong"
. . . What Captain Saris said of the Ōsaka of the seventeenth century is almost equally true of the Ōsaka of today. It is still a very great city and one of the chief seaports of all Japan; it contains, according to the Western idea, "some fair houses;" it has many "fair timber bridges" (as well as bridges of steel and stone) — "serving to pass over a river as wide as the Thames at London," — the Yodogawa; and the castle "marvelously large and strong," built by <term>Hideyoshi</term> after the plan of a Chinese fortress of the Han dynasty, still remains something for military engineers to wonder at, in spite of the disappearance of the many-storied towers, and the destruction (in 1868) of the magnificent palace.

Ōsaka is more than two thousand five hundred years old, and therefore one of the most ancient cities of Japan, — though its present name, a contraction of *Oye no Saka*, meaning the High Land of the Great River, is believed to date back only to the fifteenth cen-

<meta>This page continues the historical and economic description of Ōsaka, transitioning from its ancient origins and 15th-century name change to its role as the financial heart of Japan during the feudal era and its status in 1896.</meta>

<page-num>134</page-num>
-># IN OSAKA<-

century, before which time it was called Naniwa. Centuries before Europe knew of Japan’s existence, Ōsaka was the great financial and commercial center of the empire; and it remains so today. Throughout the feudal era, the merchants of Ōsaka were the bankers and creditors of the Japanese princes: they exchanged the rice revenues for silver and gold; they kept the national stores of grain, cotton, and silk in their miles of fireproof warehouses; and they provided great military leaders with the sinews of war. Hideyoshi made Ōsaka his military capital; Iyeyasu, jealous and sharp-witted, feared the great city and deemed it necessary to impoverish its capitalists because of their financial power.

The Ōsaka of 1896, covering a vast area, has a population of about 670,000. In terms of size and population, it is now only the second city of the empire; but it remains, as Count Okuma remarked in a recent speech, financially, industrially, and commercially superior to Tōkyō. Sakai, Hyōgo, and Kobé are really only its outer ports; and the last-named is visibly outgrowing Yokohama. It is confidently predicted, both by foreigners

Almost everything that everyone wants is made in Ōsaka; and there are few comfortable Japanese homes in any part of the empire to the furnishing of which Ōsaka industry has not contributed something. This was probably the case long before Tōkyō existed. There survives an ancient song whose refrain goes, — “*Every day to Ōsaka come a thousand ships.*” They were only junks in the time when the song was written; today they are also steamers and deep-sea travelers of all rigs. Along the wharves, you can ride for miles past a seemingly endless array of masts and funnels — though the great Trans-Pacific liners and European mail-steamers draw too much water to enter the harbor and receive their Ōsaka freight at Kobé. I hardly need to say that Ōsaka is the center of the great trade guilds,<note>There are more than four hundred commercial companies in Ōsaka.</note> and the headquarters of those cotton-spinning companies whose mills, kept running with a single shift twenty-three hours out of the twenty-four, produce double the quantity of yarn per spindle that English mills produce, and from thirty to forty percent more than the mills of Bombay.

Every great city in the world is believed to give a special character to its inhabitants; and in Japan, the man of Ōsaka is said to be recognizable almost at sight. I think it can be said that the character of the man from the capital is less distinct than that of the man of Ōsaka—just as in America, the man from Chicago is more quickly recognized than the New Yorker or Bostonian. ## II

From the reputation of Ōsaka as a center
of production and distribution, one would
imagine it the most modernized, the least
characteristically Japanese, of all Japanese
cities. But Ōsaka is the reverse. Fewer
Western clothes are to be seen in Ōsaka
than in any other large city of Japan. No
crowds are more attractively dressed, and no
streets more picturesque, than those of the
great marketplace.
Ōsaka is supposed to set many fashions;

to Japan, the dominant colors of male costume were dark—especially dark blue; any crowd of men usually presented a mass of this shade. Today the tones are lighter, and grays—warm grays, steel grays, bluish grays, purplish grays—seem to predominate. But there are also many pleasing variations—bronze colors, gold-browns, and "tea colors," for example. Women’s costumes are, of course, more varied; but the character of the fashions for adults of either sex indicates no tendency to abandon the rules of severe good taste. Bright colors appear only in the attire of children and of dancing girls, to whom are granted the privileges of perpetual youth. I may observe that the latest fashion in the silk upper-dress, or *haori*, of geisha, is a burning sky-blue—a tropical color that makes the profession of the wearer distinguishable miles away. The higher-class geisha, however, affect sobriety in dress. I must also speak of the long overcoats or overcloaks worn outdoors in cold weather by both sexes. In its architecture as much as in its fashion, Osaka remains almost as Japanese as anyone could wish. Although some wide thoroughfares exist, most of the streets are very narrow—even narrower than those of Kyoto. There are streets of three-story houses and streets of two-story houses; but there are square miles of houses only one story high. The great mass of the city is a collection of low wooden buildings with tiled roofs. Nevertheless, the streets are more interesting, brighter, and more quaint in their signs and sign-painting than the streets of Tokyo; and the city as a whole is more picturesque than Tokyo because of its waterways. It has appropriately been called the Venice of Japan; for it is traversed in all directions by canals, besides

Anything more unusual as a street vista than the view looking down one of these waterways can hardly be found in Japan. Still as a mirror’s surface, the canal flows between high stone embankments supporting the houses—houses of two or three stories, all propped out from the stonework so that their facades physically overhang the water. They are huddled together in a way suggesting pressure from behind; and this appearance of squeezing and crowding is strengthened by the lack of regularity in design—no house being exactly like another, but all having an indefinable Far-Eastern strangeness, a sort of distinctive character, that gives the sensation of being very far away in place and time. They push out peculiar little galleries with balustrades; barred, projecting, glassless windows with whimsical balconies under them, and small roofs over them like eyebrows; tiers of tiled and tilted awnings; and great eaves which, at certain hours, throw shadows down to the foun-

The colors ought to delight an artist—umbers and chocolates and chestnut-browns of old polished timber; warm yellows of mats and bamboo screens; creamy tones of stuccoed surfaces; cool grays of tiling. . . . The last such vista I saw was bewitched by a spring haze. It was early morning. Two hundred yards from the bridge on which I stood, the house fronts began to turn blue; farther on, they were transparently vapory; and yet farther, they seemed to melt away suddenly into the light—a procession of dreams. No other city in Japan has as many bridges as Ōsaka: districts are named after them, and distances are measured by them—calculating always from Koraibashi, the Bridge of the Koreans, as a center. The people of Ōsaka find their way to any place most easily by remembering the name of the bridge nearest to it. But since there are one hundred and eighty-nine main bridges, this method of navigation is of little use to a stranger. If he is a businessman, he can find whatever he needs without learning the names of the bridges. Ōsaka is the most commercially well-ordered city in the empire, and one of the best-ordered in the world. It has always been a city of guilds; and the various trades and industries are still gathered, according to ancient custom, in specific districts or particular streets. For instance, all the money-changers are

...in Kitahama, the Lombard Street of Japan; the dry-goods trade monopolizes Honmachi; the timber merchants are all in Nagabori and Nishi-Yokobori; the toy-makers are in Minami Kiuhojimachi and Kita Midōmae; the dealers in metal wares have Andojibashidōri to themselves; the druggists are in Doshiōmachi, and the cabinet-makers in Hachiman-suji. So it is with many other trades, and so with the places of amusement. The theaters are in the Dōtombori; the jugglers, singers, dancers, acrobats, and fortune-tellers are in the Sennichimae nearby.

The central part of Ōsaka contains many very large buildings, including theaters, refreshment houses, and hotels that have a reputation throughout the country. The number of edifices in Western style is, nevertheless, remarkably small. There are indeed between eight and nine hundred factory chimneys; but the factories, with few exceptions, are not constructed on Western plans. The really “foreign” buildings include a hotel, a prefectural hall with a mansard roof, a city hall with a classical porch of granite pillars, a good modern post office, a mint, an arsenal, and sundry

However, there is one purely foreign corner—the old <term>Concession</term>, dating back to a time before Kobé existed. Its streets were well laid out, and its buildings solidly constructed; but for various reasons, it has been abandoned to the missionaries—only one of the old firms, with perhaps an agency or two, remains open.

This deserted settlement is an oasis of silence in the great commercial wilderness.<note>The foreign legations left Ōsaka to take shelter at Kobé in 1868 during the civil war; for they could not be very well protected by their warships in Ōsaka. Once Kobé was settled, the advantages offered by its deep harbor decided the fate of the Ōsaka Concession.</note> No attempts have been made by the native merchants to imitate its styles of building; indeed, no Japanese city shows less favor than Ōsaka to <term>Occidental architecture</term>. This is not through a lack of appreciation, but because of economic experience. Ōsaka will build in Western style—with stone, brick, and iron—only when and where the advantage of so

<meta>This page continues the discussion on Ōsaka’s architectural and economic philosophy, specifically its cautious approach to adopting Western styles, before transitioning to a detailed description of the city's premier newspaper, the "Asahi Shimbun."</meta>

When there is a certainty, its merchants can make remarkable offers—like the one made to the government two years ago of $56,000,000 for the purchase and reconstruction of a railway. Of all the business houses in Ōsaka, the office of the “Asahi Shimbun” surprised me the most. The “Asahi Shimbun” is the greatest of Japanese newspapers—perhaps the greatest journal published in any Asian language. It is an illustrated daily, run very much like a Parisian newspaper—publishing a *feuilleton*, translations of foreign fiction, and columns of light, witty commentary on current events. It pays large sums to popular writers and spends heavily on correspondence and telegraphic news. Its illustrations—now created by a woman—offer as complete a reflection of all phases of Japanese life, old or new, as *Punch* provides of English life. It uses <term>perfecting presses</term>, charters special trains, and has a circulation reaching into most parts of the empire. So, I certainly expected to find the “Asahi Shimbun” office

<meta>This page continues the description of the "Asahi Shimbun" office building in Ōsaka and discusses the city's role as a commercial hub and its rigorous system of apprenticeship.</meta>

But it proved to be an old-style *Samurai-yashiki*—about the most quiet and modest-looking place in the entire district where it was located.

I must confess that all this sober and sensible conservatism delighted me. The competitive power of Japan must long depend upon its ability to maintain the old simplicity of life.

->III<-

Ōsaka is the great commercial school of the empire. From all parts of Japan, young men are sent there to learn particular branches of industry or trade. There are many applications for any vacancy, and the businessmen are said to be very cautious in choosing their *detchi*, or apprentice-clerks. Careful inquiries are made regarding the personal character and family history of applicants. No money is paid by the parents or relatives of the apprentices. The term of service varies according to the nature of the trade or industry, but it is generally quite as long as the term of apprenticeship in Europe—and in some branches of

<meta>This page continues the description of the daily life and strict discipline of the *detchi* apprentices in Ōsaka, including their physical confinement and the legend of Daruma.</meta>

<page-num>149</page-num>
<header>IN OSAKA</header>

except for the time necessary for sleep; he must work quietly but steadily from dawn until late in the evening; he must be satisfied with the simplest diet, must keep himself neat, and must never show ill temper. He is not expected to have any "wild oats," and no chance is given to him to sow them. Some <term>detchi</term> never even leave their shop, day or night, for months at a time—sleeping on the same mats where they sit during business hours. The trained salesmen in the large silk stores are especially confined indoors, and their unhealthy paleness is proverbial. Year after year they sit in the same place for twelve or fifteen hours every day; and you wonder why their legs do not fall off, like those of <term>Daruma</term>.¹

Occasionally there are moral breakdowns. Perhaps a <term>detchi</term> misappropriates some of the shop's money and spends it on wild living. Perhaps he does even worse. But,

--- Page 162 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the treatment and discipline of *detchi* (apprentices) in Ōsaka, following the description of their physical hardships and confinement on the previous page.</meta>

<page-num>150</page-num>
<header>*IN OSAKA*</header>

Whatever the case may be, he rarely considers running away. If he goes on a wild spree, he hides himself afterward for a day or two; then he returns on his own to confess and ask for forgiveness. He will be forgiven for two, three, or perhaps even four escapades—provided that he shows no signs of a truly wicked heart—and he will be lectured about his weakness in relation to his future prospects, to the feelings of his family, to the honor of his ancestors, and to business requirements in general. The hardships of his situation are kindly considered, and he is never dismissed for a minor transgression. A dismissal would probably ruin him for life; and every effort is made to make him aware of certain dangers. Ōsaka is truly the most unsafe place in Japan to play the fool; its dangerous and depraved classes are more to be feared than those of the capital; and the daily news of the great city provides the apprentice with tragic examples of men reduced to poverty or driven to self-destruction through neglecting those very rules of conduct that it is part of his duty to learn.

Sometimes, again, the affection of a <term>detchi</term> may show itself in strange extremes. Last year there was a curious case. The only son of a merchant—a lad of twelve—died of cholera during the epidemic. A <term>detchi</term> of fourteen, who had been much attached to the dead boy, committed suicide shortly after the funeral by throwing himself down in front of a train. He left a letter, of which the following is a fairly close translation—personal pronouns being absent in the original:

> “*Over a very long time, great help was received; honorable mercy even, not to be declared in words. Now going to die, unfaithful in the extreme; yet in another state, being reborn, this honorable mercy will be repaid. Spirit anxious only regarding the matter of my little sister.*"

->IV<-

It is not true that Old Japan is rapidly disappearing. It cannot disappear for at least another hundred years; perhaps it will never entirely disappear. Many curious and beautiful things have vanished; but Old Japan survives in art, in faith, in customs and habits, in the hearts and the homes of the people. It may be found everywhere by those who know how to look for it—and nowhere more easily than in this great city of shipbuilding, watchmaking, beer brewing, and cotton spinning. I confess that I went to Ōsaka chiefly to see the temples, especially the famous Tennōji.

Tennōji, or, more correctly, Shitennōji, the Temple of the Four Deva Kings,<note>¹ They defend the four quarters of the world. In Japanese, their names are Jikoku, Komoku, Zocho, Bishamon (or Tamon); in Sanskrit, Dhritarashtra, Virupaksha, Virudhaka, and Vaisravana—the Kuvera of Brahmanism.</note> is one of

<meta>This page continues the description of the ancient Shitennōji temple in Osaka, detailing its founding by Shōtoku Taishi and the legendary battle that led to its creation.</meta>

<page-num>153</page-num>
<header>*IN OSAKA*</header>

the oldest Buddhist temples in Japan. It was founded early in the seventh century by Umayado-no-Oji, now called <term>Shōtoku Taishi</term>, son of the Emperor <term>Yōmei</term>, and prince regent under the Empress <term>Suiko</term> (572–621 A.D.). He has been well called the Constantine of Japanese Buddhism; for he decided the future of Buddhism in the Empire, first by a great battle in the reign of his father, Yōmei <term>Tennō</term>, and afterwards by legal enactments and by the patronage of Buddhist learning.

The previous Emperor, <term>Bitatsu Tennō</term>, had permitted the preaching of Buddhism by Korean priests and had built two temples. But under the reign of Yōmei, one <term>Mononobé no Moriya</term>, a powerful noble and a bitter opponent of the foreign religion, rebelled against such tolerance, burned the temples, banished the priests, and offered battle to the imperial forces. According to tradition, these forces were being driven back when the Emperor's son—then only sixteen years old—vowed that if he were victorious, he would build a temple to the <term>Four Deva Kings</term>. Instantly, a colossal figure towered at his side in the fight, and before its face the powers of Moriya broke and fled away. The rout of the

The plaintive murmur of *<term>Namu Amida Butsu</term>*; the clanging of the bell; the deep humming of the priest’s voice, reciting the <term>Sutras</term>; the tinkle of falling coins; the sweet, heavy smell of incense; the passionless golden beauty of the Buddha in his shrine; the vibrant radiance of the toys; the shadows cast by the baby dresses; the multicolored wonder of that bell-rope made of bibs; the happy laughter of the children playing on the floor—all created for me an experience of uncanny pathos never to be forgotten.

Not far from the bell tower is another unusual building, which shelters a sacred spring. In the middle of the floor is an opening, perhaps ten feet long by eight feet wide, surrounded by a railing. Looking down over the railing, you see, in the dimness below, a large stone basin into which water is pouring from the mouth of a great stone tortoise, black with age and only half-visible—its hind part reaching back into the darkness under the floor. This water is called the Spring of the Tortoise, or *<term>Kamé-i-Sui</term>*. The basin into which it flows is more than half

<meta>This page continues the description of the Shitennoji temple complex in Osaka, specifically detailing the ritual use of the sacred Tortoise Spring (Kamé-i-Sui) for honoring the deceased.</meta>

full of white paper—countless slips of white paper, each bearing in Chinese text the <term>kaimyō</term>, or Buddhist posthumous name of a dead person. In a matted recess of the building sits a priest who, for a small fee, writes the <term>kaimyō</term>. The purchaser—a relative or friend of the dead—puts one end of the written slip into the mouth of a bamboo cup, or rather a bamboo joint, fixed at right angles to the end of a long pole. By aid of this pole, he lowers the paper, with the written side up, to the mouth of the tortoise, and holds it under the gush of water—repeating a Buddhist invocation the while—until it is washed out into the basin. When I visited the spring there was a dense crowd; and several <term>kaimyō</term> were being held under the mouth of the tortoise; meanwhile, numbers of pious people were waiting, with papers in their hands, for a chance to use the poles. The murmuring of *<term>Namu Amida Butsu</term>* was itself like the sound of rushing water. I was told that the basin becomes filled with <term>kaimyō</term> every few days; then it is emptied, and the papers are burned. If this is true, it is a remarkable proof of the force of Buddhist faith in this busy commercial city;

<summary>The author describes a ritual at the Tortoise Spring where participants use long poles to wash slips of paper bearing the names of the deceased under a stream of sacred water. This practice demonstrates the profound spiritual devotion found within the commercial atmosphere of Osaka.</summary>

for many thousands of such slips of paper would be needed to fill the basin. It is said that the water carries the names of the dead and the prayers of the living to Shōtoku Taishi, who uses his powers of intercession with Amida on behalf of the faithful.

In the chapel called the Taishi-Dō, there are statues of Shōtoku Taishi and his attendants. The figure of the prince, seated upon a chair of honor, is life-sized and colored; he is dressed in the fashion of twelve hundred years ago, wearing a picturesque cap and Chinese or Korean shoes with upturned points. One may see the same costume in the designs upon very old porcelain or very old screens. But the face, despite his drooping Chinese mustaches, is a typical Japanese face—dignified, kindly, and passionless. I turned from the faces of the statues to the faces of the people around me to see the same types—to meet the same quiet, half-curious, inscrutable gaze.

In powerful contrast to the ancient structures of Tennōji are the vast Nishi and Higashi Hongwanji, almost exact counterparts of the

<meta>This page continues the comparison between the ancient structures of Shitennoji and the more modern, massive Hongwanji temples in Osaka.</meta>

<page-num>162</page-num>
<header>IN OSAKA</header>

Nishi and Higashi Hongwanji of Tōkyō. Nearly every great city of Japan has a pair of such Hongwanji (Temples of the True Vow)—one belonging to the Western (Nishi), the other to the Eastern (Higashi) branch of this great Shin sect, founded in the thirteenth century.¹ Varying in size according to the wealth and religious importance of the locality, but usually built upon the same general plan, they may be said to represent the most modern and the most purely Japanese form of Buddhist architecture—immense, dignified, and magnificent.

But they likewise represent the almost Protestant severity of the rite in regard to symbols, icons, and external forms. Their plain and heavy gates are never guarded by the giant Ni-Ō; there is no swarming of dragons and demons under their enormous eaves;

---

<note>¹ The division of the sect during the seventeenth century into two branches had a political, not a religious cause; and the sections remain religiously united. Their abbots are of Imperial descent, which is the origin of their title <term>Monzeki</term>, or Imperial Offspring. Travelers may observe that the walls enclosing the temple grounds of this sect bear the same decorative moldings as those of the walls of the Imperial residences.</note>

<summary>The author describes the architectural grandeur and "Protestant-like" simplicity of the Nishi and Higashi Hongwanji temples, which represent a purely Japanese style of Buddhism. He notes that while the sect is divided into two branches for historical political reasons, they remain religiously united and maintain close ties to the Imperial family.</summary>
<keywords>Nishi Hongwanji, Higashi Hongwanji, Shin sect, Buddhist architecture, Monzeki, Japanese history, Osaka, religious sects</keywords>

— no golden multitudes of Buddhas or Bodhisattvas rise, row upon row, by tiers of halos, through the twilight of their sanctuaries; — no unusual or touching signs of grateful faith are ever suspended from their high ceilings, hung before their altars, or fastened to the gratings of their doorways; — they contain no <term>ex-votos</term> <note>votive offerings</note>, no paper knots recording prayer, and no symbolic image except one — and that is usually small — the figure of Amida. The reader likely knows that the Hongwanji sect represents a movement in Buddhism not unlike what Unitarianism represents in Liberal Christianity. In its rejection of celibacy and all ascetic practices; its prohibition of charms, fortune-telling, votive offerings, and even all prayer except prayer for salvation; its emphasis on hard work as the duty of life; its maintenance of the sanctity of marriage as a religious bond; its doctrine of one eternal Buddha as Father and Savior; its promise of Paradise after death as the immediate reward for a good life; and, above all, in its educational zeal — the religion of the "Sect of the Pure Land" may be justly said to have

<summary>The author describes the stark, icon-free interiors of Hongwanji temples and compares the sect's rational, reformist theology to Liberal Christianity's Unitarianism. <meta>This page continues the description of the Hongwanji (Shin-shū) sect, focusing on its appeal across different social classes and its relationship with Western intellectualism.</meta>

<page-num>164</page-num>
# IN OSAKA

much in common with the progressive forms of Western Christianity, and it has certainly won the respect of the few cultured men who find their way into the missionary legion. Judged by its wealth, its respectability, and its opposition to the cruder forms of Buddhist superstition, it might be supposed the least emotional of all forms of Buddhism. But in some respects it is probably the most emotional. No other Buddhist sect can make such appeals to the faith and love of the common people as those which brought into being the amazing Eastern Hongwanji temple of Kyōto. Yet while able to reach the simplest minds through special methods of doctrinal teaching, the Hongwanji sect can make an equally strong appeal to the intellectual classes because of its scholarship. Quite a few of its priests are graduates of the leading universities of the West; and some have won European reputations in various departments of Buddhist learning. Whether the older Buddhist sects are likely to dwindle away before the constantly increasing power of the Shin-shū is at least an interesting question. Certainly the latter has everything in its favor,

On the other hand, one is tempted to doubt the effectiveness of such advantages in a struggle against habits of thought and feeling older by many centuries than **Shinshū**. Perhaps the West provides a precedent on which to base predictions. Remembering how strong **Roman Catholicism** remains today, how little it has changed since the days of **Luther**, and how powerless our progressive creeds are to satisfy the old spiritual hunger for some visible object of worship — something to touch, or hold close to the heart — it becomes difficult to believe that the <term>iconolatry</term> of the more ancient **Buddhist** sects will not continue for hundreds of years to hold a large place in popular affection. Again, it is worth noting that one curious obstacle to the expansion of the **Shinshū** is found in a very deeply rooted racial feeling on the subject of self-sacrifice. # IN OSAKA

Ancient ideals are by no means dead; and the majority of Japanese Buddhists still disapprove of the relatively comfortable lives of the Shinshū priesthood. In some of the more remote provinces, where Shinshū is viewed with particular disfavor, one may often hear children singing a mischievous song (*Shinshū bozu e mon da!*), which might be freely translated as:

->To be a Shinshū priest—<-
->What a fine thing!<-
->He has a wife, he has a child,<-
->And he eats good fish.<-

It reminded me of those popular criticisms of Buddhist conduct uttered during the time of the Buddha himself, which are so often recorded in the Vinaya texts—almost like a refrain:
> “Then the people were annoyed; and they murmured and complained, saying: ‘These men act like people who are still enjoying the pleasures of this world!’ And they reported the matter to the Blessed One.”

ancient histories. One such site is Kōzu-no-yashiro, where the people pray to the spirit of Nintoku—the most beloved in memory of all Japanese emperors. He had a palace on the same hill where his shrine now stands; and this site—from which a fine view of the city can be seen—is the scene of a pleasing legend preserved in the *Kojiki*:

> . . . "Then the Heavenly Sovereign, climbing a high mountain and looking out over the land all around, spoke, saying: 'In the whole land no smoke rises; the land is entirely poverty-stricken. Therefore, I exempt all the people from taxes and forced labor for the next three years.' Because of this, the great palace fell into disrepair, and the rain leaked in everywhere; yet no repairs were made. The rain that leaked in was caught in troughs, and the residents moved to places where there was no leakage. Later, when the Heavenly Sovereign looked upon the land, smoke was abundant throughout the country. So, finding the people wealthy, he now collected taxes and required forced labor. Therefore, the peasantry prospered and did not suffer from the labor requirements. So, in praise of that noble reign, it was called the Reign of the Emperor-Sage." ¹

<note>¹ See Professor Chamberlain’s translation of the *Kojiki*, section CXXI.</note>

That was fifteen hundred years ago. Now, if the good Emperor could see from his shrine of Kōzu—as thousands must believe he does—the smoke of modern Osaka, he might well think, “My people are becoming too rich.”

Outside of the city, there is an even more famous Shinto temple, Sumiyoshi, dedicated to certain sea gods who aided Empress Jingo in conquering Korea. At Sumiyoshi, there are pretty child-priestesses, beautiful grounds, and an enormous pond spanned by a bridge so humped that you must cling to the parapet to cross it without taking off your shoes. In Sakai, there is the Buddhist temple of Myōkokuji, whose garden contains some very old palm trees; one of them, removed by Nobunaga in the sixteenth century, is said to have cried out and lamented until it was returned to the temple. You can see the ground under these palms covered with what looks like a thick, shiny, messy mass of fur—half reddish and half silvery gray. It is not fur. It is a pile of millions of needles thrown there by pilgrims “to feed the palms,” because these trees are said to love

Speaking of trees, I should mention the Naniwaya “Kasa-matsu,” or Hat-Pine—not so much because it is an extraordinary tree, but because it supports a large family who run a small tea-house on the road to Sakai. The branches of the tree have been trained outwards and downwards over a framework of poles, so that the whole tree takes on the appearance of an enormous green hat of the shape worn by peasants, called a *Kasa*. The pine is barely six feet high, but covers perhaps twenty square yards; its trunk, of course, is not visible at all from outside the framework supporting the branches. Many people visit the house to look at the pine and drink a cup of tea; and nearly every visitor buys some souvenir of it—perhaps a woodcut of the tree, a printed copy of verses written by a poet in praise of it, or a girl’s hair-pin, the top of which is a perfect little green model of the tree, framework of poles and all, with one tiny stork perched on it. The owners of the Naniwaya, as their tea-house is called, are not only able to make a good

I do not intend to tax my reader’s patience with descriptions of the other famous temples of Osaka—several of which are enormously old and have very curious legends attached to them. But I may venture a few words about the cemetery of the Temple of One Soul—or better, perhaps, the Temple of a Single Mind: Isshinji. The monuments there are the most extraordinary I have ever seen. Near the main gate is the tomb of a wrestler—Asahigoro Hachiro. His name is chiseled upon a large disk of stone, probably weighing a ton; and this disk is supported on the back of a stone image of a wrestler—a grotesque figure, with gilded eyes starting from their sockets and features apparently distorted by effort. It is a very strange thing—half-comical and half-furious in appearance. Close by is the tomb of one Hirayama Hambei—a monument shaped like a *hyotan*—that is to say, like a wine-gourd such as travelers use for carrying sake. The most common form of *hyotan* resembles that

My readers probably know that the Japanese *tanuki*¹ is credited with the power of assuming human shape, and of making musical sounds like the booming of a hand-drum by tapping upon its belly. It is often said to disguise itself as a Buddhist priest for mischievous purposes, and to be very fond of sake. Of course, such images in a cemetery represent nothing more than eccentricities, and are judged to be in bad taste. One is reminded of certain humorous paintings and inscriptions on Greek and Roman tombs, expressing in regard to death—or rather in regard to life—a sentiment, or an affectation of sentiment, repellent to modern feeling.

### V

I said in a former essay that a Japanese city is little more than a wilderness of wooden sheds, and Osaka is no exception.

---

<note>¹ Although *tanuki* is commonly translated as "badger," the creature so called is not a real badger, but a kind of fruit-fox. It is also termed the "raccoon-faced dog." The true badger is, however, also found in Japan.</note>

--- Page 185 ---
<meta>This page continues the transition from the discussion of cemetery eccentricities to the architectural character of Japanese cities, specifically focusing on the contrast between the plain exteriors and beautiful interiors of homes in Ōsaka.</meta>

<page-num>173</page-num>
<header>IN OSAKA</header>

But internally, a very large number of the frail wooden dwellings of any Japanese city are works of art; and perhaps no city possesses more charming homes than Ōsaka. Kyōto is, indeed, much richer in gardens—there being comparatively little space for gardens in Ōsaka; but I am speaking of the houses only. Externally, a Japanese street may appear little better than a row of wooden barns or stables, but the interior of any dwelling in it may be a wonder of beauty. Usually the outside of a Japanese house is not at all beautiful, though it may have a certain pleasing oddity of form; and in many cases the walls of the rear or sides are covered with charred boards, of which the blackened and hardened surfaces are said to resist heat and damp better than any coating of paint or stucco could do. Except, perhaps, for the outside of a coal-shed, nothing dingier-looking could be imagined. But the other side of the black walls may be an *aesthetic* delight. The comparative cheapness of the residence does not much affect this possibility—for the Japanese excel all nations in obtaining the maximum of beauty with the minimum of cost;

<summary>The author describes how Japanese houses, particularly in Ōsaka, contrast drab and dingy exteriors—often featuring charred wood siding—with artistic and beautiful interiors. He highlights the Japanese ability to create high aesthetic value despite low construction costs.</summary>
<keywords>Ōsaka, Kyōto, Japanese architecture, interior design, charred boards, aesthetics, urban housing, shou sugi ban</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the comparison between Japanese and Western aesthetics, specifically contrasting the artistic value of Japanese home interiors with Western decorative habits.</meta>

<page-num>174</page-num>
<header>IN OSAKA</header>

while the most industrially advanced of Western peoples—the practical Americans—have so far only succeeded in obtaining the minimum of beauty with the maximum cost!
Much about Japanese interiors can be learned from Morse's "Japanese Homes"; but even that admirable book gives only a black-and-white idea of the subject. More than half of the charm of such interiors is the almost inexplicable touch of color. To illustrate Mr. Morse's work in a way that captures this colorful charm would be a more expensive and more difficult feat than the production of Racinet's "Historical Costumes." Even so, the soft light, the atmosphere of perfect tranquility, and the revelations of delicacy and refinement waiting for the eye in every corner of rooms seemingly designed to catch and hold the feeling of eternal summer would remain unimagined. Five years ago, I wrote that a slight acquaintance with the Japanese art of flower arrangement had made it impossible for me to tolerate the sight of that vulgarity—or rather, brutality—which we in the West call a "bouquet." Today, I must add that familiarity with Japanese interiors has equally disgusted me with

<keywords>Japanese architecture, Edward S. Morse, interior design, aesthetics, color theory, flower arrangement, Western vs Eastern art, Ōsaka</keywords>

--- Page 187 ---
<meta>This page continues the comparison between Japanese and Western aesthetics, specifically contrasting the artistic value of Japanese home interiors with Western decorative habits.</meta>

<page-num>175</page-num>
<header>IN OSAKA</header>

Western interiors, no matter how spacious or comfortable or richly furnished. Returning now to Western life, I would feel like Thomas-the-Rhymer revisiting a world of ugliness and sorrow after seven years in a fairyland.

It is possible, as has been claimed (though I cannot believe it), that Western artists have little more to learn from the study of Japanese pictorial art. But I am quite sure that our house-builders have universes of facts to learn—especially regarding the treatment and tinting of surfaces—from the study of Japanese interiors. Whether the countless styles of these interiors can even be classified seems like a doubtful question to me. I do not think that in a hundred thousand Japanese houses there are two interiors precisely alike—(excluding, of course, the homes of the poorest classes)—for the designer never repeats himself if he can help it. The lesson he has to teach is the lesson of perfect taste combined with inexhaustible variety. Taste!—what a rare thing it is in our Western world!—and how independent of material, how intuitive, how impossible to communicate to the unrefined! But taste is a Japanese birthright.

<header>IN OSAKA</header>

It is present everywhere, although it varies in the quality of its development according to circumstances and the heritage resulting from those circumstances. The average Westerner recognizes only its more common forms—primarily those made familiar through commercial exports. And, as a general rule, what the West admires most in conventional Japanese taste is considered rather vulgar in Japan. This is not to say that we are wrong to admire anything that is beautiful in itself. Even the designs printed in shades on a two-cent towel may be truly great pictures; they are sometimes created by excellent artists. But the aristocratic severity of the best Japanese taste—the exquisite complexity of its refinements in determining proportion, quality, tone, and restraint—has never even been dreamed of by the West. Nowhere is this taste displayed as finely as in private interiors, particularly regarding color. The rules of color in the composition of a set of rooms are no less demanding than the rules of color in the matter of dress, though they permit considerable variety. The simple color tones of a private house are enough to indicate the owner’s degree of culture. There is no painting, no varnishing,

no wallpapering—only the staining and polishing of particular parts, and a sort of paper border about fifteen inches wide fixed along the bottom of a wall to protect it during cleaning and dusting operations. The plastering may be made with sands of different hues, or with fragments of shell and <term>nacre</term>, or with quartz crystal, or with mica; the surface may imitate granite, or may sparkle like <term>copper pyrites</term>, or may look exactly like a rich mass of bark; but, whatever the material, the tint used must show the same faultless taste that rules in the colors of silks for robes and sashes. . . . As yet, all this interior world of beauty—just because it is an interior world—is closed to the foreign tourist: he can find at most only suggestions of it in the rooms of such old-fashioned <term>inns</term> or <term>tea-houses</term> as he may visit in the course of his travels.
