I have been in hundreds of Japanese hotels, and I remember only one in which I could find nothing curious or pretty—a ramshackle shelter hastily put up to attract business at a newly opened railway station.

To the pillar was fastened a bamboo cup containing a pair of exquisite blossoming sprays of wisteria—one pink and the other white. ### VII

My last day in <term>Ōsaka</term> was spent shopping—chiefly in the districts of the toy-makers and the silk merchants. A Japanese acquaintance, who was a shopkeeper himself, took me around and showed me extraordinary things until my eyes ached. We went to a famous silk-house—a tumultuous place, so crowded that we had some trouble squeezing our way to the floor-platform, which, in every Japanese shop, serves as both chairs and counter. Scores of barefooted, light-limbed boys were running over it, carrying bundles of merchandise to customers; for in such shops, there is no shelving of stock. The Japanese salesman never leaves his squatting-place on the mats; but, on learning what you want, he shouts an order, and boys immediately run to you with armfuls of samples. After you have

At the time of our visit, the greater part of the matted floor space was one splendid, shimmering confusion of tossed silks and velvets of a hundred colors and a hundred prices. Near the main entrance, an elderly superintendent, plump and jovial of aspect like the God of Wealth, looked after arriving customers. Two keen-eyed men, standing upon an elevation in the middle of the shop and slowly turning round and round in opposite directions, kept watch for thieves; and other watchers were posted at the side doors. (Japanese shop thieves, by the way, are very clever; and I am told that nearly every large store loses considerably by them in the course of the year.) In a side wing of the building, under a low skylight, I saw busy ranks of bookkeepers, cashiers, and correspondents squatting before little desks less than two feet high. Each of the numerous salesmen was attending to many customers at once. The rush of business was big; and the rapidity with which the work was being done testified

"Probably about two hundred here; there are several branch houses. In this shop the work is very hard; but the working hours are shorter than in most of the silk houses, — not more than twelve hours a day."

"What about salaries?" I inquired.

"No salaries."

"Is all the work of this firm done without pay?"

"Perhaps one or two of the very cleverest salesmen may get something, — not exactly a salary, but a little special remuneration every month; and the old superintendent — (he has been forty years in the house) — gets a salary. The rest get nothing but their food."

"Good food?"

"No, very cheap, coarse food. After a man has served his time here — fourteen or fifteen years — he may be helped to open a small store of his own."

"Many of the <term>detchi</term> are graduates of commercial schools. Those sent to a commercial school begin their apprenticeship much later; and they are said to not make as good <term>detchi</term> as those taught from childhood."

"A Japanese clerk in a foreign store is much better off."

"We do not think so," answered my friend very positively. "Some who speak English well, and have learned the foreign way of doing business, may get fifty or sixty dollars a month for seven or eight hours of work a day. But they are not treated the same way as they are treated in a Japanese house. Clever men do not like to work under foreigners. Foreigners used to be very cruel to their Japanese clerks and servants."

"But not now?" I queried.

"Perhaps not often. They have found that it is dangerous. But they used to beat and kick them. The Japanese think it is shameful to even speak unkindly to <term>detchi</term> or servants. In a house like this, there is no unkindness. The owners and the superintendents never speak roughly. You see how very hard all these men and boys are working without pay."

# IN OSAKA

“No foreigner could get Japanese people to work like that, even for high wages. I have worked in foreign houses, and I know.”

It is no exaggeration to say that most of the intelligent service provided in Japanese trade and skilled industry is unpaid. Perhaps one-third of the business work of the country is done without wages; the relationship between master and servant is one of perfect trust on both sides, and absolute obedience is guaranteed by the simplest of moral conditions. This fact was what most deeply impressed me during my stay in Osaka.

I found myself wondering about it while the evening train to Nara carried me away from the cheerful turmoil of the great metropolis. I continued to think of it while watching the dusk deepen over the miles of roofs—over the gathering factory chimneys forever sending up their offering of smoke to the shrine of the good Nintoku. Suddenly, above the twinkling of countless lamps—above the white star-points of electric lights—above the growing dusk

# VIII

## BUDDHIST ALLUSIONS IN JAPANESE FOLK-
## SONG

PERHAPS only a Japanese representative of the older culture could fully inform us to what degree the mental soil of the race has been saturated and fertilized by Buddhist idealism. At all events, no European could do so; for to understand the whole relation of Far-Eastern religion to Far-Eastern life would require not only such scholarship, but also such experience as no European could gain in a lifetime. Yet for even the Western stranger, there are signs everywhere of what Buddhism has been to Japan in the past. All the arts and most of the industries repeat Buddhist legends to the eye trained in symbolism; and there is scarcely an object of handiwork possessing any beauty or significance of form—from the plaything of a child to the heirloom of a prince—which does not in some

<summary>The author reflects on the profound influence of Buddhist idealism on the Japanese people, noting that it permeates all aspects of life, art, and industry in ways a Westerner can hardly fully grasp.</summary>
<keywords>Buddhism, Japanese folk-song, idealism, East Asian religion, symbolism, cultural heritage</keywords>

--- Page 198 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the pervasive influence of Buddhism in Japan, specifically how it manifests in both high art and everyday objects, as well as in the landscape and modern infrastructure.</meta>

way proclaim the ancient debt to Buddhism of the craft that made it. One may discern Buddhist thoughts in the cheap cotton prints from an Osaka mill no less than in the patterned silks of Kyoto. The reliefs on an iron kettle, or the bronze elephant-heads forming the handles of a shopkeeper's *hibachi*; — the patterns of screen paper, or the most common ornamental woodwork of a gateway; — the etchings on a metal pipe, or the enameling on a costly vase — may all relate the traditions of faith with equal eloquence. There are reflections or echoes of Buddhist teaching in the composition of a garden; — in the countless ideographs of the long vistas of shop signs; — in the wonderfully expressive names given to certain fruits and flowers; — in the names of mountains, capes, waterfalls, villages — even of modern railway stations. And the new civilization does not yet seem to have much affected the influence manifested in this way. Trains and steamers now carry more pilgrims to famous shrines every year than visited them ever before in a twelve-month period; — despite clocks and watches, the temple bells still mark

the passing of time for millions; — the speech of the people is still filled with the poetry of Buddhist utterances; — literature and drama still teem with Buddhist expressions; — and the most ordinary voices of the street — songs of children playing, a chorus of laborers at their work, even the cries of traveling street vendors — often recall to me some story of saints and Bodhisattvas, or the text of some sutra.

Such an experience first gave me the idea of making a collection of songs containing Buddhist expressions or allusions. But in view of the scale of the subject, I could not decide immediately where to begin. A bewildering variety of Japanese songs — a variety for which the mere list of names would fill pages — offers material of this description. Among noteworthy types may be mentioned the *Utai*, dramatic songs, mostly composed by high priests, of which probably no ten lines exist without some allusion to Buddhism; — the *Naga-uta*, songs often of extraordinary length; — and the *Jōruri*, entire romances in verse, with which professional singers can delight their audiences for five or six hours at a time. The mere size of such compo—

# BUDDHIST ALLUSIONS

sitions necessarily excluded them from my plan; but there remained a legion of briefer forms to choose among. I resolved at last to limit my undertaking mainly to *dodoitsu*—little songs of only twenty-six syllables, arranged in four lines (7, 7, 7, 5). They are more regular in construction than the street songs discussed in a former paper; but they are essentially popular, and therefore more widely representative of Buddhist influences than many superior kinds of composition could be. Out of a very large number collected for me, I have selected between forty and fifty as typical of the class.

Perhaps those pieces which reflect the ideas of pre-existence and of future rebirths will prove especially interesting to the Western reader—much less because of poetic worth than because of comparative novelty. We have very little English verse of any class containing fancies of this kind; but they swarm in Japanese poetry even as commonplaces and conventionalisms. Such an exquisite thing as Rossetti's "Sudden Light," bewitching us chiefly through the penetrative subtlety of a

thought condemned by all our orthodoxies for eighteen hundred years—could interest a Japanese person only as the exceptional rendering, by a Westerner, of ideas and feelings familiar even to the most ignorant peasant. Certainly, no one will be able to find in these Japanese verses—or, rather, in my own wretchedly plain translations of them—even a hint of anything like the ghostly delicacy of Rossetti's imagination:

->I have been here before, —<-
->But when or how I cannot tell:<-
->I know the grass beyond the door,<-
->The sweet, keen smell,<-
->The sighing sound, the lights along the shore.<-

->You have been mine before, —<-
->How long ago I may not know:<-
->But just when at that swallow's soar<-
->Your neck turned so,<-
->Some veil did fall, — I knew it all of yore.<-

Yet what a strange, living difference there is between such enigmatically delicate handling of thoughts classified as forbidden fruit in the Western Eden of Dreams and the everyday Japanese expressions that spring directly out of ancient Eastern faith!

-><note>1. *Iro wa shian no*<-
->*Hoka to-wa iedo,*<-
->*Kore mo saki-sho no*<-
->*En de aro.*<-

"<term>En</term>" is a Buddhist word signifying affinity—the relation of cause and effect from life to life.</note>

-><note>2. *Sode suri-o no mo*<-
->*Tashō no en yo,*<-
->*Mashite futari ga*<-
->*Fukai naka.*<-

Reference is made here to the old Buddhist proverb: *Sode no furi-awase mo tashō no en*—"Even the touching of sleeves while passing is caused by some affinity operating from former lives."</note>

<note>3. The Buddhist word "<term>Kwahō</term>" is commonly used instead of other synonyms for Karma (such as <term>ingwa</term>, <term>innen</term>, etc.) to signify the good, rather than the bad, results of action in previous lives. However, it is sometimes used in both meanings. Here there seems to be a reference to the proverbial expression, *Kwahō no yoi hito* (literally: a person of good <term>Kwahō</term>), meaning a fortunate individual.</note>

# IN JAPANESE FOLK-SONG

Many songs of this class refer to the customary vow that lovers make to belong to each other for more lives than one—a vow perhaps originally inspired by the Buddhist aphorism:

->Oya-ko wa, is-sé;<-
->Fūfu wa, ni-sé;<-
->Shujū wa, san-zé.<-

> “The relationship between parent and child is for one life; that of husband and wife, for two lives; that of master and servant, for three lives.”

Although the tender relationship is thus limited to the span of two lives, the vow—as Japanese dramas testify, and as the letters of those who take their own lives for love bear witness—is often passionately made for seven. The following selections show a considerable variety of tone—ranging from the pathetic to the satirical—in the treatment of this topic:

->*I have cut my hair for his sake; but the deeper relationship be-*<-
->*tween us*<-
->*cannot be cut in this life, nor yet in another.* <note>1</note><-

-><note>1. *Kami wa kitté mo*<-
->*Ni-sé madé kaketa*<-
->*Fukai enishi wa*<-
->*Kiru mono ka?*<-

Literally: “Although the hair is cut, until two existences...”</note>

*She looks at the portrait of the one to whom she is promised for two lives:*
> *Happy memories return, and each brings a smile to her face.<note>1</note>*

*If in this present life we can never hope to be united,*
> *Then we shall first start our household in the Lotus-Palace beyond.<note>2</note>*

*Have we not spoken the vow that binds us for two lives?*
> *If we must separate now, I can only wish to die.*

"...a deep relationship—how can it be cut?" By the mention of hair-cutting, we know the speaker is a woman. Her husband, or possibly her betrothed lover, is dead; according to Buddhist custom, she shows her desire to remain faithful to his memory by sacrificing her hair. For detailed information on this subject, see the chapter "Of Women's Hair" in my *Glimpses of Unfamiliar Japan*.

<note>1. *Ni-sé to chigirishi*
*Shashin wo nagamé*
*Omoi-idashité*
->*Warai-gao.*<-

Literally: "Two existences that formed an alliance; looking at the photograph, the memories bring a smile to her face." The use of the term *shashin* (photograph) shows that the poem is not old.</note>

<note>2. *Totémo kono yo dé*
*Sowaré-nu naraba*
*Hasu no uténa dé*
->*Ara sétai.*<-

Literally: "If we cannot live together in this world by any means, then we shall start a new household in the Palace of the Lotus." It is with this thought that lovers voluntarily die together; the song might be called a song of *jōshi* (love-suicide).</note>

Every human being has lived and is destined to live countless lives; yet the happy moments of any single existence are no less precious because of that: —

> *Not to have met for even one night is truly a cause for sorrow;*
> *Since in a single lifetime, the same night never comes twice.*

But just as an unusually warm summer is likely to signal a winter of exceptional severity, so too much happiness in this life may mean great suffering in the next: —

> *I always suffer like this! . . . I think that in my previous life,*
> *I must have been too happy — I did not suffer enough.*

I offer some free translations from these, together with one selection from a class of compositions more elaborate and usually much longer than the <term>dodoitsu</term>, called <term>hauta</term>. In the original, at least, my selection from the <term>hauta</term> — which contains a charming simile about the firefly — is by far the prettiest: —

Evidently this is the remorseful pleading of a jealous lover. The next might be the answer of the girl whose tears he had caused to flow:

> *I cannot imagine at all by what strange manner of <term>ingwa</term>*

A more remarkable reference to the Wheel of Karma is the following: —

> *Father and mother forbade it, and so I gave up my lover; —*
> *Yet still, with the whirl of the Wheel, the thought of him comes and goes.<sup>1</sup>*

This is a *hauta*: —

> *There are numberless insects that call from dawn to evening,*
> *Crying, “I love! I love!” — but the Firefly's silent passion,*
> *Which makes its body burn, is deeper than all their longing.*
> *My love is just like that . . . yet I cannot imagine through what <term>ingwa</term>*
> *I opened my heart — alas! — to a person who is not sincere!<sup>2</sup>*

-><sup>1</sup> Oya no iken dé<-
->Akirameta no wo<-
->Mata mo rin-yé dé<-
->Omoi-dasu.<-

The Buddhist word *Rin-yé*, or *Rinten*, has the meaning of “turning the Wheel” — another expression for passing from birth to birth. The Wheel here is the great Circle of Illusion — the whirl of Karma.

-><sup>2</sup> Kaäi, kaäi to<-
->Naku mushi yori mo<-
->Nakanu hotaru ga<-
->Mi wo kogasu.<-
->Nanno ingwa dé<-
->Jitsu naki hito ni<-
->Shin wo akashité, —<-
->Aa kuyashi!<-

Lit.: “ ‘I-love-I-love ’-saying-cry-insects than, better

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Japanese folk poetry and Buddhist themes, specifically transitioning from the "Wheel of Karma" and firefly metaphors to the shared and divergent doctrines of Impermanence in Christian and Buddhist thought.</meta>

# BUDDHIST ALLUSIONS

If the previous examples seem like productions possible only for our psychological opposites, it is quite different with a group of folk songs reflecting the doctrine of Impermanence. Regarding the instability of all material things and the hollowness of all earthly pleasures, Christian and Buddhist thought are very much in agreement. The major difference between them appears only when we compare their teachings regarding spiritual matters—and especially regarding the nature of the Ego. But the Eastern doctrine that the Ego itself is an impermanent compound, and that the Self is not true Consciousness, rarely finds expression in these popular songs. For the common people, the Self exists: it is a real (though multiple) personality that passes from birth to birth. Only the educated Buddhist understands the deeper teaching that what we imagine to be the Self is a total illusion—a darkening veil woven by Karma—and that there is no Self except the Infinite Self, the eternal Absolute.

---

> The firefly never cries, yet its body scorches! Because of what Karma did I open my inmost mind to an insincere man?—ah! Regret! . . . It was previously believed that the firefly's light actually burned its own body.

<summary>This page discusses how Japanese folk songs reflect the Buddhist doctrine of impermanence, noting that while Buddhism and Christianity agree on the vanity of the material world, they differ significantly on the nature of the soul and the Ego.</summary>

<keywords>Impermanence, Ego, Self, Consciousness, Karma, Infinite Self, Absolute, folk songs, Buddhist thought, Christian thought, firefly metaphor</keywords>

# IN JAPANESE FOLK-SONG

In the following <term>dodoitsu</term>, one will find mostly thoughts or emotions that align with universal experience: —

->*Gathering clouds for the moon; — storm and rain for the flowers:*<-
->*Somehow this world of woe is never just as we like.<note>1</note>*<-

->*Almost as soon as they bloom, the scented flowers of the plum-*<-
->*tree*<-
->*By the wind of this world of change are scattered and blown*<-
->*away.*<-

->*Thinking that tomorrow remains, O frail cherry blossom of the heart?*<-
->*How do you know whether a tempest will not come this night?<note>2</note>*<-

-><note>1</note> Tsuki ni murakumo,<-
->Hana ni wa arashi:<-
->Tokaku uki-yo wa<-
->Mama naranu.<-

This song refers especially to unhappy love and contains the substance of two Buddhist proverbs: *Tsuki ni murakumo, hana ni kazé* (clouds gather around the moon; wind blows upon the flowers); and *Mama ni naranu wa uki-yo no narai* (to be disappointed is the rule in this miserable world). “<term>Uki-yo</term>” (this fleeting or unhappy world) is one of the most common Buddhist terms in use.

-><note>2</note> Asu ari to<-
->Omō kokoro no<-
->Ada-zakura:<-
->Yo wa ni arashi no<-
->Fukanu monokawa?<-

Literal translation: “Thinking that there is a tomorrow, O frail cherry blossom of the heart: is it certain that a storm will not blow in the middle of the night?”

*Shadow and shape alike melt and flow back into nothing:*
*He who knows this truth is the Daruma of snow.<note>1</note>*

*Like the moon of the fifteenth night, the heart is full until the age of fifteen:*
*Then the brightness fades, and the darkness arrives with love.<note>2</note>*

*All things change, we are told, in this world of change and sorrow;*
*But love’s way of promising never to change never changes.<note>3</note>*

---

-><note>1</note> Kagé mo katachi mo<-
->Kiyuréba moto no<-
->Midzu to satoru zo<-
->Yuki-Daruma.<-

Literal translation: “Shadow and shape as well, if they melt away, return to the original water—the Snow-Daruma understands this.” <term>Daruma</term> (<term>Dharma</term>), the twenty-eighth patriarch of the <term>Zen sect</term>, is said to have lost his legs by remaining in the posture of meditation for a long time; and many legless toy figures, which are balanced so that they always return to an upright position no matter how often they are placed upside-down, are named after him. The snowmen made by Japanese children have the same traditional form. The Japanese friend who helped me translate these verses tells me that a ghostly meaning is associated with the word “Kagé” <note>shadow</note> in the verse above; this would give a much deeper significance to the whole poem.

<note>2</note> According to the old calendar, there was always a full moon on the fifteenth of the month. The Buddhist allusion in the verse refers to <term>mayoi</term>, the illusion of passion, which is compared to a darkness hiding the Right Way.

-><note>3</note> Kawaru uki-yo ni<-
->Kawaranu mono wa<-

Literally: "Saying they are repeating prayers, the two facing the presence of the dead—small-pan cooking!" <term>Hotoké</term> means a dead person as well as a <term>Buddha</term>. (See my *Glimpses of Unfamiliar Japan*: "The Household Shrine"). <term>Konabé-daté</term> is an idiomatic expression signifying a lovers' private conversation. It is derived from the phrase, <term>Chin-chin kamo nabé</term> ("cooking a wild duck in a pan")—the idea suggested being the pleasure experienced by a romantic couple in eating out of the same dish. <term>Chin-chin</term>, an <term>onomatopoeia</term>, expresses the sound of the gravy boiling.

The interest attached to the following typical group of love songs will be found to depend chiefly on Buddhist allusions:

> *In the bed of the River of Souls, or in waiting alone at evening,*
> *The pain is no different at all: the pebble grows into a mountain.<sup>1</sup>*
>
> *Whoever wanders furthest after illusion on Love's dark pathway*
> *Is always the clearest-seeing,<sup>2</sup> not the simple or dull.*

illusion, is a state of spiritual darkness. The term “owner of my heart” is an attempted translation of the Japanese word *<term>nushi</term>*, signifying “master,” “owner”—often, also, “landlord”—and, in love matters, the lord or master of the inspired affection.

-><sup>1</sup> Sai-no-kawara to<-
->Nushi matsu yoi wa<-
->Koishi, koishi ga<-
->Yama to naru.<-

A more literal translation would be: “In the Sai-no-Kawara (‘Dry bed of the River of Souls’) and in the evening when waiting for the loved one, ‘*<term>Koishi, Koishi</term>*’ becomes a mountain.” There is a delicate pun here—a play on the word *<term>Koishi</term>*, which, as pronounced (though not as written), may mean either “a small stone” or “longing to see.” In the bed of the phantom river, Sai-no-Kawa, the ghosts of children are required to pile up little stones, the weight of which increases so much that it taxes their strength to the limit. There is also a reference here to a verse in the Buddhist *<term>wasan</term>* of *<term>Jizō</term>*, describing the children crying for their parents: “*Chichi koishi! haha koishi!*” (See *Glimpses of Unfamiliar Japan*, vol. i. pp. 59-61.)

<sup>2</sup> Clearest-sighted—that is, in worldly matters.

->*A word spoken too quickly can never be recalled:*<-
->*Therefore, the lover receives the prayer with the face of Emma.<sup>1</sup>*<-

->*My face shall be the face of Emma because of too many prayers.*<-

<note><sup>1</sup> The implication is that he has hastily promised more than he wishes to perform. Emma, or Yemma (Sanskrit: Yama), is the Lord of Hell and Judge of Souls; as depicted in Buddhist sculpture and painting, he is more than terrifying to look upon. There is an evident reference in this song to the Buddhist proverb: *Karu-toki no Jizō-gao; nasu-toki no Emma-gao* ("Borrowing-time, the face of Jizō; repaying-time, the face of Emma").</note>

<note><sup>2</sup> "Jigoku" is the Buddhist name for various hells (Sanskrit: *narakas*). The allusion here is to the proverb, *Funa-ita ichi-mai shita wa Jigoku*: "Under [the thickness of] a single boat-plank is hell,"—referring to the perils of the sea. This song is a satire on jealousy; and the boat spoken of is probably a roofed pleasure-boat, such as those used for excursions made to the sound of music.</note>

<note><sup>3</sup> *Tsuki-yo-garasu*, literally: "moon-night crows." Crows usually announce the dawn by their cawing; but sometimes on moonlight nights they caw at all hours from sunset to sun-</note>

### IN JAPANESE FOLK-SONG

> *This is my desire: To kill the crows of three thousand worlds,*
> *And then to rest in peace with the master of my heart!¹*

I have cited this last one only as a curiosity. For it has a strange history and is not what it seems—although the apparent motive was certainly suggested by a song like the one immediately preceding it. It is a song of loyalty and was composed by Kido of Chōshū, one of the leaders in that great movement which brought about the downfall of the Shogunate, the restoration of the Imperial power, the reconstruction of Japanese society, and the introduction and adoption of Western civilization. Kido, Saigō, and Ōkubo are rightly called the three heroes of the restoration. While preparing his plans at Kyoto, in company with his friend Saigō, Kido com-

There is a pun in the original; the expression *tsukenai*, “cannot tell [a lie],” might also be interpreted phonetically as “cannot strike [a bell].”</note>

<note>¹ San-zen sékai no
   Karasu wo koroshi
   Nushi to soi-né ga

<meta>This page continues the analysis of Kido Takayoshi’s song, explaining its hidden political metaphors regarding the Meiji Restoration, and begins a concluding reflection on the immense scale of the Buddhist concept of the universe.</meta>

<header>BUDDHIST ALLUSIONS</header>

...composed and sang this song as an expression of his real feelings. By the phrase, "ravens of the three thousand worlds," he designated the Tokugawa partisans; by the word <term>nushi</term> (lord, or heart’s-master) he meant the Emperor; and by the term <term>soiné</term> (resting together) he referred to the hoped-for state of direct responsibility to the Throne, without further intervention from the <term>Shōgun</term> and <term>daimyō</term>. It was not the first example in Japanese history of the use of a popular song as a medium for the expression of opinions which, if stated in plainer language, would have invited assassination.

While I was writing the previous note upon Kido’s song, the Buddhist phrase, <term>San-zen sékai</term> (which occurs twice in this collection, as the reader will have noticed), suggested a few reflections with which this paper may suitably conclude. I remember that when I first attempted, years ago, to learn the outlines of Buddhist philosophy, one fact that particularly impressed me was the vastness of the Buddhist concept of the universe. Buddhism, as I understood it, had not presented itself to

humanity as a saving creed for one inhabited world, but as the religion of “innumerable hundreds of thousands of myriads of *kôtis*<gloss>1</gloss> of worlds.” And the modern scientific revelation of stellar evolution and dissolution then seemed to me, and still seems, like a prodigious confirmation of certain Buddhist theories of cosmic law.

The scientist today cannot ignore the enormous suggestions of the new story that the heavens are telling. He finds himself compelled to regard the development of what we call the mind as a general phase or incident in the ripening of planetary life throughout the universe. He is obliged to consider the relation of our own petty sphere to the great swarming of suns and systems as no more than the relation of a single <term>noctiluca</term> to the phosphorescence of a sea. By its creed, the Oriental intellect has been better prepared than the Occidental to accept this tremendous revelation, not as a wisdom that increases sorrow, but as a wisdom to quicken faith. And I cannot help but think that out of the certain future union of Western knowledge

### IX

# NIRVANA

## **A STUDY IN SYNTHETIC BUDDHISM**

### I

> “It is not possible, O Subhûti, for this treatise of the Law to be heard by beings of little faith—by those who believe in Self, in beings, in living beings, and in persons.” — *The Diamond-Cutter*.

THERE still widely prevails in Europe and America the idea that Nirvana signifies, to Buddhist minds, nothing more nor less than absolute nothingness—complete annihilation. This idea is erroneous. But it is erroneous only because it contains half of a truth. This half of a truth has no value, interest, or even intelligibility unless joined with the other half. And regarding that other half, no suspicion yet exists in the average Western mind.

Nirvana, indeed, signifies an extinction. But if by this extinction of individual being we understand soul-death, our conception of Nir-

<meta>Catchword or word split at end of page: Nir-</meta>

<summary>The author challenges the Western misconception that Nirvana represents total annihilation, explaining that such a view only captures half of the concept's true meaning. He argues that without understanding the other half of this truth, the term remains misunderstood in the West.</summary>

<keywords>Nirvana, Buddhism, annihilation, Western thought, The Diamond-Cutter, Subhûti, extinction of self</keywords>

--- Page 224 ---
<meta>This page continues the definition of Nirvana, completing the word started on the previous page.</meta>

<page-num>212</page-num>

->NIRVANA<-

-vana is incorrect. Or if we take Nirvana to mean the kind of reabsorption of the finite into the infinite predicted by Indian pantheism, once again, our idea is foreign to Buddhism.

Nevertheless, if we state that Nirvana means the extinction of individual sensation, emotion, and thought—the final disintegration of conscious personality and the annihilation of everything that can be included under the term “I”—then we correctly express one side of Buddhist teaching.

The apparent contradiction of the preceding statements is due only to our Western notion of Self. To us, Self signifies feelings, ideas, memory, and volition; it can scarcely occur to any person unfamiliar with German idealism to even imagine that consciousness might not be the Self. The Buddhist, on the contrary, declares everything we call Self to be false. He defines the Ego as a mere temporary aggregate of sensations, impulses, and ideas, created by the physical and mental experiences of the race—all related to the perishable body, and all doomed to dissolve with it. What seems most indubitable to Western reasoning

<summary>The author clarifies that while Nirvana involves the extinction of the individual personality and the "I," it is distinct from the reabsorption theories of Indian pantheism. He argues that Westerners struggle to understand this because they incorrectly equate consciousness with a permanent Self, whereas Buddhism views the Ego as a temporary collection of dissolving sensations and thoughts.</summary>

<keywords>Nirvana, Buddhism, Indian pantheism, Self, Ego, Western philosophy, German idealism, consciousness, individual personality, annihilation</keywords>

<page-num>213</page-num>

<header>NIRVANA</header>

of realities, Buddhist reasoning pronounces the greatest of all illusions, and even the source of all sorrow and sin. “ *The mind, the thoughts, and all the senses are subject to the law of life and death. With knowledge of Self and the laws of birth and death, there is no grasping and no sense-perception. Knowing one’s self and knowing how the senses act, there is no room for the idea of ‘I,’ or the basis for forming it. The thought of ‘Self’ gives rise to all sorrows—binding the world as if with chains; but having found there is no ‘I’ that can be bound, then all these bonds are broken.* ” 1

The above text suggests very clearly that consciousness is not the Real Self, and that the mind dies with the body. Any reader unfamiliar with Buddhist thought may well ask, “What, then, is the meaning of the doctrine of Karma, the doctrine of moral progression, the doctrine of the consequences of actions?” Indeed, to try to study—using only the ontological ideas of the West—even such translations of the Buddhist Sutras as those given in the “Sacred Books of the East,” is to be

confronted on every page by seemingly hopeless riddles and contradictions. We find a doctrine of rebirth, yet the existence of a soul is denied. We are told that the misfortunes of this life are punishments for faults committed in a previous life, yet personal transmigration does not take place. We find the statement that beings are re-individualized, yet both individuality and personality are called illusions. I doubt whether anyone who is not acquainted with the deeper forms of Buddhist belief could possibly understand the following extracts which I have selected from the first volume of *The Questions of King Milinda*:

The King said, “Nagasena, is there anyone who, after death, is not re-individualized?” Nagasena answered, “A sinful being is re-individualized; a sinless one is not.” (p. 50.)

“Is there, Nagasena, such a thing as the soul?”
“There is no such thing as a soul.” (pp. 86–89.)
<note>The same statement is repeated in a later chapter (p. 111), with a qualification: “*In the highest sense, O King, there is no such thing.*”</note>

“Is there any being, Nagasena, who transmigrates from this body to another?” “No, there is not.” (p. 112.)

> "Where there is no transmigration, Nagasena, can there be rebirth?" "Yes: there can."

> "Does he, Nagasena, who is about to be reborn, know that he will be reborn?" "Yes: he knows it, O King." (p. 113.)

Naturally, the Western reader may ask, "How can there be re-individualization without a soul? How can there be rebirth without transmigration? How can there be personal foreknowledge of rebirth without personality?" But the answers to such questions will not be found in the work cited.

It would be wrong to suppose that the citations provided offer any exceptional difficulty. Regarding the doctrine of the annihilation of Self, the testimony of nearly all those Buddhist texts now accessible to English readers is overwhelming. Perhaps the *Sutra of the Great Decease* provides the most remarkable evidence contained in the "Sacred Books of the East." In its account of the Eight Stages of Deliverance leading to Nirvana, it explicitly describes what we would be justified in calling, from our Western point of view, the process of absolute annihilation. We are told that in the first of these eight stages the Bud-

Buddhist seeker after truth still retains the ideas of form—subjective and objective. In the second stage, he loses the subjective idea of form and views forms as external phenomena only. In the third stage, the sense of an approaching perception of a larger truth comes to him. In the fourth stage, he passes beyond all ideas of form, ideas of resistance, and ideas of distinction; and there remains to him only the idea of infinite space. In the fifth stage, the idea of infinite space vanishes, and the thought comes: *It is all infinite reason.* <note>Here is the ultimate limit, many might suppose, of pantheistic idealism; but it is only the halfway resting place on the path which the Buddhist thinker must pursue.</note> In the sixth stage, the thought comes, “*Nothing at all exists.*” In the seventh stage, the idea of nothingness itself vanishes. In the eighth stage, all sensations and ideas cease to exist. And *after* this comes Nirvana.

For the reader who has made any serious attempt to obtain a general idea of Buddhism, such citations are hardly necessary; because the fundamental doctrine of the chain of cause and effect contains the same denial of the reality of the Self and suggests the same puzzles. Evidently, without a preliminary solution to the riddles offered by such texts, any effort to learn the meaning of Nirvana is hopeless. Before being able to comprehend the true meaning of those sutras now made familiar to English readers by translation, it is necessary to understand that the common Western ideas of God and Soul, of matter, and of spirit, have no existence in Buddhist philosophy; their places are occupied by concepts having no real counterparts in Western religious thought. Above all, it is necessary that the reader should expel from his mind the theological idea of the Soul. The texts already quoted should have made it clear that in Buddhist philosophy there is no personal transmigration and no individual, permanent Soul.

## II

> “O Bhagavat, the idea of a self is no idea; and the idea of a being, or a living person, or a person, is no idea. And why? Because the blessed Buddhas are freed from all ideas.” — *The Diamond-Cutter*.

And now let us try to understand what it is that dies, and what it is that is reborn—what it is that commits faults and what it is that suffers penalties—what passes from states of woe to states of bliss—what enters into Nirvana after the destruction of self-consciousness—what survives “extinction” and has power to return out of Nirvana—and what experiences the Four Infinite Feelings after all finite feeling has been annihilated.

It is not the sentient and conscious Self that enters Nirvana. The Ego is only a temporary aggregate of countless illusions, a phantom-shell, a bubble sure to break. It is a creation of Karma—or rather, as a Buddhist friend insists, it is Karma. To comprehend the statement fully, the reader should know that, in this Eastern philosophy, acts and thoughts are forces integrating themselves into material and mental phenomena,

<summary>This page explores the Buddhist paradox of what experiences rebirth and Nirvana if the individual "Self" does not exist. It defines the Ego not as a permanent soul, but as a temporary creation of Karma composed of illusions.</summary>

<keywords>Bhagavat, Nirvana, Buddhas, The Diamond-Cutter, self-consciousness, Four Infinite Feelings, Ego, Karma, Eastern philosophy, rebirth, illusion</keywords>

—into what we call objective and subjective appearances. The very earth we tread upon—the mountains and forests, the rivers and seas, the world and its moon, the visible universe in short—*is the integration of acts* and *thoughts*; it is Karma or, at least, Being conditioned by Karma.¹

The Karma-Ego we call the Self is mind and is body; both perpetually decay, and both are perpetually renewed. From the unknown beginning, this dual phenomenon—both objective and subjective—has been alternately dissolved and integrated: each integration is a birth, and each dissolution is a death. There is no other birth or death but the birth and death of Karma in some form or condition. But at each rebirth, the reintegration is never the reintegration of the identical phenomenon, but of another to which it gives rise—just as growth begets growth, or as motion produces motion. So the phantom-self changes not only in form and condition, but even in actual personality with every re-embodiment. There is one Reality; but there is no permanent individual and no constant personality: there is only a phantom-self, and phantom succeeds phantom, like wave following wave, over the ghostly Sea of Birth and Death. And just as the storming of a sea is a motion of waves, not of the water itself moving from place to place—even as it is the form of the wave only, not the water itself, that travels—so in the passing of lives there is only the rising and the

<summary>This page defines the "Self" as a constantly changing "Karma-Ego" that lacks a permanent personality or individual soul. It uses the metaphor of ocean waves to illustrate how Karma moves through successive lives without the transfer of a constant, identical entity.</summary>
<keywords>Karma-Ego, phantom-self, re-embodiment, Sea of Birth and Death, impermanence, Buddhist philosophy, rebirth</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of reality and the self, specifically focusing on the concept of Nirvana as the dissolution of mental and material forms to reveal the Absolute Reality.</meta>

<page-num>222</page-num>
<header>NIRVANA</header>

vanishing of forms—forms mental, forms material. The fathomless Reality does not pass. “All forms,” it is written in the *Kongō-hannya-haramitsu-Kyō*,¹ “are unreal: he who rises above all forms is the Buddha.” But what can remain to rise above all forms after the total disintegration of the body and the final dissolution of the mind?

Unconsciously dwelling behind the false consciousness of imperfect man—beyond sensation, perception, and thought—wrapped in the envelope of what we call the soul (which in truth is only a thickly woven veil of illusion), is the eternal and divine, the Absolute Reality: not a soul, not a personality, but the All-Self without selfishness—the *Muga no Taiga*—the Buddha enwombed in Karma. Within every phantom-self dwells this divine nature; yet the innumerable are but one. Within every incarnate creature sleeps the Infinite Intelligence, unevolved, hidden, unfelt, unknown—yet destined from all eternity to awaken at last, to tear away the ghostly web of the sensuous mind, to break forever its chrysalis of flesh, and pass to the supreme

I will teach them the holy Way; — I will make them abandon their foolish thoughts, and cause them to see that the vast and deep intelligence which dwells within them is no different from the wisdom of the Buddha himself.”

Here we may pause to consider the correspondence between these fundamental Buddhist theories and the concepts of Western science. It will be evident that the Buddhist denial of the reality of the apparent world is not a denial of the reality of phenomena as phenomena, nor a denial of the forces producing phenomena objectively or subjectively. For the denial of Karma as Karma would involve the denial of the entire Buddhist system. The true declaration is, that what we perceive is never reality in itself, and that even the Ego that perceives is an unstable

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of reality and the self, specifically how the perceived Ego and the external world are seen as unstable aggregates compared to scientific and Buddhist concepts of phenomena.</meta>

<page-num>224</page-num>
<header>NIRVANA</header>

plexus of aggregates of feelings which are themselves unstable and in the nature of illusions. This position is scientifically strong—perhaps impregnable. Of substance in itself we certainly know nothing: we are conscious of the universe only as a vast play of forces; and, even while we discern the general relative meaning of laws expressed in the action of those forces, all that which is *Non-Ego* is revealed to us merely through the vibrations of a nervous structure that is never exactly the same in any two human beings. Yet through such varying and imperfect perception we are sufficiently assured of the impermanence of all forms—of all aggregates, whether objective or subjective.

The test of reality is persistence; and the Buddhist, finding in the visible universe only a perpetual flux of phenomena, declares the material aggregate unreal because it is non-persistent—as unreal, at least, as a bubble, a cloud, or a mirage. Again, relation is the universal form of thought; but since relation is impermanent, how can thought be persistent? . . . Judged from these points of view, Buddhist doctrine is not Anti-Realism, but a veritable

<meta>This page continues the exploration of the illusory nature of the self and the material world, linking Buddhist philosophy with the scientific concept of Transfigured Realism as articulated by Herbert Spencer.</meta>

<page-num>225</page-num>
<header>NIRVANA</header>

Transfigured Realism, finding its proper expression in the exact words of Herbert Spencer:
> “Every feeling and thought being only transitory; — an entire life made up of such feelings and thoughts being also only transitory; — even more, the objects amid which life is passed, though less transitory, being individually in the process of losing their identities, whether quickly or slowly, — *we learn that the one thing permanent is the Unknowable Reality hidden under all these changing shapes*.”

Similarly, the teaching of Buddhism—that what we call the Self is an impermanent aggregate, a sensory illusion—will prove, if analyzed patiently, to be something that almost no serious thinker can deny. The mind, as known to the scientific psychologist, is composed of feelings and the relationships between feelings; and feelings are composed of units of simple sensation which correspond physiologically with minute nervous shocks. All the sense organs are fundamentally alike, being evolutionary modifications of the same morphological elements; — and all the senses are modifications of touch. Or, to use the simplest possible language, the organs of sense — sight,

<summary>This page compares Buddhist teachings on the impermanence of the self to Herbert Spencer’s "Transfigured Realism," asserting that both recognize the transitory nature of thoughts and objects. It further explains the scientific view that the mind and senses are evolutionary modifications of simple sensations and the sense of touch.</summary>

<keywords>Transfigured Realism, Herbert Spencer, Unknowable Reality, Buddhism, Self, Mind, psychology, sensation, nervous shocks, evolution, morphology, touch</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the scientific and philosophical exploration of the senses, specifically the theory that all sensory perception—and even the brain itself—evolved from the sense of touch, aligning these biological findings with the Buddhist concept of the impermanent self.</meta>

<page-num>226</page-num>
<header>NIRVANA</header>

smell, taste, even hearing — have all likewise developed from the skin! Even the human brain itself, according to the modern evidence of <term>histology</term> and <term>embryology</term>, "is, at its very beginning, merely an inward folding of the <term>epidermal layer</term>;" and thought, physiologically and evolutionarily, is thus a modification of touch. Certain vibrations, acting through the visual apparatus, cause those motions within the brain that are followed by the sensations of light and color; other vibrations, acting upon the auditory mechanism, give rise to the sensation of sound; and other vibrations, causing changes in specialized tissue, produce sensations of taste, smell, and touch. All our knowledge is derived and developed, directly or indirectly, from physical sensation — from touch. Of course, this is not an ultimate explanation, because no one can tell us what actually feels the touch. "Everything physical," as Schopenhauer rightly said, "is at the same time metaphysical." But science fully justifies the Buddhist position that what we call the Self is a bundle of sensations, emotions, sentiments, ideas, and memories, all relating to the physical experiences of the human race and the individual,

<header>NIRVANA</header>

and that our wish for immortality is a wish for the eternity of this merely sensory and selfish consciousness. And science even supports the Buddhist denial of the permanence of the sensory <term>Ego</term>. “Psychology,” says Wundt, “proves that not only our sense-perceptions, but the memory images that renew them, depend for their origin upon the functioning of the organs of sense and movement. . . . A continuation of this sensory consciousness must appear to it irreconcilable with the facts of its experience. And surely we may well doubt whether such a continuation is an ethical requirement; more, whether the fulfillment of the wish for it, if possible, would not be an intolerable destiny.”

### III

> “O Subhûti, if I had had an idea of a being, of a living being, or of a person, I should also have had an idea of malevolence. . . . A gift should not be given by anyone who believes in form, sound, smell, taste, or anything that can be touched.” — *The Diamond-Cutter*.

The doctrine of the impermanence of the conscious <term>Ego</term> is not only the most remarkable in Buddhist philosophy: it is also,

<summary>This page discusses how modern psychology supports the Buddhist rejection of a permanent self, suggesting that sensory consciousness is tied to physical organs and that its eternal continuation might be an undesirable fate. <meta>This page continues the discussion on the doctrine of the impermanence of the conscious Ego, shifting from psychological evidence to its moral and ethical implications for society.</meta>

<page-num>228</page-num>
->*NIRVANA*<-

morally, one of the most important. Perhaps the ethical value of this teaching has never yet been fairly estimated by any Western thinker. How much of human unhappiness has been caused, directly and indirectly, by opposite beliefs—by the delusion of stability—by the delusion that distinctions of character, condition, class, and creed are settled by immutable law—and the delusion of a changeless, immortal, sentient soul, destined, by divine caprice, to eternities of bliss or eternities of fire! Doubtless the ideas of a deity moved by everlasting hate—of the soul as a permanent, changeless entity destined to changeless states—of sin as unatonable and of penalty as never-ending—were not without value in former savage stages of social development. But in the course of our future evolution, they must be completely discarded; and it may be hoped that the contact of Western with Oriental thought will have, as one positive result, the acceleration of their decline. While even the feelings which they have developed linger with us, there can be no true spirit of tolerance, no sense of human brotherhood, and no awakening of universal love.

<summary>This page argues that the Buddhist doctrine of impermanence has profound ethical value, contrasting it with Western "delusions" regarding a permanent soul and eternal punishment. The author suggests that moving past these rigid beliefs will lead to greater human brotherhood and universal love.</summary>
<keywords>Nirvana, ethical value, Western thought, delusion of stability, immortal soul, divine caprice, evolution, Oriental thought, tolerance, human brotherhood, universal love</keywords>

--- Page 241 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the moral implications of Buddhist doctrine, contrasting its inherent tolerance with the limitations of Western concepts of permanent personality.</meta>

<language>English</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>229</page-num>
->NIRVANA<-

Buddhism, on the other hand, recognizing no permanency, no finite stabilities, no distinctions of character or class or race, except as passing phenomena—indeed, no difference even between gods and men—has been essentially the religion of tolerance. Demon and angel are only varying manifestations of the same *Karma*; hell and heaven are mere temporary halting-places on the journey to eternal peace. For all beings there is only one law, immutable and divine: the law by which the lowest must rise to the place of the highest, the law by which the worst must become the best, the law by which the vilest must become a Buddha. In such a system, there is no room for prejudice and hatred. Ignorance alone is the source of wrong and pain; and all ignorance must finally be dissipated in infinite light through the decomposition of *Self*.

Certainly while we still try to cling to the old theories of permanent personality, and of a single incarnation only for each individual, we can find no moral meaning in the universe as it exists. Modern knowledge can discover

no justice in the cosmic process; the most it can offer us by way of ethical encouragement is that the unknowable forces are not forces of pure malevolence. “Neither moral nor immoral,” to quote Huxley, “but simply amoral.” Evolutionary science cannot be made to agree with the notion of a permanent personality; and if we accept its teaching of mental growth and inheritance, we must also accept its teaching of individual dissolution and of the cosmos as inexplicable. It assures us, indeed, that the higher faculties of man have been developed through struggle and pain, and will long continue to be so developed; but it also assures us that evolution is inevitably followed by dissolution—that the highest point of development is also the point from which regression begins. And if we are each and all mere perishable forms of being—doomed to pass away like plants and trees—what comfort can we find in the assurance that we are suffering for the benefit of the future? How can it concern us whether humanity becomes more or less happy in another myriad of ages, if there remains nothing for us but to live and die

in comparative misery? Or, to repeat the irony of Huxley, “what compensation does the Eohippus get for his sorrows in the fact that, some millions of years afterwards, one of his descendants wins the Derby?”

But the cosmic process may take on quite another aspect if we can persuade ourselves, like the Buddhist, that all being is Unity—that personality is only a delusion hiding reality—that all distinctions of “I” and “you” are ghostly films spun out of perishable sensation—that even Time and Place, as revealed to our petty senses, are phantasms—that the past, the present, and the future are truly One. Suppose the winner of the Derby were quite able to remember having been the Eohippus? Suppose a being, once a man, were able to look back through all the veils of death and birth, through all the cycles of evolution, even to the moment of the first faint growth of sentience out of non-sentience—able to remember, like the Buddha of the Jatakas, all the experiences of his myriad incarnations, and to relate them like fairy tales for the sake of another Ananda?

We have seen that it is not the Self but the Non-Self—the one reality underlying all phenomena—which passes from form to form. The striving for Nirvana is a perpetual struggle between false and true, light and darkness, the sensual and the supersensual; and the ultimate victory can be gained only by the total breaking down of the mental and the physical individuality. Not one conquest of self can suffice: millions of selves must be overcome. For the false Ego is composed of countless ages and possesses a vitality that lasts beyond universes. At each breaking and shedding of the chrysalis, a new chrysalis appears—perhaps thinner, perhaps more transparent, but woven of similar sensory material—a mental and physical texture spun by Karma from the inherited illusions, passions, desires, pains, and pleasures of countless lives. But what is it that feels—the phantom or the reality?

All phenomena of *self-consciousness* belong to the false self—but only in the same way a physiologist might say that sensation is a product of the sensory apparatus, which would not explain the nature of sensation itself. No more in Buddhism than in physiological psychology is there any real

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of the "Self" in Buddhism, specifically how the "false self" acts as a distorting medium for the Absolute, leading to the dream-like experience of sentience and suffering.</meta>

teaching of *two* feeling entities. In Buddhism, the only entity is the Absolute; and to that entity, the false self stands in the relationship of a medium through which right perception is deflected and distorted—in which and because of which sentience and impulse become possible. The unconditioned Absolute is above all relations: it has nothing of what we call pain or pleasure; it knows no difference between “I” and “you”—no distinction of place or time. But while conditioned by the illusion of personality, it is aware of pain or pleasure, just as a dreamer perceives unrealities without being conscious of their unreality. Pleasures and pains, and all the feelings relating to self-consciousness, are hallucinations. The false self exists only as a state of sleep exists; and sentience and desire, and all the sorrows and passions of being, exist only as illusions of that sleep.

But here we reach a point at which science and Buddhism diverge. Modern psychology recognizes no feelings that were not evolutionarily developed through the experiences of the human race and the individual; but Buddhism asserts the existence of feelings which are immortal and

It may be said, in Western religious terms, that throughout the greater part of this vast pilgrimage, and in all the zones of desire, the temptations increase according to the spiritual strength of resistance. With every successive ascent there is a further expansion of the possibilities of enjoyment, an increase of power, and a heightening of sensation. Immense is the reward of self-conquest; but whoever strives for that reward strives after emptiness. One must not desire heaven as a state of pleasure; it has been written, *Erroneous thoughts regarding the joys of heaven are still entwined by the tight cords of lust.* One must not wish to become a god or an angel. “Whatever brother, O <term>Bhikkus</term>,” — the Teacher said — “may have adopted the religious life thinking to himself, ‘*By this morality I shall become an angel*,’ his mind does not lean toward zeal, perseverance, or exertion.” Perhaps the most vivid exposition of the duty of the winner of happiness is that given in the <term>Sutra of the Great King of Glory</term>. Also, there are many varieties of special doctrine held by the different sects and sub-sects—so that, to make any satisfactory outline of Buddhist ontology, it is necessary to form a synthesis of the more important and non-conflicting tenets among these many beliefs. I need hardly say that popular Buddhism does not include concepts such as those we have been examining. The people hold to the simpler creed of a true transmigration of souls. The people understand <term>*Karma*</term> only as the law that determines the punishment or reward for faults committed in previous lives. The people do not trouble themselves about <term>*Nehan*</term> or <term>*Nirvana*</term>¹; but they think much about heaven (<term>*Gokuraku*</term>), which the members of many sects believe can be attained immediately after this life by the spirits of the good. <meta>This page continues the discussion on the distinctions between various Buddhist doctrines, specifically moving from popular concepts of heaven to the higher teachings regarding the attainment of Nirvana and the classifications of existence.</meta>

Followers of the greatest and wealthiest of the modern sects—the *Shinshū*—hold that, by invoking Amida, a righteous person can pass immediately after death to the great Paradise of the West—the Paradise of the Lotus-Flower-Birth. I am not considering popular beliefs in this brief study, nor doctrines unique to only one sect.

However, there are many differences in the higher teachings regarding the attainment of Nirvana. Some authorities hold that supreme happiness can be achieved, or at least glimpsed, even on this earth. Others declare that the present world is too corrupt to allow for a perfect life, and that only by earning the privilege of rebirth into a better world through good deeds can men hope for the opportunity to practice the holiness that leads to the highest bliss. The latter opinion, which assumes superior conditions of existence in other worlds, better expresses the general thought of contemporary Buddhism in Japan.

The conditions of human and animal existence belong to what are termed the Worlds of Desire (*Yoku-Kai*), which consist of four in

Below these are the states of torment or hells (*Jigoku*), about which many curious things are written; but neither the Yoku-Kai nor the Jigoku need be considered in relation to the purpose of this short essay. We are only concerned with the course of spiritual progress from the world of men up to Nirvana—assuming, with modern Buddhism, that the pilgrimage through death and birth must continue, at least for the majority of mankind, even after reaching the highest conditions possible on this globe. The path rises from earthly conditions to other and superior worlds—passing first through the Six Heavens of Desire (*Yoku-Ten*); then through the Seventeen Heavens of Form (*Shiki-Kai*); and lastly through the Four Heavens of Formlessness (*Mushiki-Kai*), beyond which lies Nirvana.

&emsp;&emsp;The requirements of physical life—the need for food, rest, and sexual relations—continue to be felt in the Heavens of Desire, which would seem to be higher physical worlds rather than what we commonly understand by the expression "heavens." <header>NIRVANA</header>

might be supposed to exist on planets more favored than our own—in larger spheres warmed by a more genial sun. And some Buddhist texts actually place them in remote constellations, declaring that the Path leads from star to star, from galaxy to galaxy, from universe to universe, up to the Limit of Existence.<sup>1</sup>

In the first of the heavens of this zone, called the Heaven of the Four Kings (*Shi-Tennō-Ten*), life lasts five times longer than life on this earth in terms of years, and each year there is equal to fifty terrestrial years. But its inhabitants eat and drink, and marry and give in marriage, much like the fashion of mankind. In the succeeding heaven (*Sanjiu-san-Ten*), the duration of life is doubled, while all other conditions are correspondingly improved; and the grosser forms

---

<note><sup>1</sup> This astronomical placement of higher states of being, or of other “Buddha-fields,” might provoke a smile; however, it suggests undeniable possibilities. There is nothing absurd about supposing that the potential for life, growth, and development actually passes—through nebular diffusion and concentration—from dying systems to new systems. Indeed, given our current state of knowledge, it is hardly possible for a rational mind not to suppose this.</note>

<meta>This page continues the description of the various Buddhist heavens, specifically moving through the increasingly refined levels of the Heavens of Desire (Yoku-Kai).</meta>

<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>245</page-num>

of passion disappear. The union of the sexes continues, but in a way strangely similar to what a certain Father of the Christian Church hoped might be possible—a simple embrace resulting in the birth of a new being. In the third heaven (called <term>*Emma-Ten*</term>), where lifespan is again doubled, the slightest touch can create life. In the fourth, or Heaven of Contentment (<term>*Tochita-Ten*</term>), lifespan increases even further. In the fifth, or Heaven of the Transmutation of Pleasure (<term>*Keraku-Ten*</term>), strange new powers are acquired. Subjective pleasures are changed at will into objective pleasures; thoughts as well as wishes become creative forces; and even the act of looking can cause conception and birth. In the sixth heaven (<term>*Také-jizai-Ten*</term>), the powers gained in the fifth heaven are developed further; and the subjective pleasures transformed into objective ones can be presented to or shared with others, just like material gifts. But the look of a moment—a single glance of the eye—can generate a new <term>Karma</term>.

The <term>Yoku-Kai</term> are all heavens of sensuous life—heavens that might fulfill the dreams of artists, lovers, and poets. But

There are three of these, each higher than the last, and they are named the Heaven of Sanctity, the Heaven of Higher Sanctity, and the Heaven of Great Sanctity. After these come the heavens called the Heavens of Luminous Observation of Non-Existence and of Calm Meditation upon Non-Existence (<term>*Mūjin-mushi-shōryo*</term>). These are also three; and their names, in their order, signify *Lesser Light*, *Light Unfathomable*, and *Light Making Sound*, or *Light-Sonorous*. Here, the highest degree of supersensual joy possible within temporary conditions is reached. Above these are the states named <term>*Riki-shōryo*</term>, or the Heavens of the Meditation of the Abandonment of Joy. The names of these states, in their ascending order, are *Lesser Purity*, *Purity Unfathomable*, and *Purity Supreme*. In these states, neither joy nor pain, nor any kind of forceful feeling exists; there is

<meta>This page continues the description of the Formless Realm (Mushiki-Kai), detailing the final stages of mental dissolution before the attainment of Nirvana.</meta>

<page-num>248</page-num>

state of the Mushiki-Kai, this idea of space vanishes; and its place is filled by the Idea of Infinite Reason. But this idea of reason is anthropomorphic: it is an illusion; and it fades out in the third state of the Mushiki-Kai, which is called the "State-of-Having-Nothing-to-Hold-Onto," or *Mū-sho-ū-shō-jō*. Here is only the Idea of Infinite Nothingness. But even this condition has been reached by the aid of the action of the personal mind. This action ceases: then the fourth state of the Mushiki-Kai is reached—the *Hisō-hihisō-shō*, or the state of "neither-namelessness-nor-not-namelessness." Something of personal mentality continues to float vaguely here—the very uttermost expiring vibration of Karma, the last vanishing haze of being. It melts; and the immeasurable revelation comes. The dreaming Buddha, freed from the last ghostly bond of Self, rises at once into the "infinite bliss" of Nirvana.<note>The expression "infinite bliss" as synonymous with Nirvana is taken from the *Questions of King Milinda*.</note>

Some beings pass to Nirvana immediately after their present life; some after a single new birth; some after two or three births; while many rise directly from this world into one of the Supersensuous Heavens. All such are called *Chō*,—the Leapers,—of whom the highest class reaches Nirvana immediately after their death as men or women. There are two great divisions of *Chō*: the *Fu-Kwan*, or Never-Returning-Ones,<note>In the *Sutra of the Great Decease*, we find an instance of a woman reaching this condition: "Sister Nanda, O Ananda, by the destruction of the five bonds that bind people to this world, has become an inhabitant of the highest heaven—to pass entirely away there, and from there never to return."</note> and the *Kwan*, Returning-Ones, or *revenants*. Sometimes the return may be in the nature of a prolonged retrogression; and, according to a Buddhist legend of the origin of the world, the first men were beings who had fallen from the *Kwō-on-Ten*, or Heaven of Sonorous Light. --- Page 262 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from the previous text regarding the non-linear nature of spiritual progression toward Nirvana, describing it as a rhythmical process of undulations rather than a straight line.</meta>

<page-num>250</page-num>
<header>NIRVANA</header>

(except in very rare cases) as an advance in straight lines, but as an advance by undulations—a psychical rhythm of motion. This is exemplified by the curious Buddhist classification of the different short courses by which the Kwan, or *revenants*, may hope to reach Nirvana. These short courses are divided into Even and Uneven; the former includes an equal number of heavenly and earthly rebirths, while in the latter class, the heavenly and earthly intermediate rebirths are not equal in number. There are four kinds of these intermediate stages. A Japanese friend has drawn for me the accompanying diagrams, which explain the subject clearly.

This may be called fantastic, but it harmonizes with the truth that all progress is necessarily rhythmical.

Though all beings do not pass through every stage of the great journey, all beings who attain to the highest enlightenment by any course whatever acquire certain faculties not belonging to particular conditions of birth, but only to particular conditions of psychical development. These are the *Roku-Jindzū*.

(*Abhidjñâ*), or Six Supernatural Powers:<note>Different Buddhist systems give different lists of these mysterious powers, of which the Chinese names literally mean: (1) Calm-Meditation-outward-pouring-no-obstacle-wisdom; (2) Heaven-Eye-no-obstacle-wisdom; (3) Heaven-Ear-no-obstacle-wisdom; (4) Other-minds-no-obstacle-wisdom; (5) Former-States-no-obstacle-wisdom; (6) Leak-Extinction-no-obstacle-wisdom.</note> —

<meta>This page continues the explanation of the Six Supernatural Powers, specifically detailing how the memory of past and future births expands as a practitioner progresses through the stages of holiness toward Buddhahood.</meta>

<page-num>254</page-num>
<header>NIRVANA</header>

In the first state of holiness, for example, comes the memory of a certain number of former births, together with the capacity to foresee a corresponding number of future births; in the next higher state, the number of births remembered increases; and in the state of <term>Bosatsu</term>, all former births are visible to memory. But the <term>Buddha</term> sees not only all of his own former births, but likewise all births that ever have been or can be—and all the thoughts and acts, past, present, or future, of all past, present, or future beings. . . . 

Now these dreams of supernatural power merit attention because of the ethical teaching in regard to them—the same teaching which is woven through every Buddhist hypothesis, whether rational or inconceivable—the teaching of self-abnegation. The <term>Supernatural Powers</term> must never be used for personal pleasure, but only for the highest benefit—the propagation of doctrine and the saving of others. Any exercise of them for lesser ends might result in their loss, and would certainly signify retrogression on the path.<note>Beings who have reached the state of <term>Engaku</term> or of <term>Bosatsu</term> are not thought to be capable of retrogression or of any serious error; but it is different in lower spiritual states.</note>

<summary>This page describes how the memory of past and future lives grows more extensive as one advances toward Buddhahood. It emphasizes that supernatural powers must only be used for the selfless purpose of helping others and spreading Buddhist teachings.</summary>
<keywords>Nirvana, Bosatsu, Buddha, Engaku, retrogression, self-abnegation, Supernatural Powers, past lives, spiritual stages, omniscience</keywords>

--- Page 267 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the proper use of supernatural powers and transitions into a definition of <term>Nirvana</term> as a state of spiritual emancipation rather than mere extinction.</meta>

<page-num>255</page-num>
<header>NIRVANA</header>

To show them for the purpose of exciting admiration or wonder would be to trifle wickedly with what is divine; and the Teacher himself is recorded as having once severely rebuked a needless display of them by a disciple.<sup>1</sup>

This giving up not only of one life, but of countless lives—not only of one world, but of innumerable worlds—not only of natural but also of supernatural pleasures—not only of selfhood but of godhood—is certainly not for the miserable privilege of ceasing to exist, but for a privilege infinitely outweighing all that even paradise can give. <term>Nirvana</term> is not a cessation, but an emancipation. It means only the passing of <term>conditioned being</term> into unconditioned being—the fading of all mental and physical phantoms into the light of <term>Formless Omnipotence</term> and <term>Omniscience</term>. But the <term>Buddhist hypothesis</term> suggests the persistence of that which was once able to remember all births and states of limited being—the persistence of the identity of the <term>Buddhas</term> even

---

<sup>1</sup> <note>See a curious legend in the <term>Vinaya texts</term>—*<term>Kullavagga</term>*, v. 8, 2.</note>

<summary>This page explains that displaying supernatural powers for vanity is a misuse of the divine and defines Nirvana as an emancipation into a state of unconditioned being and omnipotence. <meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of identity within Nirvana, specifically addressing how multiple Buddhas can be considered manifestations of a single unity.</meta>

<page-num>256</page-num>
# NIRVANA

in <term>Nirvana</term>, despite the teaching that all <term>Buddhas</term> are one. How can we reconcile this doctrine of <term>monism</term> with the assurance of various texts that the being who enters <term>Nirvana</term> can, when they so desire, reassume an earthly personality? There are some very remarkable texts on this subject in the <term>Sutra of the Lotus of the Good Law</term>: those, for instance, in which the <term>Tathâgata Prabhûtarâtna</term> is pictured as sitting "*perfectly extinct upon his throne*" and speaking before a vast assembly to which he has been introduced as "*the great Seer who, although perfectly extinct for many <term>kotis</term> of <term>aeons</term>, now comes to hear the Law.*" These texts themselves present us with the riddle of multiplicity in unity; for the <term>Tathâgata Prabhûtarâtna</term> and the myriads of other extinct <term>Buddhas</term> who appear simultaneously are said to have all been incarnations of but a single <term>Buddha</term>.

A reconciliation is offered by the hypothesis of what might be called a *<term>pluristic monism</term>*—a sole reality composed of groups of consciousness, at once independent and yet interdependent—or, to speak of pure mind in terms of matter, an *atomic spiritual ultimate*. This

<summary>This page explores the paradox of individual identity within the monism of Nirvana, citing the Sutra of the Lotus of the Good Law to show how multiple Buddhas can be seen as incarnations of a single unity. It proposes the concept of "pluristic monism" to describe a reality made of independent yet interdependent groups of consciousness.</summary>
<keywords>Nirvana, monism, Sutra of the Lotus of the Good Law, Tathâgata Prabhûtarâtna, Buddha, pluristic monism, atomic spiritual ultimate, kotis, aeons</keywords>

--- Page 269 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of "pluristic monism," explaining the Buddhist concept of the Absolute as a unity composed of individual units of consciousness, similar to the scientific conception of ether.</meta>

<page-num>257</page-num>

# NIRVANA

hypothesis, though not doctrinally enunciated in Buddhist texts, is distinctly implied by both text and commentary. The <term>Absolute</term> of <term>Buddhism</term> is one, just as ether is one. Ether is conceivable only as a composition of units.<note>This position, it will be observed, is very different from that of Hartmann, who holds that “all plurality of individuation belongs to the sphere of phenomenality.” (vol. ii. page 233 of English translation.) One is rather reminded of the thought of Galton that human beings “may contribute more or less unconsciously to the manifestation of a far higher life than our own—somewhat as the individual cells of one of the more complex animals contribute to the manifestation of its higher order of personality.” (Hereditary Genius, p. 361.) Another thought of Galton’s, expressed on the same page of the work just quoted, is still more strongly suggestive of the Buddhist concept: “We must not permit ourselves to consider each human or other personality as something supernaturally added to the stock of nature, but rather as a segregation of what already existed, under a new shape, and as a regular consequence of previous conditions. . . . Neither must we be misled by the word ‘individuality.’ . . . We may look upon each individual as something not wholly detached from its parent-source—as a wave that has been lifted and shaped by normal conditions in an unknown and limitless ocean.”</note>

The reader should remember that the Buddhist hypothesis does not imply either individuality or personality in <term>Nirvana</term>, but simple entity—not a *spiritual body*, in our meaning of the term, but only a divine consciousness. “Heart,” in the

<summary>This page explains that the Buddhist Absolute is a single entity composed of units, similar to the concept of ether, and distinguishes this state from Western ideas of individual personality. It cites Francis Galton to illustrate the idea of individuals as waves in a larger ocean or cells within a higher organism.</summary>
