The <term>Absolute</term> is conceivable only (according to any attempt to synthesize the <term>Japanese Buddhism</term> doctrines) as being composed of <term>Buddhas</term>. But here the student finds himself traveling further, perhaps, beyond the limits of the thinkable than Western philosophers have ever ventured. All are One; — each through union becomes equal with All! We are not only commanded to imagine the ultimate reality as being composed of units of conscious being, — but to believe that each unit

> sense of the divine mind, is a term used in some Japanese texts to describe such an entity. In the *<term>Dai-Nichi Kyō Sō</term>* (Commentary on the Dai-Nichi Sutra), for example, is the statement: — “When all seeds of <term>Karma-life</term> are entirely burned out and annihilated, then the *vacuum-pure <term>Bodhi-heart</term>* is reached.” (I should observe that Buddhist metaphysicians use the term “<term>vacuum-bodies</term>” to describe one of the high conditions of being.) The following, from the fifty-first volume of the work called *<term>Daizō-hō-sū</term>*, will also be found interesting: — “Through experience the <term>Tathâgata</term> possesses all forms, — forms in a multitude as countless as the dust-grains of the universe. . . . The <term>Tathâgata</term> is born in such places as he desires, or in accordance with the desires of others, and there saves [literally, ‘carries over’ — that is, over the Sea of Birth and Death] all sentient beings. Wherever his will finds a resting point, there is he embodied: this is called the <term>Will-Birth Body</term>. . . . The Buddha makes Law his body, and remains as pure as empty space: this is called the <term>Law-Body</term>.”

<summary>This page explains the Buddhist concept of the Absolute as a collective of Buddhas and defines the "Bodhi-heart" and the different forms of existence a Buddha may take, such as the Will-Birth and Law bodies.</summary>
<keywords>Nirvana, Absolute, Japanese Buddhism, Buddhas, Karma-life, Bodhi-heart, vacuum-bodies, Tathâgata, Will-Birth Body, Law-Body, Dai-Nichi Kyō Sō, Daizō-hō-sū</keywords>

--- Page 271 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the individual units that compose the Absolute, explaining that each unit is permanently equal and infinite in its potential.</meta>

<page-num>259</page-num>
<header>NIRVANA</header>

is permanently equal to every other and infinite in potential.¹ The central reality of every living creature is a pure Buddha; the visible form and thinking self that encase it are nothing but Karma. With some degree of truth, it might be said that Buddhism replaces our theory of a universe of physical atoms with the hypothesis of a universe of psychic units. It does not necessarily deny our theory of physical atoms, but rather takes a position that might be expressed in these words: “What you call atoms are really combinations—unstable aggregates that are essentially impermanent, and therefore essentially unreal. Atoms are only Karma.” And this position is suggestive. We know nothing at all about the ultimate nature of substance and motion, but we have scientific evidence that the known has evolved from the unknown; that the atoms of our elements *are combinations*; and that what we call *matter* and *force* are merely different manifestations of a single and infinite Unknown Reality.

---

<note>¹ Half of this Buddhist thought is actually embodied in Tennyson’s line:

> “Boundless inward, in the atom; boundless outward, in the Whole.”</note>

<vocab>Nirvana, Buddha, Karma, physical atoms, psychic units, Unknown Reality, Tennyson</vocab>

<summary>This page describes how Buddhism views every living creature as fundamentally a pure Buddha, with physical forms and even atoms being temporary manifestations of Karma. It draws a parallel between this metaphysical view and the scientific understanding of matter as a manifestation of an underlying Unknown Reality.</summary>
<keywords>Nirvana, Buddha, Karma, physical atoms, psychic units, Unknown Reality, Alfred Tennyson, metaphysics, evolution of matter</keywords>

--- Page 272 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the abstract concept of psychic units and atoms to a visual metaphor, comparing the composition of the universe to Japanese Buddhist paintings made entirely of microscopic text.</meta>

<page-num>260</page-num>
<header>NIRVANA</header>

There are wonderful Buddhist pictures which at first sight appear to have been made, like other Japanese pictures, with bold, free sweeps of a skilled brush, but which, when closely examined, prove to have been executed in a much more marvelous manner. The figures, the features, the robes, the aureoles—also the scenery, the colors, the effects of mist or cloud—all, even to the tiniest detail of tone or line, have been produced by groupings of microscopic Chinese characters, tinted according to position and more or less thickly massed according to the need for light or shade. In brief, these pictures are composed entirely out of the texts of Sutras: they are mosaics of minute ideographs—each ideograph a combination of strokes, and simultaneously the symbol of a sound and of an idea.

Is our universe composed this way?—an endless phantasmagoria made only by combinations of combinations of combinations of combinations of units finding quality and form through unimaginable affinities; now thickly massed in solid glooms; now palpitating in tremulosities of light and color; always and everywhere grouped by some stupendous art into one vast

<vocab>Nirvana, Buddhist pictures, Japanese, Sutras, ideographs, phantasmagoria, combinations, affinities, tremulosities</vocab>

<summary>This page uses the metaphor of Japanese Buddhist "text-pictures"—images composed entirely of microscopic characters from sacred Sutras—to suggest that the universe itself is a complex mosaic formed from combinations of fundamental units.</summary>
<keywords>Buddhist art, Sutras, ideographs, microcosm, macrocosm, phantasmagoria, universal composition, Japanese painting</keywords>

--- Page 273 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical reflection on the universe as a complex composition of units, moving from the visual metaphor of the previous page to a discussion of individuality and the evolution of religious thought.</meta>

<page-num>261</page-num>

mosaic of polarities; yet each unit in itself is an inconceivable complexity, and each in itself is also only a symbol, a character, a single ideograph of the undecipherable text of the Infinite Riddle? . . . Ask the chemists and the mathematicians.

### V

> . . . “All beings that have life shall lay
> Aside their complex form—that aggregation
> Of mental and material qualities
> That gives them, either in heaven or on earth,
> Their fleeting individuality.”
> ->*The Book of the Great Decease.*<-

In every teleological system, there are concepts that cannot withstand the test of modern psychological analysis. In the preceding incomplete outline of a great religious hypothesis, some “ghosts of beliefs haunting those mazes of verbal propositions in which metaphysicians habitually lose themselves” will undoubtedly be recognized. But truths will be perceived as well—grand recognitions of the law of ethical evolution, of the price of progress, and of our relation to the changeless Reality abiding beyond all change.

--- Page 274 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of individuality and the evolution of religious thought, moving into the scientific and Buddhist perspectives on the difficulty of moral progress.</meta>

<page-num>262</page-num>
<header>NIRVANA</header>

The Buddhist assessment of the massive opposition to moral progress that humanity must overcome is fully supported by our scientific knowledge of the past and our perception of the future. Mental and moral advancement has so far been achieved only through constant struggle against inheritances older than reason or moral feeling—against the instincts and the appetites of primitive animal life. And the Buddhist teaching—that the average person can hope to leave their lower nature behind only after the passage of millions of future lives—is much more of a truth than a theory. Only through millions of births have we been able to reach even our present imperfect state; and the dark legacies of our darkest past are still strong enough at times to triumph over reason and ethical feeling. Every future step forward on the moral path will have to be taken against the collective effort of millions of ghostly wills. For those past selves which priests and poets have told us to use as stepping stones to higher things are not dead, nor even likely to die for a thousand generations to come: they are too much alive;—they still have the power to

<summary>The author argues that the Buddhist concept of needing millions of lifetimes to overcome one's lower nature is supported by scientific understanding of evolutionary instincts. He suggests that moral progress is a constant struggle against the "ghostly wills" of our primitive ancestors that still live within us.</summary>
<keywords>moral progress, evolution, Buddhism, karma, rebirth, instincts, ancestral influence, ethical feeling</keywords>

--- Page 275 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the Buddhist perspective of moral progress, specifically how the struggle against primitive instincts remains even as society advances.</meta>

<page-num>263</page-num>
# NIRVANA

clutch the climbing feet—sometimes even
to fling back the climber into the primeval
slime.

Again, in its legend of the Heavens of Desire—progress through which depends upon the ability of triumphant virtue to refuse what it has won—Buddhism gives us a wonder-story full of evolutionary truth. The difficulties of moral self-elevation do not disappear with the improvement of material social conditions; in our own day, they rather increase. As life becomes more complex and more multifaceted, the obstacles to ethical advance likewise increase, as do the consequences of thoughts and acts. The expansion of intellectual power, the refinement of sensitivity, the broadening of sympathies, and the intense sharpening of the sense of beauty—all multiply ethical dangers just as certainly as they multiply ethical opportunities. The highest material results of civilization, and the increase of possibilities for pleasure, demand an exercise of self-mastery and a power of ethical balance that were unnecessary and impossible in older and lower states of existence.

Yet, in spite of this rejection of the very foundations of Western religion, it has been able to give us the revelation of larger religious possibilities—the suggestions of a universal scientific creed nobler than any which has ever existed. Precisely in that period of our own intellectual evolution when faith in a personal God is passing away—when the belief in an individual soul is becoming impossible—when the most religious minds shrink from everything that we have been calling religion—when the universal doubt is an ever-growing weight upon ethical aspiration—light is offered from the East. There we find ourselves in the presence of an older and a vaster faith—holding no crude anthropomorphic conceptions of the immeasurable Reality, and denying the existence of the soul, but nevertheless teaching a system of morals superior to any other, and maintaining a hope which no possible future form of positive knowledge can destroy. ## THE REBIRTH OF KATSUGORŌ

### I

The following is not a story—at least, it is not one of *my* stories. It is only the translation of an old Japanese document—or rather, a series of documents—properly signed and sealed, and dating back to the early part of the present century. My friend Aménomori, who is always seeking rare Japanese and Chinese manuscripts and seems to have some supernatural power for discovering them, found this one in the library of Count Sasaki in Tokyo. Thinking it to be a curious thing, he kindly obtained permission to have a copy of it made for me; and from that copy, the translation was completed. I am responsible for nothing beyond a few notes added to the text.

Although the beginning will probably prove to be dry reading, I venture to advise reading through it

<summary>The author introduces a translation of a series of authenticated 19th-century Japanese documents regarding the rebirth of a person named Katsugorō. The manuscripts were discovered by his friend Aménomori in the library of Count Sasaki in Tokyo.</summary>
<keywords>Katsugorō, Aménomori, Japanese manuscripts, Count Sasaki, Tokyo, reincarnation, translation, historical documents</keywords>

--- Page 280 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction to the documents regarding Katsugorō and begins the official report of the case.</meta>

of the whole translation from first to last, because it suggests many things besides the possibility of remembering former births. It will be found to reflect something of the feudal Japan that has passed away, and something of the old-time faith—not the higher Buddhism, but what is incomparably more difficult for any Westerner to obtain a glimpse of: the common ideas of the people concerning pre-existence and rebirth. And in view of this fact, the exactness of the official investigations and the credibility of the evidence accepted necessarily become questions of minor importance.

## II

### 1. — COPY OF THE REPORT OF TAMON DEMPACHIRŌ.

*The case of Katsugorō, nine years old, the second son of Genzō, a farmer on my estate, living in the village called Nakano-mura in the district called Tamagōri in the province of Musashi.*

Sometime during the autumn of last year, the above-mentioned Katsugorō, the son of Genzō, told his elder sister the story of his

During the twelfth month of the past year, Genzō himself questioned Katsugorō about the matter, after which Katsugorō declared: —

That he had been, in his former existence, the son of a certain Kyūbei, a farmer of Hodokubo-mura, which is a village within the jurisdiction of Lord Komiya, in the district called Tamagōri, in the province of Musashi; —

That he, Katsugorō, the son of Kyūbei, had died of smallpox at the age of six years; — and

That he had been reborn after that into the family of the previously mentioned Genzō.

Though this seemed unbelievable, the boy repeated all the circumstances of his story with so much exactness and apparent certainty that the Headman and the elders of the village made a formal investigation of the

The people of other neighboring villages seem to have heard of the matter; and now people come daily from various places to see Katsugorō.

### 3.—COPY OF THE LETTER OF MATSUDAIRA KWANZAN [DAIMYŌ] TO THE PRIEST TEIKIN OF THE TEMPLE CALLED SENGAKUJI.

I enclose and send you the account of Katsugorō's rebirth. I have written it in a popular style, thinking that it might help to silence those who do not believe in the doctrines of the Buddha. As a literary work, it is, of course, a poor thing. I send it to you assuming that it might only amuse you from that point of view. But as for the account itself, it is accurate; for I heard it myself from Katsugorō's grandmother. When you have read it, please return it to me.

->[Signed] KWANZAN.<-

*Twentieth day.* [No date.]

->[COPY.]<-

This is an account of a factual event; for it was written by Matsudaira Kwanzan Sama, who personally went [*to Nakano-mura*] on the twenty-second day of the third month of this year for the specific purpose of investigating the matter.

### [*Family of Hanshirō.*]

**Tōzō.** — Died at the age of six in Hodokubo village (*Hodokubo-mura*), in the district called Tamagōri in the province of Musashi. He was from the estate of Nakané Uyemon, whose residence (*yashiki*) is on Atarashi-bashi-dōri street, in Shitaya, Yedo. This was under the jurisdiction of Komiya. [Tōzō] was born in the second year of Bunkwa [1805] and died at about the fourth hour of the day [*10 o’clock in the morning*] on the fourth day of the second month of the seventh year of Bunkwa [1810]. The illness from which he died was smallpox. He was buried in the graveyard on the hill above the aforementioned village, Hodokubo-mura. His parish temple is Iwōji in Misawa-mura, of the Zen sect (*Zen-shū*). Last year, the fifth year of Bunkwa [1822], the *jiū-san kwaiki*<note>Buddhist memorial services for the dead are celebrated at regular intervals, which increase in length until one hundred years after death. The *jiū-san kwaiki* is the service for the thirteenth year after death. In this context, "thirteenth" means the year of death is counted as the first year.</note> was performed for Tōzō.

"Elder Sister, where did you come from before you were born into our household?"

Fusa answered him: —

"How can I know what happened to me before I was born?"

Katsugorō looked surprised and exclaimed:

"Then you cannot remember anything that happened before you were born?"

"Do you remember?" asked Fusa.

"Indeed I do," replied Katsugorō. "I used to be the son of Kyūbei San of Hodo-kubo, and my name was then Tōzō — don't you know all that?"

"Ah!" said Fusa, "I will tell father and mother about it."

But Katsugorō at once began to cry, and said: —

"Please do not tell! — it would not be good to tell father and mother."

Fusa answered, after a little while: —

"Well, this time I will not tell. But the next time that you do anything naughty, then I will tell."

After that day whenever a dispute arose between the two, the sister would threaten the brother, saying, "Very well, then — I will

Thinking Katsugorō must have been doing something wrong, they wanted to know what the matter was, and Fusa, being questioned, told them the truth. Then Genzō and his wife, and Tsuya, the grandmother of Katsugorō, thought it a very strange thing. They summoned Katsugorō, therefore, and tried, first by coaxing and then by threatening, to make him explain what he had meant by those words.

When I was five years old, Kyūbei San died; and a man called Hanshirō San took his place, who loved me very much. But in the following year, when I was six years old, I died of smallpox. In the third year after that, I entered mother’s honorable womb and was born again.”

The parents and the grandmother of the boy were greatly amazed at hearing this; and

They decided to make every possible inquiry about the man called Hanshirō of Hodokubo. But as they all had to work very hard every day to earn a living, and so could spare but little time for any other matter, they could not carry out their intention immediately.

Now Sei, the mother of Katsugorō, had to nurse her little daughter Tsuné nightly, who was four years old <note>Children in Japan, among the poorer classes, are not weaned until an age much later than what is considered the proper age for weaning children in Western countries. I was buried on a hill. There was a hole made in the ground, and the people let the jar drop into that hole. It fell *pon!* — I remember that sound well. Then somehow I returned to the house, and I rested on my own pillow there.¹ In a short time, an old man — looking like a grandfather — came and took me away. I do not know who or what he was. As I walked, I went through empty air as if flying. I remember it was neither night nor day as we went; it was always like sunset. I did not feel warm, cold, or hungry. We went very far, I think; but I could still always hear, faintly, the voices of people talking at home and the sound of the <term>Nembutsu</term>² being said for me.

<note>— called <term>Kamé</term>. Such jars are still used, although a large proportion of the dead are buried in wooden coffins of a style unknown in the West.</note>

<note>1. The idea expressed is not that of lying down with the pillow under the head, but of hovering near the pillow, or resting upon it as an insect might do. The bodiless spirit is usually said to rest upon the roof of the home. The appearance of the old man mentioned in the next sentence seems to be a <term>Shintō</term> concept rather than a <term>Buddhist</term> one.</note>

<note>2. The repetition of the <term>Buddhist</term> invocation <term>Namu Amida Butsu!</term> is known by this name. The <term>nembutsu</term> is recited by many <term>Buddhist</term> sects besides the <term>Amida</term> sect itself — the <term>Shinshū</term>.</note>

I also remember that when the people at home set out offerings of hot <term>botamochi</term> <note>1. *Botamochi* is a type of sweetened rice cake.</note> before the household shrine (<term>butsudan</term>), I inhaled the vapor of the offerings. . . . Grandmother, never forget to offer warm food to the honorable dead (<term>Hotoké Sama</term>), and do not forget to give to the priests—I am sure it is very good to do these things. <note>2. This kind of advice is common in Japanese Buddhist literature. By *Hotoké Sama* here, the boy does not mean the actual Buddhas, but the spirits of the deceased, affectionately called "Buddhas" by those who loved them—much as people in the West sometimes speak of our dead as "angels."</note> . . . After that, I only remember that the old man led me by a roundabout way to this place—I remember we passed the road beyond the village. Then we came here, and he pointed to this house and said to me: "Now you must be reborn, for it has been three years since you died. You are to be reborn in that house. The person who will become your grandmother is very kind, so it will be good for you to be conceived and born there." After saying this, the old man went away. I stayed for a little while under the <term>kaki</term>-tree (persimmon tree) before the entrance of this house. Then I was about to enter...

I remember that I was born without any pain at all. — Grandmother, you may tell this to father and mother, but please never tell it to anyone else.”

The grandmother told Genzō and his wife what Katsugorō had told her; and after that, the boy was not afraid to speak freely.

there what the name of the owner of the house was. “Hanshirō,” one of them answered. She asked the name of Hanshirō’s wife. “Shidzu,” was the reply. Then for the first time, Tsuya was convinced that Katsugorō had spoken the truth, and she could not help shedding tears. She related to the people of the house everything that Katsugorō had told her about his memories of his former birth. Then Hanshirō and his wife were greatly amazed. In the meantime, Katsugorō was looking all around; and seeing the roof of a tobacco shop opposite Hanshirō’s house, he pointed to it and said, “That wasn't there before.” And he also said, “The tree over there wasn't there before.” All this was true. So from the minds of Hanshirō and his wife, every doubt vanished <gloss>ga wo orishi</gloss>.

On the same day, Tsuya and Katsugorō returned to Tanitsuiri in Nakano-mura. Afterward, Genzō sent his son to Hanshirō's house several times and allowed him to visit the tomb of Kyūbei, his real father in his previous life.

Sometimes Katsugorō says, "I am a *Nono-Sama* <note>1. *Nono-San* (or *Sama*) is the word children use for the spirits of the dead, for the Buddhas, and for the Shintō gods—the *Kami*. *Nono-San wo ogamu*, meaning "to pray to the Nono-San," is the child's phrase for praying to the gods. According to Shintō thought, the spirits of ancestors become *Nono-San*—*Kami*.</note>; therefore, please be kind to me." Sometimes he also says to his grandmother, "I think I will die when I am sixteen; but, as Ontaké Sama <note>2. The reference here to Ontaké Sama is particularly interesting but requires a detailed explanation. Ontaké, or Mitaké, is the name of a famous holy peak in the province of Shinano and a major destination for pilgrims. During the Tokugawa Shogunate, a priest named Isshin of the Risshū Buddhists went on a pilgrimage to that mountain. After returning to his home in Sakamoto-chō, Shitaya, Yedo, he began to preach certain new doctrines and to make a reputation for himself as a miracle-worker, by virtue of powers said to have been gained during his pilgrimage to Ontaké. The Shogunate considered him a dangerous person and banished him to the island of Hachijō,</note> has taught us,"

where he remained for some years. Afterward, he was allowed to return to Edo, and there to preach his new faith—to which he gave the name of Azuma-Kyō. It was Buddhist teaching in a Shinto disguise—the deities especially adored by its followers being Okuninushi and Sukunahikona as Buddhist avatars. In the prayer of the sect called Kaibyaku-Norito it is said:
> "The divine nature is immovable (*fudō*); yet it moves. It is formless, yet manifests itself in forms. This is the Incomprehensible Divine Body. In Heaven and Earth it is called Kami; in all things it is called Spirit; in Man it is called Mind. . . From this only reality came the heavens, the four oceans, the great whole of the three thousand universes;—from the One Mind emanate three thousand great thousands of forms." . . .

In the eleventh year of Bunka (1814) a man called Shimoyama Osuke, originally an oil merchant in Heiyemon-chō, Asakusa, Edo, organized, on the basis of Isshin's teaching, a religious association named Tomoyé-Ko. It flourished until the overthrow of the Shogunate, when a law was issued forbidding the teaching of mixed doctrines and the blending of Shinto with the Buddhist religion. Shimoyama Osuke then applied for permission to establish a new Shinto sect, under the name of Mitaké-Kyō—popularly called Ontaké-Kyō; and the permission was given in the sixth year of Meiji <note>1873</note>. Osuke then remodeled the Buddhist sutra Fudō Kyō into a Shinto prayer book, under

The village people no longer call him Katsugorō; they have nicknamed him “Hodokubo-Kozō” (the Acolyte of Hodokubo).¹ Whenever anyone visits the house to see him, he immediately becomes shy and runs to hide in the inner rooms. the title, Shintō-Fudō-Norito. The sect still thrives; and one of its main temples is located about a mile from my current home in Tōkyō.

“Ontaké San” (or “Sama”) is a popular name given to the deities worshiped by this sect. It literally means the Deity dwelling on the peak of Mitaké, or Ontaké. However, the name is also sometimes applied to the high priest of the sect, who is thought to be oracularly inspired by the deity of Ontaké and to reveal truths through the power of the divinity. In the words of the boy Katsugorō, “Ontaké Sama” refers to the high priest of that time [1823], almost certainly Osuké himself—who was then the leader of the Tomoyé-Kyō.</note>

---

¹ *Kozō* is the name given to a Buddhist acolyte, or a youth studying for the priesthood. But it is also sometimes given to errand boys and little boy-servants—perhaps because, in earlier times, the heads of young boys were shaved. I believe the meaning in this text is “acolyte.”

### 7. — (NOTE BY THE TRANSLATOR.)

The preceding text is taken from a manuscript entitled *<term>Chin Setsu Shū Ki</term>*, or "A Collection of Uncommon Stories," compiled between the fourth month of the sixth year of <term>Bunsei</term> and the tenth month of the sixth year of <term>Tempō</term> [1823–1835]. At the end of the manuscript is written: “*From the years of Bunsei to the years of Tempō. — Minamisempa, Owner: Kurumachō, Shiba.*”

<summary>The account concludes by noting that the only virtuous acts the family performed were the grandmother's daily prayers and small charitable donations. The translator identifies the source of the story as a 19th-century manuscript titled Chin Setsu Shū Ki.</summary>
<keywords>Katsugorō, Nembutsu, mon, virtuous deeds, charity, Chin Setsu Shū Ki, Bunsei era, Tempō era, Minamisempa, Shiba</keywords>

<header>THE REBIRTH OF KATSUGORŌ</header>

“*Yedo.*” Under this, again, is the following note: — “*Bought from Yamatoya Sakujirō Nishinokubo: twenty-first day <unclear>?</unclear>, Second Year of Meiji <note>1869</note>.*” From this, it appears that the manuscript was written by *Minamisempa*, who collected stories told to him or copied them from manuscripts he obtained during the thirteen years from 1823 to 1835, inclusive.

### III

Perhaps someone will now be unreasonable enough to ask whether I believe this story—as if my belief or disbelief had anything to do with the matter! The question of the possibility of remembering former births seems to me to depend on the question of what it is that remembers. If it is the <term>Infinite All-Self</term> in each one of us, then I can believe the whole of the <term>*Jatakas*</term> without any trouble. As for the <term>False Self</term>, the mere fabric of sensation and desire, I can best express my idea by relating a dream I once had. Whether it was a dream of the night or a dream of the day need not concern anyone—since it was only a dream.

<summary>The translator traces the manuscript's history to an owner named Minamisempa and a purchase date in 1869. He then reflects on the possibility of remembering past lives, suggesting it depends on whether the memory belongs to the eternal self or the temporary ego.</summary>
<keywords>Minamisempa, Yedo, Meiji era, reincarnation, Infinite All-Self, Jatakas, False Self, memory, philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 303 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the bibliographic history of the manuscript to a philosophical exploration of the "False Self" through the narrator's reflection on the overwhelming, inexpressible pain of remembering past lives.</meta>

## XI

### WITHIN THE CIRCLE

Neither personal pain nor personal pleasure can be truly expressed in words. It is never possible to communicate them in their original form. It is only possible, by a vivid portrayal of the circumstances or conditions causing them, to awaken in sympathetic minds some related qualities of feeling. But if the circumstances causing the pain or the pleasure are totally foreign to common human experience, then no representation of them can fully reveal the sensations which they evoked. Therefore, any attempt to describe the real pain of seeing my former births is hopeless. I can only say that no combination of suffering possible for an individual being could be compared to such pain—the pain of countless interwoven lives. It seemed as if every nerve of mine had been extended into some monstrous web of <term>sentiency</term> <note>the capacity to feel or perceive</note> spun back through a million

<vocab>former births, sentiency, sympathetic minds, interwoven lives</vocab>

<summary>The narrator reflects on the limitations of language in expressing the profound, unique agony of remembering past lives, which he describes as a vast, interconnected web of suffering.</summary>
<keywords>reincarnation, sentiency, former births, memory, suffering, human experience, interwoven lives</keywords>

--- Page 304 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrator's sensory experience of recalling past lives, transitioning from the feeling of being part of a vast web of existence to the profound relief of returning to a single identity.</meta>

### WITHIN THE CIRCLE

years—and as if the whole of that measureless <term>woof and warp</term> <note>the intersecting threads of a woven fabric; used here to describe the structure of past lives</note>, over all its shivering threads, were pouring into my consciousness, out of the abysmal past, some ghastliness without name—some horror too vast for the human brain to hold. For, as I looked backward, I became double, quadruple, octuple; I multiplied by arithmetical progression; I became hundreds and thousands—and I feared with the terror of thousands, and despaired with the anguish of thousands, and shuddered with the agony of thousands; yet I knew the pleasure of none. All joys, all delights appeared as only mists or mockeries: only the pain and the fear were real—and they were always, always growing. Then, in the moment when <term>sentiency</term> <note>the capacity to feel or perceive</note> itself seemed to be bursting into dissolution, one divine touch ended the frightful vision and brought back to me the simple consciousness of the single present. Oh! How unspeakably delicious was that sudden shrinking back out of multiplicity into unity!—that immense, immeasurable collapse of Self into the blind, oblivious numbness of individuality!

### WITHIN THE CIRCLE

...one who had thus saved me, — “To others in a similar state, it has been permitted to see something of their preexistence. But no one among them could ever endure to look far. The power to see all former births belongs only to those eternally released from the bonds of the Self. Such beings exist outside of illusion—outside of form and name—and pain cannot come near them.

“But to you, who remain in illusion, not even the Buddha could give the power to look back more than a little way.

“Still, you are bewitched by the follies of art, poetry, and music—the delusions of color and form, the delusions of sensuous speech, and the delusions of sensuous sound.

“Still, that apparition called Nature—which is but another name for emptiness and shadow—deceives and charms you, and fills you with dreams of longing for the things of sense.

“But he who truly wishes to know must not love this phantom Nature; he must not find delight in the radiance of a clear sky, nor in the sight of the sea, nor in the sound of the flowing of rivers, nor in the forms of peaks, woods, and valleys, nor in their colors.

### WITHIN THE CIRCLE

> “He who truly wishes to know must not find delight in contemplating the works and the deeds of men, nor in hearing their conversation, nor in observing the puppet-play of their passions and of their emotions. All this is merely a weaving of smoke—a shimmering of vapors—an impermanence—a phantasmagoria.
>
> “For the pleasures that men call lofty or noble or sublime are but larger sensualities, subtler falsehoods: poisonous, fair-seeming blooms of selfishness—all rooted in the ancient slime of appetites and desires. To rejoice in the radiance of a cloudless day—to see the mountains shift their colors with the turning of the sun—to watch the passing of waves and the fading of sunsets—to find charm in the blossoming of plants or trees: all this belongs to the senses. No less truly a part of the senses is the pleasure of observing actions called great or beautiful or heroic—since it is one with the pleasure of imagining those things for which men miserably strive in this miserable world: brief love, fame, and honor—all of which are as empty as passing foam.
>

### WITHIN THE CIRCLE

woods, the plains; — all splendors and forms and colors — are spectres. The feelings, thoughts, and acts of men — whether considered high or low, noble or ignoble — all things imagined or done for any reason except the eternal purpose, are but dreams born of dreams and producing emptiness. To those with clear sight, all feelings of self — all love and hate, joy and pain, hope and regret — are equally shadows; youth and age, beauty and horror, sweetness and foulness, are not different; death and life are one and the same; and Space and Time exist only as the stage and the sequence of the perpetual shadow-play.

> “All that exists in Time must perish. To the Awakened, there is no Time or Space or Change — no night or day, no heat or cold, no moon or season, no present, past, or future. Form and the names of form are equally nothingness: Knowledge alone is real; and to whoever gains it, the universe becomes a ghost. But it is written: — ‘*He who has overcome Time in the past and the future must be of an exceedingly pure understanding.*’

<meta>This page continues the dialogue regarding the illusory nature of existence, specifically focusing on the cycles of rebirth and the nature of time as an obstacle to understanding the ultimate truth.</meta>

### WITHIN THE CIRCLE

to your eyes the shadow seems the substance, — and darkness, light, — and emptiness, beauty. And therefore to see your former births could give you only pain.”

I asked: —
“Had I found strength to look back to the beginning, — back to the edge of Time, — could I have read the Secret of the universe?”

“No,” was the answer made. “Only by Infinite Vision can the Secret be read. Could you have looked back incomparably further than your power permitted, then the Past would have become for you the Future. And could you have endured even yet more, the Future would have circled back for you into the Present.”

“Yet why?” I murmured, marveling. . . . “What is the Circle?”

“There is no Circle,” was the response; — “there is no Circle but the great phantom-whirl of birth and death to which, by their own thoughts and deeds, the ignorant remain condemned. But this has being only in Time; and Time itself is an illusion.”

<vocab>Secret of the universe, Infinite Vision, Past, Future, Present, Circle, phantom-whirl, birth and death, Time, illusion</vocab>

<keywords>birth and death, illusion, Time, Infinite Vision, the Secret, Circle, phantom-whirl, rebirth</keywords>
